# Translator's Note

## 1. The text

The English of this volume renders the **1577** Leipzig imprint of Nikolaus Selnecker's ***Symbolorum Apostolici, Niceni, et Athanasiani Exegesis*** — the enlarged reissue, from the press of *Johann Rhamba,* of the *editio princeps* of 1575. It is a running exposition of the three ancient Creeds of the Western Church — the *Apostolicum,* the *Nicaeno-Constantinopolitanum,* and the *Quicunque vult* — set forth *contra recentes horum temporum blasphemias,* that is, against the fresh Christological errors that had arisen in Selnecker's own generation of the Late Reformation. The volume opens with a Dedicatory Epistle to *Johann Günther, Count of Schwarzburg-Arnstadt-Sondershausen-Leutenberg,* dated *Leipzig, April 1575,* and carried without alteration into the 1577 reissue.

This volume is the third in a Scholastic Lutherans series devoted to Selnecker's mature systematic-dogmatic and creedal-Christological output. The two previously issued companion volumes — *The Institution of the Christian Religion, Part One* (Jena, Tröster, 1573; reissued 1579) and *The Institution of the Christian Religion, Part Two* (Jena, Tröster, 1579) — cover the Prolegomena, the Doctrine of God, Creation, Angels, Anthropology, Sin, the Law, the Cross, and Consolation (Part One), and Justification, Penitence, Christian Liberty, the Power of the Church, the Doctrine of the Sacraments, and the Rationale of Human Life (Part Two). The present *Exposition of the Creeds* takes up what those two volumes only touched on: the mature Lutheran Christology — the eternal divinity of the Son, the hypostatic union of the two natures in Christ, the *communicatio idiomatum* in its three genera, the majesty and ubiquity of the Son of Man, the substantial Real Presence of the body and blood of Christ in the Supper. It is the natural continuation of the Institutio's dogmatic arc, though the *Institutio* itself is a two-part work: what a modern reader might expect as "Institutio Part Three" was in fact never written by Selnecker under that title. The 1577 *Symbolorum exegesis* stands in its place, and stands as the mature intersection of Selnecker's dogmatic and catechetical work.

The English text-base is the digital facsimile of the 1577 Leipzig imprint held by the **Bayerische Staatsbibliothek München** (BSB Munich shelfmark stamp *00021305* on the verso of every folio in the scan; Google-Books identifier *hyfSeau9q60C*), a 368-page octavo. Where the printed original abbreviates, the abbreviation has been silently expanded; the long *ſ* has been silently modernised to round *s;* Selnecker's *maiestas / maiestate / maiestatica* has been kept as *majesty / majestic* rather than the older *majestie* form; and no other emendation has been made.

## 2. What the author supplies

Selnecker's own hand supplies the entirety of the volume — the *Dedicatory Epistle* to Count Johann Günther (April 1575); a first block of Ancient Symbols printed at the head of the volume (the three Creeds themselves, together with *Basil's* shorter Nicene rendering, *Epiphanius'* Nicene-Constantinopolitan expansion against the heresies, the *Chalcedonian* Definition, and two Christological confessions of the Emperor *Justinian*); a Psalm-block appended to that first cluster (Psalm 141 in the Vulgate numbering, that is, Psalm 142 in the Hebrew/English numbering, and Psalm 124/125, followed by Selnecker's own Latin distich-versification of Psalm 124/125 dated 1573); the great running *Enarratio Symbolorum* — the systematic prolegomena on why the Church recites the Creeds and how the ancient synods relate to Prophetic and Apostolic teaching; and the exposition of the Three Articles themselves, arranged so that Article II (the Christological article) opens out into the mature Lutheran Christological *Doctrina de Communicatione Idiomatum,* the Ascension article, and the ubiquity question *An Christus sit omnipraesens etiam secundum humanam naturam.* The volume closes with two great Sacramental appendices bound in with the exposition of the Third Article — the **1560 Heidelberg Disputation on the Lord's Supper** (which Selnecker had defended at the Academia Heidelbergensis under Frederick III before Frederick's turn toward the Reformed side and Selnecker's own departure to Jena), and the *De Iuda accipiente corpus et sanguinem Christi* patristic catena on the eating of the body of Christ by the unworthy — followed only by the printer's colophon.

His treatments quote — often at length — from Scripture (with clear preference for the Johannine Prologue, the great Pauline Christological passages of Colossians and Philippians, and the whole of Isaiah); from the church fathers (Basil, Athanasius, Cyril of Alexandria, Chrysostom, Ambrose, Hilary of Poitiers, Leo the Great, Fulgentius, Bernard, Jerome, Oecumenius, Epiphanius); from the ancient synods and their symbols (Nicaea, Constantinople, Ephesus, Chalcedon); from Luther and from Melanchthon (his *praeceptor* at Wittenberg); and from the Lutheran confessional documents — with special emphasis on the *Formula of Concord* Articles VII (Lord's Supper) and VIII (Person of Christ), which Selnecker himself had helped draft in the years immediately preceding this volume.

## 3. The Heidelberg Disputation of 1560

Toward the end of the volume, immediately after the exposition of the Third Article and before the *De Iuda accipiente* patristic catena, Selnecker prints as a self-standing document his own set of theses ***Propositiones de vera Coena Domini sententia,*** defended *iuxta Confessionem Augustanam adversus quorundam certamina* at the *Academia Heidelbergensis* on **3-4 June 1560,** under Elector Frederick III. This is a document of first-rate historical interest, for it fixes Selnecker's Real-Presence position — substantial, oral, *manducatio indignorum* included, and rejecting alike the Zwinglian *nuda signa* and the Calvinist *virtute-non-substantia* mediation — at a date fully seventeen years before the *Formula of Concord* codified that position as Article VII of the Lutheran confessional settlement.

The disputation was defended at the very hinge point of Frederick III's own theological journey — before Frederick's transition to the Reformed side and the composition of the *Heidelberg Catechism* in 1563, and before Selnecker's own departure from Heidelberg to the Chair of Theology at Jena in 1565. That the mature Selnecker chose in 1575 to reprint verbatim, at the close of his Christological *Exegesis,* the very theses he had defended fifteen years earlier at Heidelberg is his own way of establishing the doctrinal continuity between the young *Hofprediger* of the Dresden court and the mature co-author of the *Solida Declaratio* — and it is his own way of enrolling the young Frederick's own *Academia Heidelbergensis* in the witness of the confessional-Lutheran Real-Presence position.

## 4. A note on Scripture quotations

**Every scripture quotation in the body text is the translator's own English rendering of Selnecker's own Latin citation.** Selnecker cites Scripture in Latin — sometimes from the Vulgate, sometimes from his own conflate hand, sometimes from paraphrases available in the Reformation-era Latin Bibles (Erasmus, Pagninus, Münster, the Stephanus Vulgate) — and always in his own working numbering. Rendering these citations back into English from Selnecker's Latin, rather than substituting a modern English version, preserves both the exegetical force of what Selnecker actually places on the page and his citation-idiom. **No copyrighted modern English Bible translation is quoted anywhere in this volume.** The reader who wishes to consult a standard modern English rendering of any given passage may of course do so; the numbering here follows Selnecker's own (which for the Psalms in particular differs from the Hebrew/English numbering by one in most cases — Psalm 141 in Selnecker's citation is Psalm 142 in the Hebrew/English numbering, and so on).

The same principle applies to patristic quotations: what Selnecker renders in Latin from the Greek Fathers is here rendered in English from Selnecker's Latin, not from modern critical editions or from Migne. Where he preserves Greek in continuous script rather than in Latin translation — and he does this often in this volume, since much of the Christological doctrine of the Fathers turns on the exact Greek term (*οὐσία, ὑπόστασις, ὁμοούσιος, ἀνθρωπολάτρης, ψυχοφθόρος,* the whole vocabulary of the *communicatio idiomatum* in the Greek Fathers, the Chalcedonian *ἐκ δύο φύσεων* formula) — the Greek is preserved in the English rendering, with an English gloss.

## 5. Editorial conventions

- **Register:** scholarly / academic-critical English; reverent but not archaising. The substance of Selnecker's expository style — the numbered *distinctiones,* *quaestiones,* *definitiones,* *argumenta,* *fontes solutionum,* and *usus practicus* — is preserved as the *Loci-format* which Selnecker inherits from Melanchthon and here bends to the shape of a running exposition of the Creed.
- **Technical Latin terms** are preserved on first occurrence in each Article in italic (*hypostasis, ousia, homoousion, communicatio idiomatum, genus idiomaticum, genus apotelesmaticum, genus maiestaticum, extra Calvinisticum, unio hypostatica, maiestas, ubiquitas, praesentia realis, manducatio oralis, manducatio indignorum, manducatio impiorum, filioque, dextera Dei,* and so forth), briefly glossed, and used in English thereafter — unless the term is itself the subject of the section, in which case the Latin is retained throughout.
- **Greek** appears often in this volume; it is preserved in polytonic script with English gloss on first substantive occurrence in each section.
- **Hebrew** appears occasionally where Selnecker quotes Old Testament sources; it is preserved with transliteration and English gloss on first occurrence.
- **Confessional documents:** Selnecker cites the pre-*Concord* Lutheran confessions (AC, Apology, Small and Large Catechisms, Smalcald Articles — 1530-1537) as ***confessio nostra*** and the *Formula of Concord* (1577) as ***Formula Concordiae*** or ***nostra Concordia.*** Modern editions are consigned to the endnote apparatus (BSLK · Kolb-Wengert).
- **Names of contemporaries and predecessors:** first-occurrence life-dates appear in the endnote apparatus, not in the body.
- **Ornamental divider:** the aldus leaf ` — ❦ — ` marks section transitions, per the convention adopted across this English series.

## 6. Structural notes

The 1577 imprint of the *Exegesis* is an *enlarged reissue* of the 1575 *editio princeps* (also from the Rhamba press at Leipzig, and also preserved at Munich in the copy Google-Books identifies as *QyE8AAAAcAAJ*). The Dedicatory Epistle to Count Johann Günther bears the *original April 1575 date* without alteration — Selnecker did not re-date it for the reissue. What the reissue *adds* to the *editio princeps* is largely the sharpening of the polemic against the freshest of the "recentes horum temporum blasphemias," and — most importantly — the incorporation of the 1560 Heidelberg Disputation and the *De Iuda accipiente* catena as the closing Sacramental section.

The 1577 imprint carries **no printed back-matter apparatus.** Unlike the companion Pars II volume, this edition has no *Elenchus locorum Scripturae* (index of scripture passages), no *Patrum Sententiae* (Fathers'-quotations index), and no *Index Rerum et Verborum Memorabilium* (alphabetical subject-and-word index). The book runs straight from the *De Iuda accipiente* catena to the printer's colophon. A constructed reader-facing Table of Contents has been added to the English edition (below); no subject index has been constructed.

## 7. Authorship

The Scholastic Lutherans translation team.

## Front Matter

- Title Page (as printed, 1577 Leipzig imprint)
- Translator's Note
- Nikolaus Selnecker — Biographical Sketch
- Dedicatory Epistle to Johann Günther, Count of Schwarzburg-Arnstadt-Sondershausen-Leutenberg (Leipzig, April 1575)

## The Three Creeds and their Ancient Accompaniments

- The Apostles' Creed
- The Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed (with the *Filioque*)
- The Athanasian Creed (*Quicunque vult*)
- Basil's Shorter Rendering of the Nicene Creed
- Epiphanius' Nicene-Constantinopolitan Symbol against the Heresies
- The Symbol of the Ecumenical Synod of Chalcedon
- The Confession of the Emperor Justinian
- The Edict of Justinian to the People of Constantinople
- Psalm 141 (Vulgate; 142 Heb./Eng.) *Domine, ad te clamabo*
- Psalm 124 (Vulgate; 125 Heb./Eng.) *Nisi Dominus ipse fuisset nobiscum*
- Selnecker's Latin Distich Versification of Psalm 124/125 (1573)

## Prolegomena — *Enarratio Symboli*

- Why the Church recites Creeds
- The four causes for which God ordains the ecumenical synods
- Synods witness to Prophetic and Apostolic doctrine, and do not create new dogmas
- The customary threefold division of the Creed — Creation · Redemption · Sanctification
- The subdivision of Article II into six sub-articles and Article III into five, per the twelve-Apostles tradition

## Article I — *Credo in Deum Patrem Omnipotentem*

- Part 1. *Quid sit Credere* — Historical vs Justifying Faith · *Credere Deum, in Deum, Deo*
- Part 1 (continued). *De una Dei essentia et tribus personis* — the vocabulary of *essentia, persona, hypostasis;* against Sabellius and Arius
- Part 2. *De prima Trinitatis persona* — the First Person as the Creed presents Him
- Part 2 (continued). *De Creatione* — creation *ex nihilo,* the grades of creatures, angels, man, the *imago Dei,* the four states of man
- Part 3. *De Providentia, Contingentia, Lapsu, Libero Arbitrio, Peccato, Lege* — Providence, contingency, the Fall, free will, sin, and the Law
- Part 3 (closing). The six-fold Law-Gospel *discrimen* and the bridge to Article II

## Article II — *Credo in Iesum Christum Filium Dei*

- Part 1. *Persona Christi · Divinitas · Officium* — the arcane counsel of the Trinity to send the Second Person as Mediator; anti-Nestorian formulations
- Part 2. *Incarnatio* — the Nicene-clause exegesis: *Deum de Deo, Lumen de Lumine, genitum non factum, consubstantialem Patri, per quem omnia facta sunt;* the Passion, the Descensus, the Resurrection
- Part 3. Closing exposition and transition into the *Communicatio Idiomatum*
- ***Doctrina de Communicatione Idiomatum*** — the Chalcedonian formula in Greek, the three *genera communicationis,* the anti-Reformed *extra Calvinisticum* refuted
- *De Ascensione* — the Ascension as the exaltation of Christ's human nature *super omnes caelos ad dexteram Dei,* not a mere local translation
- *An Christus sit omnipraesens etiam secundum humanam naturam* — the ubiquity of Christ's human nature; the *finitum non capax infiniti* rebuttal
- *De Sessione ad dexteram Dei* — the *dextera Dei* as universal dominion, not a physical location; the Sessio as the office-formal ground for the ubiquity of Christ's human nature
- *De Adoratione Christi et de Judicio venturo* — the adoration of the incarnate Christ; the *Veni Domine Iesu* longing for the return; the last judgment as the vindication of Christ's kingdom

## Article III — *Credo in Spiritum Sanctum · Ecclesiam · Remissionem Peccatorum · Resurrectionem Carnis · Vitam Aeternam*

- *De Spiritu Sancto* — the five *beneficia Spiritus Sancti* (docere, renovare-sanctificare, iuvare, obsignare, arrhabo)
- *De Ecclesia* — the Church as *ubicunque sonat vox Evangelii;* the Epiphanius Greek Creed-fragment on the Spirit

*Propositiones de vera Coena Domini sententia, defended by Nikolaus Selnecker at the Academia Heidelbergensis under Elector Frederick III, iuxta Confessionem Augustanam.*

## *De Iuda Accipiente Corpus et Sanguinem Christi*

*A patristic catena on the eating of the body and blood of Christ by the unworthy — Augustine, Bernard, Jerome, Oecumenius, with a closing rule on the interpretation of the Fathers' language.*

## Colophon (Leipzig, Johann Rhamba, 1577)

## Prolegomena — *Enarratio Symboli Apostolici, Niceni, et Athanasiani*

## Why the Church Recites the Creeds

**When in the Church we recite the Symbol,** or expound other writings, we testify that we abhor from a new *kind* of doctrine, and embrace that *one perpetual voice of the Catholic Church of God* which the first Parents, the Prophets, and the Apostles sounded. For we know that the Church is gathered and preserved by God in this life for this very cause: that there may be some assembly a ***custos verae doctrinae*** — a guardian of true doctrine — handing down not a *new* doctrine, but *propagating* to posterity the divine discourse, with the significations of words explained, with the members of doctrine distributed and numbered so far as may be, with perspicuous *testimonia* adjoined (taken from the Prophetic and Apostolic writings, and from the Catholic Symbols, or the approved histories of the synods), and with corruptions and opinions warring with this *Light of truth* being simultaneously rejected.

And since it is necessary that the summa of doctrine, briefly comprehended, always be in the view of all — and that no one carry about with him in mind a *mutilated* doctrine but, as it were, the *whole body* of doctrine, and by this Confession that each be erudited, confirmed, and instituted in his own invocation, and at the same time separate himself from the opinions of others who are either *foreign* to the Church and its doctrine or *scatter manifest corruptions* of doctrine — therefore in the Church there have been *written brief Symbols,* containing a brief collection and *summa* of the articles, or of all, or of the chief points of the Evangelical doctrine.

To these Symbols the pious and erudite have always adjoined a *longer explication,* and have collected the testimonies handed down by God, so that men might be certain of the *foundation* of the Symbols, whence the doctrine in the Symbols was drawn, and that **faith should lean not on human sentences, or on the authority of the synods** which composed the Symbols, **but on the word of God.** For **synods do not create new articles or new dogmas,** but are only *witnesses* concerning dogmas and sentences which have previously been handed down in the Prophetic and Apostolic writings; and they show that those dogmas are not new, but have always been handed down thus in the Church of God from the Prophetic and Apostolic writings. To these dogmas also *firm faith* is to be adhibited, not on account of the authority of the synods, or their grade, or their location, but on account of the manifest testimonies of the word of God. **Which testimonies the synods, when they do not offer them, are not to be assented to; and when they depart from the sentence of the word of God, they must be contradicted, because it is established that they err.**

## The Four Causes for which God Ordains Synods

But God wills that pious *synods* be instituted for four causes, and that the judgments of the pious in the Church be heard.

**The first cause:** that the *purity of doctrine* may be retained, and the corruptions of doctrine refuted.

**The second:** that the weaker may be *confirmed* by the collation of Prophetic and Apostolic sentences, and that the erring may be *reduced into the way.*

**The third:** that it may be known *where and what* is the true Church of God, sensing rightly and setting forth the true doctrine by an open and by no means ambiguous Confession, and rejecting the contrary.

**The fourth:** that *posterity also* may be rightly erudited and confirmed, and may be a *witness* of the truth of doctrine and of our constancy.

For these causes, judgments concerning doctrine have always been heard in the Church, which are called *synods.* And they are *true* synods when they judge rightly — that is, when they render a plain and true confession, or a true testification — in whatsoever mode they are convened: whether by the authority of Kings, or by private studies, as *Mary, Elizabeth, Zechariah* convened (Luke 1–2); as also the *Apostles* (Acts 15), who are also reported to have composed in a peculiar synod the Symbol which we call *Apostolicum²⁸* — which Irenaeus and Tertullian for this very cause name the ***Apostolic tradition,*** consonant in all things with Scripture. And Irenaeus' words are these:

> *"It is not necessary to seek the truth elsewhere, which it is easy to take from the Church, since the Apostles, as it were into a repository, have conferred into her most fully all things which are of the truth, so that whosoever will may draw from her the drink of life. For she is the introit of life; but all the rest are thieves and robbers (John 10). And on this account we ought to shun those men, and to love with great diligence the things which are of the Church, and to lay hold of the tradition of truth."*²⁹

Thus antiquity has always sensed that the Symbol called *Apostolic* was composed by the Apostles themselves. And so we too retain the appellation *Apostolic Symbol,* whether because it was composed by the Apostles themselves before they set out into the diverse regions of the world (as Cyprian and Rufinus judge), and was handed down through the Church's hands so that it might be a *form* and *summa* of doctrine to be propagated among men; or because in this Symbol Apostolic doctrine, collected as it were into one *bundle,* is briefly expressed.

Hence some have named it a *collatio* or a *symbola,* because in this one place the whole Catholic doctrine of Faith is briefly *collected together* — ***ἀπὸ τοῦ συμβάλλειν,*** *"from bringing together."* In the feminine gender they name it a *collatio* or a *contribution to a banquet* — thus Terence uses ***symbolam*** (*Eunuchus* III, 4.2), and Aristophanes uses ***συμβολάς***³⁰ for what is put down for the drinking-party. In the neuter, ***symbolum*** is often the *sign of another thing:* thus one names by *collection* the *military tesserae* (watch-passwords) by which in war companions are simply distinguished from enemies — the *Loosung, Kennzeichen* in the German. Plautus also uses it in the masculine gender: ***symbolus,*** *collecta.* And thus *symbolus, symbola,* and *symbolum* are found in the same signification.³¹

Some — from the *brevity of a sentence signifying great and divine things, and expressing lucidly the faith of the soul by a certain rotund apophthegm* — will have it that a *symbol* is so called: as we call the sentences of great men *symbols,* as of *Alfonso: For law, and for the flock.* For it delights us that each Christian be pleased with this *his own* Symbol, so to speak, as with a **little pledge of his faith and doctrine.** Others deduce the name *symbolum* from the letters, which we call in German *Passport,* and which promise the *obligation of faith,* or a safe-conduct, or *sichergeleit,* to those to whom they are given, and at the same time commend the same to others for helping and promoting. For it is agreed that it was divinely given to Christians for this reason to have a *symbol* — that by its faith and confession they may be fortified as it were as *pilgrims* against the incursions of enemies, and may pass through safely, and be marvellously helped.

But leaving aside these reasons for the appellation brought forward by the pious, we retain this true and approved account: that from the *similitude of a military sign* (which the Latins call *tessera* and the Greeks also ***σύνταγμα,*** by which allies are known from enemies and from spies) the ***Apostolic Symbol*** is so called, that by its confession, as by a *tessera,* Christians might be distinguished from heathens, and true doctors from pseudo-Apostles. And the *Apostolicum* is *older than the Nicenum.*³²

## The Origin of the Nicene Symbol

The *Nicene* takes its name from the city of *Nicaea* in **Bithynia,** in which — in the year of Christ 328³³ — the Emperor *Constantine the Great,* against the blasphemies of *Arius* denying the eternal essence, divinity, and majesty of the Son of God with the Father, indicted an **Ecumenical Council,** calling three hundred and eighteen bishops, whom the Emperor caused to be brought by public conveyances; and if any of them were (as many were) *old martyrs* who had shown their constancy in the sufferings under Diocletian, Maximinus, and Licinius — mutilated in some part of the body and bearing about wounds and scars — the Emperor received these both *honorifically* and *kissed the mutilated parts.* There were present, moreover, at the Synod composing the Nicene Symbol, not only the Emperor himself, the bishops, and the presbyters, ***οἱ τοποτηρηταί*** (or *legates* of other bishops who on account of old age and illnesses could not attend), but very many *laymen* also, men learned and good.

**Now it profits to consider diligently the cause and occasion of this Nicene synod being gathered,** since similar things happen very often — especially in this delirious old age of the world — in the Church. The cause was this: Constantine had now made peace in the Empire, five tyrants — **Diocletian, Maximian, Maxentius, Maximinus, and Licinius,** the most cruel persecutors of the Church and of the pious — having been defeated and destroyed, whom nevertheless even *dissensions among the Christians themselves,* divided against one another, and many *deserters and pointers* from these, incited and armed.³⁴ **This peace, constituted in the Churches and in the commonwealth,** the Devil — hating the Son of God repressing and punishing the persecutors of His Church — attempted anew to disturb, kindling a horrible fire in Egypt (which spread through the whole East, and from which the *Mahometan blasphemies* against the Son of God were augmented), by driving on ***Arius,*** a presbyter of Alexandria, to spread cunningly a *coloured impious dogma against the divinity of Christ.*

Writers report that Arius first defended in the Church of Alexandria the impious dogma of ***Meletius,*** who had denied that those lapsed into manifest sins were to be received again — for which cause *Peter,* Bishop of Alexandria, expelled Arius from the Church. But when this Bishop Peter was taken by a glorious death for the name of Christ, ***Achilles*** was made bishop, who received Arius and set him over the theological school. Achilles being dead, Arius, ambitiously seeking the bishopric, when he had suffered a *repulse,* moved by the pain of the repulse, set himself against ***Alexander,*** whom he had seen preferred to himself. And when Bishop Alexander — a man illustrious in age, doctrine, piety, and experience — assiduously and with great zeal handed down the doctrine of the eternal divinity of the Son, that the Son is with the Father ***συναΐδιος, ὁμοούσιος,*** and ***ὁμότιμος,***³⁵ Arius began cunningly to impugn this doctrine.

He did not indeed dare to deny expressly that the Son (whom John according to Scripture names ***λόγος***) is ***ὑπόστασις,*** or *a person subsisting through Himself* — but he built up cunningly other kinds of ambushes, saying that this ***λόγος*** is not ***συναΐδιος*** with the Father, but that the Father was before the ***λόγος*** was; and that the ***λόγος*** is *from things not existing,* or, *out of nothing constituted the first and highest creature of all,* more excellent than all other creatures, indeed the head of all creatures, but neither of the essence of the Father, nor omnipotent.³⁶

When Arius had drawn certain bishops into this his opinion, and (as tends to happen) a great and perilous discord had arisen which could no longer be composed by peaceable embassies and collations — although by Constantine many arguments and ways were undertaken by writings and by legates sent (especially ***Ossius,*** Bishop of Cordoba)³⁷ — at length a **Council** was indicted by the most pious Emperor, in which each side might be heard, the truth laid open, and confirmed by execution. Present therefore was the Emperor himself. He sat down in the midst among the bishops on a seat not higher but lower, made of gold; and first gave thanks to God for the assembling of his friends and fellow-ministers; then exhorted them to a *placid collation,* to an *investigation of the truth,* all sophistry being removed and the acerbity of minds put down. The first opinion was rendered by ***Eustathius of Antioch,*** the bishop, who was the supreme *governor* of the Synod. For the doctrine of the Son of God had also earlier at Antioch been illustrated with weighty testimonies in the *refutation of Paul of Samosata,* whose contagion Arius had drunk (indeed sensing the same as *Ebion, Samosatenus,* and the like had sensed).³⁸

Eustathius ordered that the dogma of Arius be read. And when they came to the words: ***"The λόγος is not eternal God, but was constituted and made by God, and is diverse from the nature or essence of the Father, and is not Omnipotent, because He is a Creature, to whom the eternal essence and divine majesty is not competent,"*** — many rose from their seats in *just indignation,* and by unanimous consent tore in pieces the papers of Arius, and decreed that Arius with his little band should be cast out by public proclamation from the assembly of the Orthodox. And Arius's ***στροφή*** (that *cunning delay, that fraudulent tergiversation and deception, that skill of playing false — sich wenden* in the German) is to be diligently considered — a thing usual in all times to evil natures and heretics, who *are never open, and never act candidly.*³⁹

When Arius was refuted — that Christ is God — he said this indeed to be true; for even the *Angels* and all the *saints* are and are called Gods and Sons of God. When again he was convinced that Christ is **true God of true God,** he affirmed that he conceded this also — meaning by *true God* one that was *made* of *true God,* that is, meaning nothing other than a *Creature,* because every *made-thing of God* is a *true made-thing.* Convinced also here that Christ was **before the creation of all things,** or before the beginning and universal world, he responded that he sensed no otherwise, but that he was of one confession with them — meaning that *Christ was before all other creatures,* that is, *constituted ἀχρόνως* (*without time*). When the Orthodox pressed on, and showed that through the Son *all things* are constituted, he said he assented — meaning that Christ was *first* constituted before all things, and thereafter through Christ the rest of things were constituted. When again convinced that Christ is **begotten, not made,** he affirmed he sensed the same — *begotten* as all believers are said to be born of God; *not made,* namely among the Creatures, but *made before the other Creatures.*

At length, that they might come to the very *scopus of the truth,* and that every occasion of slipping might be cut off from Arius, the Orthodox showed that **the Son of God is coeternal, and of one essence and dignity with the Father.** Here Arius *stopped* and *fell silent,* and either would not, hindered by malice, or could not, punished by hardening, *confess* the Son of God to be either eternal or coeternal with the Father, much less coessential and of the same dignity. After long contests the *Nicene Symbol* was written, which — by the example of Constantine, who is reported to have commanded *Marcus, Bishop of Rome,* that the Symbol should be sung with clear voice after the reading of the Gospel — is still retained in the Churches. Which Symbol also very many approved by their own subscription; and from these, eleven Arian bishops — fearing the Emperor, and for a suitable time reserving and *deferring* their dogma, to be spewed forth in *venom* — subscribed with hypocritical and false mind and their own hand.

And the Emperor himself subscribed, and addressed the bishops with most clement words: *"I have been present,"* he said, *"as one of you, and I rejoice that I have been your fellow-servant, and that inquiry has been made, and truth having been brought into the light, concord has been established."*

But when in the Nicene Synod the *blasphemies of Samosatenus and Arius* had been damned, and the *error of Meletius* concerning not receiving those lapsed and returning to penitence had been refuted — where the voice of the Emperor to *Acesius,* bishop of the Novatianists, is worthy of memory, saying: **"Set up a ladder, Acesius, and ascend into heaven alone!"** — and after very many other things (as concerning the marriage of priests, and the *ἀνωμάλα*⁴⁰ of private offences, especially among the teachers) had been usefully constituted, with a grave exhortation to purity of doctrine, piety, and concord premised, and a royal banquet spread, the Emperor imposed an end to the Council, which had lasted more than three years and not without the greatest expenses.

And although the solicitude, piety, will, and severity of the Emperor were great — for Arius was driven into exile, and with him six others — nevertheless the *Arian fire* could neither then be entirely extinguished nor afterwards taken away, since some time after it wandered wider in Asia than before, and the Churches were distracted, and others out of *doubting*⁴¹ began to *hate the doctrine.* And the multitude, wearied of obscure disputations and quarrels among the Teachers, more willingly at length received the plausible ***dogma of Mahomet*** in profane genius, by whose profession it could preserve peace, liberated from the quarrels of teachers.⁴² There returned also to their vomit the *eleven bishops* who had before, by their subscription, dissembled that they embraced the Nicene Symbol, when they had secretly sensed with Arius. And the affair was by *court craft* reduced to this: that Constantine himself, persuaded by the opinion of Arius's *innocence,* recalled Arius from exile, and ordered the cause again to be examined in the city of Tyre, and soon at Constantinople.

Here, when all human help was ceasing, and *court artifices* were proceeding, and *Athanasius* was in the hatred of the more powerful, and many were most cunningly transacting their own affairs, and were deceiving and leading astray the Emperor — who rightly-sensing and most excellently wishing well to the best cause was thinking either that Arius was *innocent,* or that Arius was *not rightly understood,* and that those contradicting him were *unquiet* — and were sublevating, excusing, commending, defending Arius (among whom ***Eusebius of Nicomedia*** virulently withstood *Alexander,* the Orthodox Bishop of Constantinople) — **divine help began marvellously to appear.**

For when it was about to be met in the morning, and a great struggle was imminent, Bishop *Alexander,* remaining through the night in the temple, prayed God that He might either *repress Arius,* or take away himself and the other rightly-sensing from this life, and might Himself *defend His own truth.* Then at length in the morning Arius, being brought magnificently with court solemnity to the temple, deviates from the way; oppressed by a sudden *consternation of mind,* and sitting in a latrine with his intestines poured out — his heart and spleen with them — he passed away by a *foul death,* worthy of his foul mind and tongue, and similar to *Judas's* end.⁴³ The multitude, on becoming aware of his destruction, dispersed; and again in some manner the authority of the rightly-sensing began to be greater.

A little after, Constantine also died in the city of Nicomedia, in a suburban villa, and left the Churches *dissonant,* being deceived by Arian bishops and *sycophants* — chiefly by a certain court priest who had authority with ***Constantia,*** the Emperor's sister who had been married to Licinius. Constantine had sent *Athanasius* into exile in the city of *Trier* upon receiving Arius back. **But the pious were sustained by the counsels and instruction of Athanasius.** For in the year 371⁴⁴ a **Synod** was gathered at Alexandria against the Arians and *Pneumatomachians;* Athanasius was called to it; and there the Symbol which we today call ***Athanasianum*** in the Churches is judged to have been composed.

## Four Questions on the Origin of the Three Catholic Creeds

Since these things have been said concerning the origin of the three Catholic Symbols, before proceeding further to their exposition, it is worth the pains to respond to these *four questions:*

**I.** Since Constantine gathered the Nicene Council, it is wont to be asked: **Does the care of ordering synods pertain also to political power?**

**II.** Do the judgments and decrees of the synods have the same *validity* by which civil judgments have validity and are approved — on account of the *authority and consensus of the judges?*

**III.** Can or should ***consensus*** be the *norm of doctrine,* as our adversaries wish?

**IIII.** When two assemblies dissent, whence may it be known which one rightly senses?

### Answer to the First

The divine Law commands not only *Doctors,* or the *ecclesiastical jurisdiction* (as they call it), but also the *political power,* to take away *blasphemies* and *idols* (Lev. 24: *"He who has blasphemed the name of the Lord, let him be put to death;"* Deut. 12: *"Overturn the altars of the idols;"* 1 Cor. 10: *"Flee from the cultivation of idols,* or *idolatry"*).⁴⁵ And the *judgment concerning doctrine,* and the *cognition of the truth,* is not only of one grade or order, but of *all the members of the Church who are especially fit for judging.* Therefore when dissensions arise, the *political power,* by reason of its own office divinely committed to it, ought altogether to institute a legitimate *cognition,* in which the doctors and the other pious members of the Church — and indeed the Princes themselves — the matter having been examined, can render their sentences *according to the norm of the divine word.*

And individual Princes in their own dominion ought in this way to preserve concord, and *not* to serve the hatreds of others, and *not* to bear scandals disturbing the Church, and *not* to undertake anything by force apart from a *pious cognition.* For each party must be heard (as God often commands); and afterwards, when the truth being laid open is established, *execution* is at length to be done in this way: that the Doctors *refute and damn manifestly the manifest blasphemies and impious errors,* while the political power *prohibits the external profession of impiety* and *removes the untractable blasphemers,* lest they exhale their venom upon others.

And there extends the *constitution of Justinian the Emperor,* worthy of memory (Novella 123):

> *"We command,"* he says, *"that the presidents of the provinces, if they see anything of those things which have been sanctioned by us concerning the assembling of synods to be neglected, first indeed press the metropolitans and other bishops to celebrate the annual synods, and preserve all things which we have constituted concerning synods. But if they see them contriving delay and pursuing the business more sluggishly, let them make us more certain of this by suggestion, so that against those who defer to celebrate synods a worthy correction may proceed from us — the presidents themselves knowing, as also the cohorts who obey them, that if they less observe these things, they shall be subjected to the extreme punishments."*⁴⁶

And the words of Emperor *Marcian* are celebrated in the Chalcedonian Council: **"We,"** he said, **"have judged to be present at the synod by the example of the Emperor Constantine — to confirm the faith, not to ostentate our virtue — lest hereafter the multitude of the people, drawn to depraved institutions, be found dissentient."**

But let the *norm of judgment* be the Prophetic and Apostolic Scriptures, and the Symbols — according to that of *Ambrose:* **"Follow the Scripture, that you cannot err."** And Ps. 119: *"Thy word is a lamp unto my feet, O Lord, and a light in my way."*

### Answer to the Second

In *civil controversies,* the interpretation of the magistrate prevails on account of his grade and the office of his person — provided he does not depart from the *rule of the law of nature,* or from honesty. For God wills that there be an end of civil discords, and that a *moderate tranquillity* be constituted, even if some error is present in that civil interpretation, which nevertheless cannot be so grave or perilous, since the business is subject to *human reason.* And by no means is the authority of things judged to be shaken; but by the *sword* also are they to be coerced who *contumaciously* oppose themselves to the cognition of political power and authority (Rom. 13), for otherwise no end of discords would there be.

**But in the judgments of heavenly doctrine, interpretation by no means prevails on account of grade or place, but only prevails on account of the truth and authority of Scripture,** of which those who judge concerning controversies ought to render *confession and testification.* Not according to the disciples of Pythagoras (to use Jerome's words): the *prejudged opinion of the Master* is not to be weighed, but the *reason of the doctrine.*⁴⁷ And the rule of 1 Thess. 5[:21] must be held: **"Prove all things, and hold fast that which is good."** And the Saviour's words, saying: **"Be good money-changers, that if any coin is adulterated and does not have the figure of Caesar, and is not marked with the public coin, it may be reprobated; but he who bears the face of Christ with clear light — let him be laid up in the purse of our heart."**⁴⁸

We therefore do not assent to any interpretation of the Nicene Council (as *Augustine* rightly speaks⁴⁹), or of Ambrose, Augustine, Hilary, Cyprian, Jerome (as Augustine himself expressly names these and himself), or of the sentences of others *on account of grade or place;* but rather, as *faithful and attentive hearers,* aided by the reading and interpretation of the Doctors, we seek in the Prophetic and Apostolic sayings the *causes of interpretation.* And when it is established that they err, we do not assent to *erroneous* opinions — as today we contradict the errors of the Synod of Trent — because we have a manifest mandate: **"If an angel from heaven should teach another gospel, let him be anathema"** (Gal. 1). For it is necessary that the voice of God be preferred to the sayings of men. And the Church is to be heard *not* on account of the authority of this assembly which is called the Church, since faith does not lean on human authority, but on account of the *confession of the truth* sounding in that assembly, or on account of the *certain and illustrious testimonies handed down in the Prophetic and Apostolic writings.*

But there is objected the *mass and abundance of dissensions,* the end of which cannot come to be, if in this manner the authority of things judged is limited. But the true answer is: **Never has the greater part of Kings, Princes, and peoples approved, nor will it approve, heavenly doctrine, and acquiesce in the Prophetic and Apostolic writings.** Wherefore ***ἀπιστία*** and ***κακοπιστία*** will never be uprooted from the root, which in all times give cause for great and tragic dissensions. But among such great dissensions there are also, in all times, some rightly-sensing; and God preserves some assembly sounding the Gospel, which He wills to be heard, and which sometimes is more illustrious and more frequent, according to God's clemency and paternal blessing — sometimes more obscure and more slender, according to the wrath and the malediction poured out upon the sins and ingratitude and *security of men.* But with dissensions growing, we ought to defend the *truth known* and *confirmed by an ingenuous Confession,* and to await *the judgment of God;* and meanwhile to *bear patiently* the savagery of the enemies of doctrine, and — if there is need — even *unto blood* to sustain it, even if we are on that account killed, as the Apostles were killed by the Princes of the Jews. For God at length, by the *execution of His own sentence,* the adverse part having been destroyed, imposes an end to dissensions — and will impose it in His last judgment, which is daily to be expected by us.

### Answer to the Third

The adversaries of our Churches have always objected to our party the *consensus* of the Fathers, of the Councils, and of the chief realms. And although we all — one and all — ought to be *zealous of consensus,* and no one, unless compelled by God's express mandate and by the reason of his own faith and conscience, ought to disturb or hinder consensus and public tranquillity without wounding his own conscience and violating his name before God and men — nevertheless it behoves us to be mindful of the divine rules: **"Thou shalt not bear false witness;"** *"We must obey God rather than men"* (Acts 5); *"Flee idols"* (1 Cor. 10); *"Beware false Prophets"* (Matt. 7); *"He who loves father or mother more than me, or son or daughter above me, is not worthy of me"* (Matt. 10).

Further, although we also always allege and rightly esteem the consensus of the chief Fathers and Councils, **nevertheless this consensus is *not the norm of doctrine,* but is a *testification* of the truth of doctrine — or of that very norm which is handed down and contained in the Prophetic and Apostolic writings.** Into which norm if Fathers, Councils, or others impinge, or set out certain things less properly or less purely, we ask nothing else to be conceded to us than the *dexterity of judgment* and *candour,* and — in disapproving *manifest errors which fight with the foundation of doctrine* — *liberty,* together with a consideration of the *perpetual sentence of the Fathers* (out of which the *improper sayings* which fall out even from all who speak diligently are not to be excerpted). Rather, the ***consensus of pure doctrine*** — that is, of the *articles of faith* and of the *chief loci of Evangelical doctrine* — is to be considered.

And when certain errors are detected, on account of which we nevertheless *do not throw away the writings of the Fathers,* we must return to the sources — that is, to the Prophetic and Apostolic writings and to the Symbols. For scarcely any age is there which does not have some errors — among whom nevertheless God from time to time in all ages excites some minds who teach more purely than others speaking less properly. And if in the ***foundation*** consensus in the Church is constituted (as it is necessary that it be), pious minds easily acquiesce, and can bear more placidly the *warts* of others, and do not immediately damn others speaking less properly, if in the *foundation* they agree with us.

And we name **the foundation** the Prophetic and Apostolic writings, the articles of the faith recensed in the Symbols, and the chief heads or Loci of heavenly doctrine rightly narrated — as it is written, Eph. 2: **"Ye are built upon the foundation of the Apostles and Prophets, Jesus Christ Himself being the chief corner-stone."** Afterwards, however, it must be known that *no consensus* can be entered upon in doctrine, if in the articles of faith and in the chief loci of Evangelical doctrine faith and confession are diverse, and not bound to one and the same *foundation.* And every consensus, in whatsoever mode it is exaggerated and commended, serving and yielding more to the *estimation and authority of men* than to the *truth of the word of God,* is a **heresy and a sect, a faction and a conspiracy,** and is dissolved with a sad end, as it were an *apostasy.* Nor can that be called a *Christian concord* which is instituted with the impious, and with those *erring in the foundation.* Rather (to use the words of our preceptors against the enemies of the doctrine of our Churches), *"Cursed be every charity which is adverse to the doctrine of Faith, to which all things must yield, but which itself yields to nothing."*

And since today dissensions increase beyond measure, **we affirm our consensus to be in the Prophetic and Apostolic writings and in the Symbols,** with which the Catechisms and Confessions, or writings, of *Dr. Luther,* and the *Body of Doctrine* composed by *Dr. Philip,* sound and teach the same. And because, among other things, in the doctrine of the ***Supper of the Lord*** — which the Son of God willed to be the *perpetual and closest bond of consensus, the norm, the pledge, and the testimony* — plausible-to-reason but too tragic and (in a certain manner) even *bloody* struggles are stirred up everywhere, our Churches of these regions confess — the *magistrate* himself, and the *chief men* who preside, and those who acknowledge themselves to owe *obedience to God and to the magistrate,* unanimously abhor from those struggles and *acquiesce with simple faith* in the words of Christ's Testament, and in the *repetition* of those words handed down by Paul the Apostle on the ***Presence, Manducation, Substance, Use, and Fruit of the Body and Blood of Christ in the Supper.*** And they adjoin themselves to these as to a place of *complete and open confession* — not to just-any books, which offend simpler readers with labyrinths of disputations, but *expressly and by name:*

- **Luther's writings and Confession of the Supper against the Zwinglians;**
- likewise the ***Body of Doctrine,*** especially the ***Apology;***
- **Dr. Pomeranus's opinion of the Supper,** as it stands extant in his writings;⁵⁰
- **Prince George of Anhalt's sermons on the Supper of the Lord;**⁵¹
- **Dr. Johann Matthesius's most grave and most pious sermons on the Supper;**⁵²
- and, according to these writings, **the *Repetition composed at Torgau,* and this year now published**⁵³ —

and they disapprove others teaching otherwise, and do not admit the *fanatical opinions* dissenting from the doctrine expounded and comprehended in those books. But God is to be prayed by us, that He may both govern them and make them one in Himself, unto the glory of His own divine name and unto our salvation.

### Answer to the Fourth Question

Some ask: **When two assemblies in the Church dissent from one another, and each assembly cites for itself Scripture, whence can it be known which senses rightly?**

We answer. **Where men can be convinced by manifest testimonies, the discrimination is easy** — as in *marriage,* in the *discrimination of foods,* in the *Mass,* in *both kinds* (as they call them) in the Supper, in the *invocation of God alone,* in the *righteousness of faith* by which alone we stand before God's tribunal, and in like matters. For even if many *pertinacious* ones are unwilling to yield to manifest testimonies, nevertheless by these they are refuted and confounded, and the truth shines even *without their assent* — until God Himself refutes their pertinacity, and shows that no wisdom is to be preferred to the divine discourse.

**But when some ambiguity, or some contradiction of sayings, appears to occur, the discrimination is more difficult** — as when *Arius* alleged the saying: *"The Father is greater than I;"* and the *Orthodox* alleged this: *"I and the Father are one"* (John 14, John 10 respectively). In rightly discriminating these, **four things** must be done.

**First of all,** it must be seen *whether anything is cited from a right and pious zeal for asserting the truth, or from calumny and perversion.* And this — not stupid or secure minds, but pious minds which consider the cause itself, and behold the *studies* of each party — can easily observe: namely, that the *true part* seeks *more* the truth and glory of God than any other thing; and from the sentiment of each party, one considers whether *impiety, blasphemy, bad conscience, danger of salvation, doubting of doctrine, contempt of ministry, and carnal security* arise; and whether one presses *more* on the judgment of mere human reason and on natural rules, or on the *omnipotence, truth, and glory* of God.

**Second,** it must be seen *where there are more testimonies of Scripture, clear, perspicuous, not foreign, not falsely or violently adduced.* For the ***consensus of the Prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles*** surpasses all human authority, and is the *norm and foundation of certitude.*

**Third,** the *testimonies of the first and purer Church* are also chiefly to be consulted — as of *Ignatius, Irenaeus, Tertullian, Justin Martyr, Cyprian, Basil, Nazianzen, Ambrose, Epiphanius, Augustine, Cyril,* and the like — so that the *confession of true doctrine* may be more alacritous, being strengthened by the *testification of the chief doctors of the Church,* and it may be able to be discerned *what is new and foreign to the sentiment of doctrine which they faithfully handed down,* and what has been either after their death or lately brought in by others, and obtruded on the Churches — that is, *what has always been unknown to the purer Church.*

**Fourth,** it must be seen *in which sayings there is an ambiguity, or a figure, or something else which appears to breed ambiguity,* when in reality there is no ambiguity or contradiction — even if sometimes some *species* (that is, the circumstance of time, place, person, purpose, occasion, and mode) appears to work out an *antilogy* and an *antinomy.* Which *species* needs only *conciliation according to the analogy of faith* — as: **"I and the Father are one"** — namely, by reason of *essence, eternity, omnipotence, majesty.* **"The Father is greater than I"** — namely, because *as Father, He sends me* to perform the work of the redemption of the human race, and because I *am made man,* who am willing to die for the human race.

This whole *commemoration* is certain to be true; and it is beyond doubt that it profits the pious, because from it they learn that **faith rests on the divine discourse,** and yet **the pious are confirmed by the testification of synods and by the writings and labours of others rightly teaching.** Now we shall consider the *words of the Symbols.*

## QUAESTIO — Why It Is Necessary and Useful Daily to Recite the Symbol

The causes are many, of which the *chief* are these:

**I.** That *before God you may profess* what you sense concerning Him — that is, concerning the *essence and will of God* — and render Him worship, since you sense that He is the *true God whom you invoke.*

**II.** That you may *separate* your confession and invocation from the *ethnic* and *Turkish* invocation, which is ignorant of God's essence and will. For these are the two chief distinctions between true and false invocation: the first *concerning the essence of God;* the second, *concerning the will of God.*

**III.** That you may be *reminded* of the chief works and benefits of God toward us all and each — as: **Creation, Patefaction, Redemption, Sanctification, Gathering, Preservation, and Salvation of the Church**⁵⁴ — and stir up your mind to *thanksgiving* for so many and so great benefits, of which we are unworthy.

**IIII.** That by true faith you may *apply to yourself* the benefits of the Son of God. For the *pious mind* by this very recitation determines that the benefits of the Son of God *pertain to himself,* and therefore says: ***I believe.*** And *Bernard's* saying is often alleged in this connection: *"It is necessary first of all to believe that you cannot have remission of sins except by God's indulgence — but add, that you may believe also this, that ***through Him, to YOU,*** sins are forgiven. This is the testimony which the Holy Spirit gives in your heart, saying: Thy sins are forgiven thee"* (Mark 2, Luke 5).⁵⁵

**V.** That in the greatest perils and temptations you may have a *present and firm consolation,* and against the *fiery darts of Satan* oppose the articles of faith by which you may confound all his ambushes and assaults. As, against the temptation of *rejection,* oppose: **"I believe in God the Father."** If Father, therefore He rejects me not. Against the *sense of sin:* **"I believe the remission of sins."** Against the sense of *God's wrath and damnation:* **"I believe in Jesus Christ the Saviour,"** and so on.

**VI.** That you may put to flight and drive away the *Devil* hearing this your profession.

**VII.** That you may accustom yourself to *considering the testimonies of doctrine, the patefactions of God, and the miracles.*

## The Customary Division of the Symbols

Customarily, the Symbol is distributed into **three Articles** or grades, according to the three Persons of the Divinity. **The first** is *of Creation.* **The second** is *of Redemption.* **The third** is *of Sanctification.*

A **subdivision** was added by antiquity, that the *second* article of the Apostolic Symbol be distributed into *six,* and the *third* into *five smaller articles* — according to the number of the twelve Apostles.

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

# Article I — *Credo in Deum Patrem Omnipotentem*

> **Credo in (unum) Deum, Patrem Omnipotentem, Creatorem (factorem) caeli et terrae (visibilium omnium et invisibilium).**

**I believe in (one) God, the Father Almighty, Creator (maker) of heaven and earth (of all things visible and invisible).**

## The Order of Treatment

Here we must speak in order concerning these five heads:

**I.** *Quid sit Credere,* or *what Faith is;* and *of how many kinds is the acknowledgement of God.* Likewise, *what it is to believe God, to believe in God, and to believe God* — and *for what causes each one ought to say:* ***I believe in God.***

**II.** *Of the one Essence of God, and of the three Persons,* and of their *properties* and *distinctions.* Likewise, *what God is, what Essence is, what Person is.*

**III.** *What we ought to believe concerning the first Person of the Trinity,* according to the words of the Symbol.

**IV.** *Of the Creation of things* — what it is, and whose *Person's* work it is; likewise, *of the grades of created things,* *of the Angels, of Man, of the Image of God, of the fourfold state of man.*

**V.** *Of the Providence of God, of contingency, of the Fall, of free will, of sin, and of the Law.*

***

## Part I — *Quid Sit Credere:* What It Is to Believe

### The Two Senses of the Word *Credere*

**To believe,** in the first sense, signifies, in divine matters, *a general knowledge of the history and profession of the articles of heavenly doctrine, but without a firm confidence acquiescing in God.* This is called by the ancients **historical Faith,** of which James speaks in ch. 2: ***The demons also believe, and tremble.*** And in the Church a part of men has a general and historical faith in the doctrine, in the sacraments, in the piety to be instituted, and in the judgement.

**To believe,** in the second sense, signifies *a special assent and firm confidence, acquiescing in the promise of God, and by faith applying to itself the merit and benefits of the Son of God.* This is called ***true, living, and justifying Faith,*** which the living members of the Church have — according to that: *"The just shall live by his own faith"* (Hab. 2). For as Faith is twofold — one which James condemns as vanity and folly, since it is only historical notice; the other which is true and living — so also in the Church there are two kinds of members, dead and living. The **dead** are those who indeed hear, know, and profess the dogmas of the Church, but without faith and without penitence — that is, they are only *hypocrites intermixed among the good,* even if they hear the word and use the sacraments. Such Paul, in the use of the Lord's Supper, names *unworthy,* that is, not undertaking penitence.²⁸ But the **living** members of the Church are all who *truly believe,* who have a faith which is not dead but justifying, and at the same time efficacious through its own fruits — that is, undertaking penitence.

### The Definition of True Faith

Hence the definition of true faith is customarily thus handed down:

> **Christian Faith is the notice of the whole doctrine handed down by God, and the assent by which we embrace the whole doctrine — and in this the promise of the gratuitous reconciliation, or of the imputation of righteousness given for the sake of Christ — and the confidence acquiescing in Christ the Mediator according to the promise, and determining with certainty that sins are remitted to us, that we are pleasing to God, that we are received and heard, for the sake of Christ alone, gratuitously, not for the sake of our own or others' worthiness.**

### The Ancient Distinction: Faith as *Apprehensiva* and *Quietativa*

The ancients said: Faith is *apprehensiva* and *quietativa.* For in the *cogitative faculty* there is *apprehension,* that is, notice and assent. And in the *heart and will* there is a movement or *confidence* acquiescing in the object apprehended.

### The Isaiah 26 and Hebrews 11 Definitions

**Isaiah 26** defines faith in this way: ***Iezer samuch thizor schalom, schalom ki becha batuach*** — that is, *"Faith is a formation (an impression, a cogitation, an apprehension of the object) sustained (firm, stable, sustained, leaning — that is, given and confirmed by God and leaning on the word, and lacking doubt), keeping peace, peace I say, because in Thee is one's hope."*⁵⁶

**Hebrews 11** carries this definition: *"Faith is the ὑπόστασις of things hoped for, and the ἔλεγχος of things not appearing."* The ***ὑπόστασις*** is *expectation* — that is, a firm and constant *expecting confidence* — by which what is believed is determined to *stand,* as it were *substantially* (*ὑποστατικῶς*) and *in effect present, complete.*⁵⁷ The ***ἔλεγχος*** is *conviction* and *immovable proof* — that is, the highest certitude and conclusion confirmed by the word, promise, examples, testimonies, and internal motions. The ***τὰ ἐλπιζόμενα*** ("things hoped for") are things future — the help of God in adverse matters, deliverance which is certainly hoped for even if the adversity endures for some time; the resurrection of bodies; the day of judgement; eternal life. The ***τὰ οὐ βλεπόμενα*** ("things not seen") are all things either long ago done or now present but not subjected to the eyes — as, the doctrine concerning God, Creation, the Fall, the benefits of Christ, the justification of man, and so on.

*(A marginal cross-reference: Wisd. 16 uses the same participial idiom* — ***στάσις σε τῶν σῶν γλυκύτητα πρὸς τέκνα ἐνεφάνισεν*** — *of the divine sweetness "standing forth" toward His children.)*

### The Two Kinds of Acknowledgement of God — Universal and Peculiar

As of *twofold Faith* and of *twofold members of the Church* it has been said, so also of the ***acknowledgement of God*** it must be said that one is *universal,* and the other *peculiar.*

**The universal acknowledgement of God** is that which is common in the whole race of men, both outside and within the Church — as, that God is, and *what kind of being* God is, namely an *eternal mind, the cause of the good in nature;* and that there is *providence.* Of this universal acknowledgement Paul speaks in Rom. 1 and 2.

**The peculiar acknowledgement of God** is proper to the Church, in which the *whole doctrine* is handed down concerning *the essence of God, the three Persons, the Creation, the Fall, sin, the Law, the Gospel, justification, good works, the sacraments, the Cross, the Resurrection, and eternal life.* And this peculiar acknowledgement of God is arisen out of the ***patefaction*** of God Himself — who, by the express word of the Law and of the Gospel, and by the *testimonies* added, either for the confirmation of the word (as miracles are) or for the confirmation of confidence (as sacrifices were, and as the sacraments are), has revealed to us His essence and will.

And this acknowledgement also is either *historical only,* literal, and *carnal* — the same with the *historical faith* — in those not regenerated, but hypocrites, secure, mere philosophers, and not undertaking penitence: or is *spiritual* and *internal, efficacious for life and salvation,* or ***salutary,*** proper to those truly believing and to the sons of God, converted and regenerated — of which is said in John 17: ***This is life eternal, that they know Thee, the only true God, and Him whom Thou hast sent, Jesus to be the Christ.***

### The Two Confessions — βουλητική and προαιρετική

In the schools they sometimes name *confessio* ***βουλητική*** and *confessio* ***προαιρετική.*** That is called ***βουλητική*** in which the words indeed of the Church are recited, but the mind is *turned away from God* — as the devils and the secure can also employ and recite certain forms concerning the articles of faith, not to the end that they may render glory to God, or that they themselves may be converted, but *by custom,* or *to deceive others.* But the ***προαιρετική*** acknowledgement, faith, and confession is that in which *the mind of the confessor is turned toward God,* and looks to a *congruent end,* and renders to God and to the Son of God the *due honour* from the soul and from the heart in true confidence and humility. Of this confession Paul speaks in 1 Cor. 12: ***No one can say "the Lord Jesus" except by the Holy Spirit*** — namely, not by a *βουλητική* but by a ***προαιρετική*** confession, and by perseverance in the whole doctrine.⁵⁸

### *Credere Deum, Credere Deo, Credere in Deum*

**To believe God** (*credere Deum*) is *to attribute essence to God,* that He is God.

**To believe God** (*credere Deo,* dative) is *to attribute truth to God, to assent to His word.*

**To believe in God** (*credere in Deum,* accusative) is *by a firm confidence to acquiesce in God, and in the divine promises, and to subject oneself wholly and humbly to the word of God* — as Gen. 15: ***Abraham believed in the Lord, and it was reputed to him for righteousness.***

Some — and indeed rightly — speak thus: **Credere Deum** is *notice;* **Credere Deo** is *assent;* **Credere in Deum** is *firm confidence.* And all these one word of Faith embraces.

### The Five Causes Why Each Ought to Say "I Believe in God"

The causes for which each ought to say ***I believe in God*** are chiefly these five:

**1.** That you may *discern your faith* from the *intuitive notice* which contemplates things subjected to the eyes.

**2.** That you may *show that you detest* the Academic doubting and suspension lacking a certain assent concerning God and the divine matters.

**3.** That you may *testify* that you have not only historical faith, but a truly Christian one, and ***πληροφορίαν*** (full assurance) — and that you *confide* in God, in mind, will, and heart — in notice, assent, and confidence.⁵⁹

**4.** That you may *show* that you are certain of the *application* of the benefits of God, that they are applied to *you the believer* — and that by your own faith (which is *special*) you apprehend the promises of God, and do not have only a general faith.

**5.** That you may *confess* that you *reject all idols,* which are nothing (1 Cor. 8), and that you simply *place all your confidence in God patefacted to you in the word,* propounded to you to be believed, and *acquiesce in Him without contradiction,* and *are willing to persevere constantly,* whatever reason or the judgement of others *not consenting to the word of God* may bring forth to the contrary.

<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
