## The Phil. 2 Exegesis and the Three Similitudes for the Personal Union

### On Phil. 2: *Qui cum esset in forma Dei*

> ***"Qui cum esset in forma Dei, non rapinam duxit esse aequalem Deo, sed seipsum exinanivit, formam servi accipiens, in similitudinem hominum factus, et habitu inventus ut homo, humiliavit semetipsum, factus obediens usque ad mortem, mortem verò crucis: quapropter etiam Deus ipsum exaltavit (in summam sublimitatem extulit, ὑπερύψωσε) et donavit ei nomen quod est super omne nomen, ut in nomine Iesu omne genu se flectat caelestium, terrestrium et inferorum, et omnis lingua confiteatur quod Dominus Iesus Christus est in gloria Dei Patris."***

**"Who, when He was in the form of God, did not judge equality with God to be rapine, but emptied Himself, taking the form of a servant, made in the similitude of men, and found in habit as a man, humbled Himself, made obedient unto death, and the death of the cross: wherefore God also has exalted Him (elevated Him into the highest sublimity, ὑπερύψωσε) and gifted Him the name which is above every name, that in the name of Jesus every knee may bow of heavenly, earthly, and infernal, and every tongue confess that the Lord Jesus Christ is in the glory of God the Father."**

Great is the variety of the interpretations. We shall rehearse, however, the *most-usual and most-simple* sentence.

### *Qui cum esset in forma Dei*

That is: *when He was such as God is,* namely, ***Omnipotens, aeternus, συναΐδιος, καὶ ὁμοούσιος καὶ ὁμότιμος καὶ ὁμοδύναμος.*** For Paul says ***ὑπάρχων*** — *existing* — that is, *when He was,* not *when He began to be,* but ***when He was, Iehova,*** per that: *"In the beginning was the Word, and God was the Word."* And Paul says *not simply* ***"God and man,"*** but ***"in the form of God"*** and ***"the form of man"*** — because *not only the substances of the natures* (namely, the divinity, and afterwards the humanity), *but also the qualities* by which each nature is *clothed* — that is, the *glory and majesty befitting God alone,* and the *imbecility of human nature* — he proposes to us to be considered.

***μορφή*** — that is, *form* — signifies *nature.* ***μορφὴ Θεοῦ, φύσις Θεοῦ, οὐκ ἐνέργεια· καὶ μορφὴ δούλου, φύσις δούλου, οὐκ ἐνέργεια,*** says **Oecumenius,** *against Paul of Samosata,* who said that *the Lord is a mere man,* ***λεγόμενος ὑπὸ Θεοῦ*** — *from God efficaciously fashioned and made partaker.* Nor is there any doubt but that the Apostle Paul referred to the *phrasis* which David used in 1 Chr. 17: *"Thou dost behold me in the form of a man, who art in the highest, Iehovah God."* ר ו ת ב = *in the form,* or *in the disposition and lineaments and operations of a man,* or *according to the form and disposition of a man,* or *according to the order of a man,* as *the condition and property of human nature bears.* For ר ו ת signifies *ordination made with great inquisition and deliberation.* The Septuagint rendered ὅρασιν: ***ἐπεῖδές με ὡς ὅρασις ἀνθρώπου, ὕψωσάς με κύριε ὁ Θεός.*** 2 Sam. 7 has ת ר ו ת — *constitution, dignity, and grade.* The Syriac in Paul retained the word ***Demuth,*** of *similitude,* namely, *of substance and qualities.* For God is *plainly similar to Himself,* and there is *identity, or* ***ταυτότης,*** *of substance and qualities.* **Augustine** *De fide ad Petrum Diaconum* says:

> *"By the name of 'form' you ought to understand natural plenitude. In the form of God the Lord Jesus Christ was, because in the nature of God the Father He always was, of whom He is born. He is therefore one nature with the Father, equally and coeternally with Him, and immense; equally immortal and incommutable, equally invisible and inenarrable, equally good and just, equally-Merciful, patient and much-merciful and truthful; equally strong and sweet, equally wise and Almighty. In that plenitude He is God, in the form of God equal to God. And the form of God has not lost the plenitude, in which He always is eternal and incommutable Lord; remaining in the form of God true God, of the same nature as the Father is. In the form of God with the Father and the Holy Spirit He is the one only Fabricator of all things, God."*

### *Non rapinam duxit*

**"He did not judge it rapine"** — because it *is not rapine, but* ***nature,*** *that the Son with the Father is equal in divinity.* Rightly **Faustus the Bishop** says: ***"The equality of divinity which the rebellious Angel willed to assume by rapine, Christ possesses by nature."***

*He did not judge it rapine* — that is, *even if by His own right He could have used it,* and *inflicted injury on no one,* even if by *His divine majesty and equality with the Father He might have shaken off every yoke of servitude,* yet ***He yielded His own right,*** and *did not act contrary to His mission and vocation;* nor did He use His own power, which He could most freely have used had He willed; nor did He *snatch the use of Omnipotence for His own defence.*

*"Rapinam ducere"* is *(as it were in a riddle)* opposed to what one has *properly* and *by nature.* He did *not think* Himself to have ***τὸ εἶναι ἴσα Θεῷ*** (***θεότητα καὶ ἰσότητα,*** *divinity and equality*) ***ἁρπαγήν,*** by rapine — but He knew Himself to be *truly God* ***οὐσιωδῶς,*** and this *to be* to Him ***φυσικὸν καὶ ἴδιον*** (as Oecumenius speaks) — that is, *an eternal, natural property* — to be ***ἴσον καὶ ὁμοδύναμον*** to God the Father. **This** ***φυσικόν καὶ συναΐδιον ἀξίωμα*** **is His.** This is the true sentence of this phrase.

### Arius's Abuse — and the Response

***Arius,*** however, abusing this phrase, thus argued: *"To seize is to take by force what one does not have by nature. Not to seize is even not to receive — much less to have by nature. Christ did not seize equality with God. Therefore He does not have eternal equality with the Father."*

**The response to the Pauline phrase is plain.** Christ did not seize *because He had it by nature.* The divinity of Christ is *not acquired* (***θεότης ἐπίκτητος***), but is ***οὐσιώδης.*** Therefore Paul says ***ὑπάρχων*** — *when He was in the form of God* — he does not say ***γενόμενος,*** *made in the form of God.* ***τὸ δὲ εὐμορφῇ Θεοῦ ὑπάρχειν, ὄξιμ ἴσομ δύναμιν τοῦ ἐν οὐσίᾳ Θεῷ ὑπάρχειν, καὶ τῆς θείας οὐσίας ἰδιότητα ἔχειν,*** says **Basil** *contra Eunomium* (cited by Theodoret Dial. 3 p. 43).

**That Paul uses this phrase** (*non rapuit,* or *rapinam duxit*) He does by a certain collation and respect to *those things which happen in the world.* Christ *by nature is eternal God and Excelsus.* But *in the world* often *men are made excelsi and are not excelsi by nature;* they are made excelsi for the most part ***ἁρπαγῆς,*** *by rapine,* and ***πλεονεξίαν,*** by *avarice,* and thus procure to themselves and acquire *sublimity by marvellous arts* which they do not have *by nature.* Man by rapine and by eating the fruit *willed to be as God.* And in the Church *the violent seize the kingdom of the heavens,* and *some violence is needed for obtaining the right of salvation.* So therefore Paul's exhortation now proceeds: *He who by nature is God and Excelsus,* and *from elsewhere had nothing of glory seized to Himself,* ***humbled Himself below all of us on our account.*** Therefore *we,* who *by nature are humble, wretched, and abject,* and without much zeal and sweat, and above all *without God's peculiar favour have nothing of glory,* *ought not to be proud, but in true humility to serve one to another.*¹³⁵

### ***τὸ εἶναι ἴσα Θεῷ***

That is, ***τὴν θεότητα.*** ***ἴσον εἶναι*** is *to be equal,* ***ὁμότιμον καὶ ὁμοδύναμον,*** *equal to God the Father.* ***ἴσα Θεῷ*** is *to be in all things and per omnia equal to God.* *He did not judge rapine that very thing which is "equal to God,"* that is, *the equality of God.* For the plural neuter adjective is set down in place of a singular appellative, ***συνθετικῶς.***

### *Seipsum exinanivit* — *ἑαυτὸν ἐκένωσε*

That is: *He took up the human nature.* For the ***exinanitio*** comprehends two things:

**1.** The ***ἐνανθρώπησιν*** itself. ***Gregory Nyssen*** says: *"What is more miserable than the form of a servant, if compared to God? Or what so unworthy of the King of the whole universe as to demit Himself and come into the society of our imbecile and mendicant nature?"*

**2.** The *as-it-were occultation* of the divinity and divine power — that in the state of humiliation, and especially in the passion, the divine majesty and power did *not manifestly exert itself in the human nature,* nor exercise its own strengths, but *rested* — not that it was ever absent or separated, or that it had *lost its plenitude,* but that it was not manifestly employed in the assumed human nature.

***ἐκένωσε*** — *He evacuated* — is from ***κενός*** (*empty, vacuous, devoid of operation-use*), as 1 Cor. 15: *"The grace of God not* ***κενή,*** *not inefficacious, not idle."* For the power of the divinity of the Logos *with the Father and the Holy Spirit* everywhere and always *administers all things potently and efficaciously from eternity to eternity,* even at the time when that ***exinanitio*** was: but in the *truly assumed human nature* it *rested at the time of exinanition,* and *its operation, as though covered by the infirmities, it hid and drew back for the most part.*

- **Cyril of Alexandria** *ad Theodoretum:* *"The exinanition is: for God the Word, not knowing mutation, to do something and to say something of those things which are human — on account of the dispensative conventum ad carnem. But even if He was made man, in no way did the reason of the sacrament wound Him in His own nature. For He remained what He was, even in descending to the humanity for the world's salvation and life."*
- **Macarius** Homily 32: *"Each indeed — both His magnitude and His exility — is equally incomprehensible. And His dispensation is found to be directed and destined to tribulations and passions and stigmas, and to all things bitter and adverse; all these things on account of your soul and your life He undertook."*
- **Basil** in Ep. *ad Caesarienses:* *"The Son is therefore less than the Father, because on your account He died, made a corpse, that He might liberate you from mortality, and make you a partaker of the celestial life; that He might recover the lost sheep, and lead back the one going down to Jericho surrounded by robbers, into His own homeland, restored to pristine health."*
- **Augustine** Ep. 66: *"For Christ, to be equal to God in the form of God, was not rapine but nature — since it was assumed by being born, not presumed by pride. But therefore the Father is said to be greater, because He emptied Himself, taking the form of a servant, not losing that of God. On account of which servile form, not only than the Father, but also than Himself and the Holy Spirit He was made less; and not only than this most-excellent Trinity, but even was diminished a little below the Angels. He was also less than men, when He was subject to His parents,"* and so on.¹³⁶

### *Formam servi accipiens*

***φύσιν δούλου*** — that is, ***ἄνθρωπος ἐγένετο.*** A *servant,* however, is:

**1.** By reason of the *exinanition.*
**2.** By reason of the *office,* by which He served the human race.
**3.** By reason of the *desertion,* by which He alone labours, and has *no defence in the passion.*

He says ***λαβὼν γενόμενος,*** and does *not* say ***ὑπάρχων ἐν μορφῇ δούλου,*** as before of the divinity he said. For *by nature He is the eternal God.* But the *human nature* is ***κτιστή, ἡ προσληφθεῖσα,*** *assumed.*

### *Factus in similitudine hominum*

That is: *He was a man, similar to others, passible and mortal, but without sin.* ***ἐν ὁμοιώματι ἀνθρώπων γενόμενος*** — that is, ***ὅμοιος τοῖς ἄλλοις ἀνθρώποις γενόμενος.*** It is a Hebraism. And rightly the Greek interpreters noted that it is said ***ὁμοίωμα ἀνθρώπου,*** because Christ had many things also ***ὑπὲρ ἄνθρωπον,*** which today we name ***ὑπερφυσικά*** — as: *that He was born beyond the series of things,* ***ἀνωμοιάς,*** and had many *prerogatives* exceeding the *common course and order of nature.* Whence **Ambrose** on Rom. 1 says: *"Christ is above man, since He differs from the human law of nativity."*

### *Habitu inventus vt homo*

That is: *by the senses and affections* — *hunger, thirst, joy, sadness, mercy, tears, wrath, indignation.* For ***σχῆμα*** signifies *the dispositions and affections of the flesh.*

### *Humiliauit semetipsum, factus obediens usque ad mortem*

That is: *He did not repel death, which He could have repelled, but since He knew that this was the will of the eternal Father, that thus the penalty be paid, He willed this obedience,* and *demonstrated honour and reverence toward the Father unto the extreme and worst kind of death.*

### *Quapropter Deus ipsum exaltavit*

**Basil** [warns]: *"If after the Incarnation on account of obedience the Father gifted to the Son of God the name which is above every name, and every tongue confesses this Lord — then He was made greater after the Incarnation than He was before the Incarnation — which is absurd. Such also is this: 'All power is given to me in heaven and on earth.' These things it behoves to understand of the humanity, not of the divinity."*

**Augustine** *Meditationes* bk. 1, ch. 15: *"Christ, glorifying the human nature with the holy stole of resurrection and immortality, carried it above all the heavens, above all the choirs of Angels, above the Cherubim and Seraphim, placing it at the right of God the Father. Him the Angels praise, the Dominations adore,"* and so on.

**Hence the Arians took the argument:** *"Nature-God cannot be exalted. Christ is exalted. Therefore He is not by nature and from eternity God."*

**The Orthodox always responded to the minor:** *Christ is exalted* — namely, *according to the human nature,* not according to the divine, which from eternity to eternity is *Omnipotent* and *has glory and perfection through and in Itself,* as **Epiphanius** *contra Arianos* speaks.

- **Athanasius:** *"God super-exalted Him, and gifted Him the name which is above every name. He speaks of the temple of His body, not of the divinity. For the Excelsus is not exalted, but the flesh of the Excelsus is exalted. And to the flesh of the Excelsus He gifted the name which is above every name."* And again *contra Arianos:* *"The Lord's glory is not glorified, but the flesh of the Lord accepts glory, ascending with Him into heaven."* The same, in the same place: *"Whatever Scripture says the Son has received (namely, in time) and been glorified, on account of His humanity it says, not on account of His divinity."*

Theodoret rehearses these same words in Dial. 6 fol. 41. And fol. 44 he adds the words of **Gregory Nyssen:** *"Who has been exalted by the right of God? The humble or the Excelsus? But what is humble, if not that which is human? What is Excelsus, if not that which is divine? But God, who is always Excelsus, does not need exaltation. Wherefore the Apostle says that which is human has been exalted — and which by the union the Lord has drawn up to His own sublimity."*

- **Augustine:** *"Christ according to the man is Lord of the celestial, terrestrial, and infernal."*

And hither pertain the rules of antiquity:
- **Athanasius:** *"When Scripture speaks of the glorification of Christ, it speaks of the flesh which has received the glory."*
- **Hilary:** *"What is of time received its glory from the glory of clarity, which is without time."*
- **Leo:** *"Whatever Christ received in time, He received according to the man, on whom the things He did not have are conferred. For according to the divine power of the Word, indifferently all things which the Father has, also the Son has."*
- **Ambrose:** *"The magnificence which the Son of God had eternally by nature, the Son of the Virgin was to receive in time by grace, and to have eternally."*
- **Augustine:** *"Whatever Christ the man does not have in His human nature by nature, that He has by grace — namely of the Logos, which assumed it."*
- **Cyril:** *"To have received glory, power, and kingdom over all things, is to be referred to the conditions of the humanity."*

These rules antiquity used against the Arians; and one who sensed otherwise, they held for an Arian — as **Leo** *ad Clerum et plebem Urbis Constantinopolitanae* manifestly writes: ***"Him who thinks otherwise, Arius snatches into his own society, whose perversity is much favoured by this impiety of theirs, when namely it is said, 'The Son has been exalted not according to the human, but only according to the divine nature.'"***

**But** — we shall speak of the *glorification of the human nature in Christ* below. Now this admonition too must be added: ***Obedience does not take away equality of power.*** And it was *one time of humiliation,* another *of glorification.* Therefore the saying *"Christ has been exalted"* is not to be received of *the time of humiliation* (in which the Logos *rested,* as before concerning the Exinanition was said), but *of the time of glorification and liberation from death,* or of the *end and cessation of the exinanition.* And this admonition does not disagree from the sentence of antiquity, but *shows that in the glorification of the human nature in Christ at length the Logos exerted His divine power revealed, acknowledged, and celebrated,* and *adorned His assumed human nature with divine and ineffable glory at the right of the Father.*

And in this sentence **Oecolampadius** on the saying *"Higher than the heavens made"* rightly says: *"These things are to be understood not of the divinity — since God is not exalted — but of the humanity of Christ."*¹³⁷

### The Alternative Explanation — Erasmus and Others

I have rehearsed most-simply the *usual* sentence of Paul's saying. I shall add, however, an *alternative one* — of **Erasmus** and others — a singular one, which although it does *not correspond to the prior* in explication of the vocable ***τῆς μορφῆς,*** must by no means be exploded.

*Christ, even though He was in the form of God* — namely, on account of the union, since the assumed human nature was in the Person of the Logos, and thus was not *the form of God itself,* which is *the very* ***θεότης τοῦ Λόγου,*** *but* was *in the form of God,* *enriched and made-abundant with divine operations* — *nevertheless did not always perform such gestures as He who is in the form of God could have performed,* because in the manger He lies, hungers, thirsts, grieves, suffers, dies, in the form of a servant — that is, *utterly as a servant and abject, humbles Himself, until He complete the work of His obedience.* Afterwards, the servile form and exinanition laid down, He is *exalted,* and so on.

**This explanation** considers only the *human nature in Christ* and its operations, and takes ***μορφή*** *not of nature and essence,* but *of gestures and external demonstrations by which is shown either divine majesty or human imbecility.* And to this sentence *cognate* is what **Ambrose** rehearses, saying: *"He was in the form of God, because although He seemed a man, He did the works of God, so that in things done He might appear to be God, who was seen as man. Retaining, however, His virtue, that in Him — as He was seen as man — it should not appear."*

**Dr. Luther** also retains this sentence in his exposition of the Sunday Epistle for Palm Sunday.

Whatever be said, **in each sentence the personal union is shown** (of which we here chiefly treat), and the *consensus is of the same doctrine* against the enemies of the Son of God who assail either His divine or His human nature.

The *prior sentence,* which we rehearsed, takes the *form of God* of the *eternal divinity of the Son.* The *posterior sentence* takes the *form of God,* and what Paul adds, of the *assumed human nature in the Person of the Logos.*

### On *"Forma Dei"* vs *"in forma Dei"*

Some also inquire, what is the *discrimen* between these: ***"Christ is the form of God"*** and ***"Christ is in the form of God."***

**Those who retain the latter explication of Paul's saying** respond: *"Christ is the form of God"* — that is, *the essential and eternal Image of God the Father, according to the divine nature.* *"Christ is in the form of God,"* according to the *human nature, which is assumed by the Logos, and adorned with divine operations, power, and majesty* — just as also this: *"Christ is the right of God"* according to the divine nature; *"Christ is at the right of God,"* according to the human nature.

**The older** answered thus: *"Christ is the form of God,"* that is, the *Image of the Father.* *And He also is in the form of God* — that is, in the *nature and essence of God,* since with the Father and the Holy Spirit He is *eternal God.* For **form** — *which gives being to the thing* — they explicated by the word ***τῆς φύσεως*** (*of nature*).

### Pauline Antithesis Against the Heretics — Oecumenius

But let this be the mode of subtleties, and let the *pious sentence of antiquity be consulted,* which used Paul's words *against the heresies* directed at Christ's Person — as **Oecumenius** says: *"See how from few words of the Spirit all heresies are dissolved. First, saying* ***ὑπάρχων*** *(when He was), he manifested that which is coeternal with the Father,* ***τὸ συναΐδιον τοῦ Πατρί,*** *and destroyed Arius; then Marcion of Pontus. For if He was in the form of God, how do you say that from Mary He began? With this Paul of Samosata is refuted, who said the Lord is a mere man, from God* ***ἐνεργούμενον*** *fashioned with efficacy. And now we ought to say to him: as the form of a servant is the nature of a servant, not the efficacy, so the form of God is the nature of God, and not the efficacy. And Nestorius sensing the same is overturned. With these Marcellus of Galatia, and Sophronius, and Photinus are subverted. See also Sabellius falling. 'Non rapinam duxit quod esset aequalis Deo.' For "equal" is not said of one person, but of two and many. How therefore do you say the naked names of one hypostasis said of the Father, of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit? See secondly Arius struck. For let us say to him: What is 'the form of a servant He accepted'? It is evident that He was made man. Therefore also 'having the form of God' being said, He was God. For the diction of 'form' is adjoined to two — of God and of man,"* and so on.¹³⁸

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

## The Three Similitudes for the Personal Union

**These things having been rehearsed,** now we shall recount the *similitudes by which antiquity used to adumbrate the hypostatic union in some measure.*

**"Our mysteries,"** says **Justin,** *"are* ***ὑπὲρ νόμον, ὑπὲρ λόγον, καὶ ὑπερκατάληψιν τῆς κτιστῆς φύσεως*** *(above law, above reason, and beyond the grasp of created nature). Therefore when doubting occurs, faith supported by God's word must be brought forward, which is the most-ready solution. But to inquire of divine things 'How?' is a manifest sign of unbelief. For the explication of this sacrament transgresses even the measure of the holy Apostles — nay, of the whole creature. I know that the Word was made Flesh, but 'how' I know not. Are you astonished that I know not? Every creature is ignorant,"* says **Chrysostom.**

The similitudes are ***three.***

### The First Similitude — the Rational Soul and the Body

**The first is rehearsed in the Athanasian Symbol** in these words:

> ***"Sicut anima rationalis, et Caro, unus est homo: ita Deus et homo unus est Christus."***
>
> *"As the rational soul, and the flesh, is one man; so God and man is one Christ."*

**The union of the soul and the body in man** is the mode of union *which most closely approaches the similitude of the hypostatic union of the two natures in Christ.* Gravely and truly, however, **Justin** says: *"The example fits, if not altogether, at least in part. For just as a man, since he is one, has in himself two diverse natures, and by one of his parts he counsels, by the other executes the counselled — with the mind decreeing the structure of the ship, and with the hands completing the decreed work — so the one Son, since He is of twin nature, according to one performs miracles, according to the other receives what is humble."*

**Diligently it must be considered where the similitude fits, and where it does not fit.**

**It fits in these:**

**I.** As *each man is only one man, not two, and yet consists of two distinct natures, body and soul coupled;* so *Christ is one, or one Person, having two distinct natures — divine and human — coupled to each other by personal union.*

**II.** As *the soul of a man and the body of a man, each one has its own proper and distinct nature and its own idiomata,* as long as the man lives, *and there is no mutation or commixtion of one nature into the other,* but *the body is and remains body,* and *never is mutated into the soul,* and *the soul is and remains soul,* never turned into the body — and the same must be established of the *properties* — so *in Christ's Person the divine nature is and remains divine,* and *never is mutated into another nature;* and *the human nature never is converted into the divine nature;* but *each nature retains its own properties.*

**And the differences of each nature always are and remain — which briefly can thus be rehearsed:**

**1.** The *divine nature in Christ is eternal,* and *lacks beginning,* since *"In the beginning was the Word,"* ***λόγος οὐσιώδης καὶ συναΐδιος τῷ Πατρί.*** But the *human nature 1,574 years ago began to be,* assumed in the womb of the Virgin Mary, and *before the assumption was not.*

**2.** The *divine nature in Christ is Creator* — for through the Logos all things were made, and without Him was made nothing which was made. But the *human nature is a creature of God,* by the operation of the Holy Spirit in the womb of the ever-virgin Mary, from the sanctified flesh and blood of Mary, created.

**3.** The *divine nature in Christ is Spirit,* or *spiritual essence, not having flesh and bones* (John 20). But the *human nature is a corporeal creature,* not only in soul, but also in body, flesh, bones, and human members constant.

**4.** The *divine nature in Christ,* when it is said to be Almighty, Wise, Just, and so on, is so said because *its essence is such* — or because *it is itself the eternal Omnipotence, Wisdom, and Justice* — and this is its *essential idiom, to be Almighty and All-knowing.* But the *human nature in Christ is not the very Omnipotence, and is not the very Wisdom and eternal Justice* (otherwise it would be mutated into the divine essence), *even though it has all power in heaven and on earth given to itself.*

**5.** The *divine nature in Christ has all things from eternity, and received nothing from the human nature.* But the *human nature received from the divinity of the Logos certain gifts given to itself — the divine glory* with the cult of adoration in the Person of Christ, and the *majesty in heaven and on earth.* Whence **Cyril** says: *"It is proper of the human nature to receive from God. Christ therefore as man by grace receives from the deity what, as God, He has naturally; and from Himself He Himself receives, as man, from Him as God."* And **Eusebius** bk. 4, ch. 13: *"The Word from Himself communicated His own things to man; not, however, in turn from the mortal received into Himself the things which are his; but He supplied the divine virtue to the mortal, and did not in turn — for the sake of the mortal's consortium — was the nature of the Word detracted."* Likewise **Basil:** *"The human flesh of the Lord was made partaker of the divinity; it did not deliver to the divinity its own imbecility."* And **Augustine** *contra Felicianum:* *"By the injury of His own body I do not confess the divinity to have been afflicted, just as by the majesty of the divinity we know the flesh to have been glorified."* And *the same all the orthodox both sensed and wrote always,* nor is here need of *heaping up testimonies.*

**6.** The *divine nature is an infinite essence, never and nowhere circumscribed.* But the *human nature is finite,* and *per se* ***περίγραπτη.***

**7.** The *divinity is incommutable* (Num. 23), *impassible, immortal,* existing and remaining *always in one and the same state and grade.* But the *human nature,* in the state of humiliation, *needed food, drink, sleep, and suffered and died, was pressed, and was exalted, and evected into the highest sublimity, and to the seat of the glory and majesty of the divine.*

**III. Return to the similitude.** As the *soul is so united with the body* that, *even if by itself, if it were alone, it could exercise and expedite its own actions* (as it does when separated from the body), nevertheless *on account of the union with the body, as long as it is in the body, it neither can nor wills to execute them,* but *has all its actions common with the body, and in the body, through the body, and with it completes all things which it wills to do* — through the eyes, or by the corporeal eyes, it sees; by the ears of the body it hears; by the tongue speaks; by the hands touches; by the feet walks; by the remaining members feels; and for understanding uses the ministry of the head, the brain, and the senses — and yet through this communication (which in this conjunction of soul and body truly is *physical communication,* ***κατ᾿ οὐσίαν,*** *essential and natural,* as **Cyril** and **Bernard** speak) *neither is the soul mutated into the body, nor the body into the soul,* nor is there *any commixtion, equalisation, or confusion of the natures,* but *the soul retains its own nature and its properties,* and *the body preserves its own essence and its idiomata,* and *the actions of the man are attributed in one mode to the soul, and in another mode to the body,* since the *soul sees through the eye as through an organ, and by the eyes sees by the virtue of the soul* — **so the Son of God has the works of His Omnipotence (and chiefly the offices of the Messiah and the benefits of Salvation) truly and in reality personally common with His assumed human nature evected to the right of the Omnipotent God,** and exercises, administers, and effects them *not by the actions of one nature only* (divine or human), *but in each, with each, and through each, and according to each nature,* not *halved,* but *integral and whole everywhere, God and man,* the *difference of each nature nevertheless being preserved* — and this *not in a crass, carnal, or physical and natural, or any mode of this age which in this life we can attain* (for the similitudes fit ***οὐχ ἁπλῶς, ἀλλὰ τι τί,*** as said), but *in a supernatural and celestial mode, truly however and substantially.*

**Athanasius,** Dial. 5 *contra Apollinaristas:* *"As the soul in the body, so God the Word united to the humanity performs miracles, not separated from the assumed nature; but it pleased Him, through it, in it, and with it to exercise His own divine power."*¹³⁹

### Where the First Similitude Does *Not* Fit

We have said where the similitude fits. Now it must be shown *in which it does not fit.* It does not fit in these:

**First,** *soul and body are simultaneously creatures constituted by God in time.* But the *divine and human nature in Christ are not simultaneously either uncreated or created;* only the *human nature is a creature constituted at a certain time,* but the *divine nature is not created, but eternal,* the *Creator of all creatures.*

**Second,** *soul and body constitute human nature as consisting of these two parts,* of which *neither one without the other is so perfect* that *body alone is perfect man or soul alone is the whole human nature;* but the *soul is only a part of man,* and the *body is only a part of human nature.* But *in Christ no third nature is constituted from the union of divinity and humanity;* nor is Christ's Person constituted from two imperfect parts, but *stands of two whole, perfect, and integral natures — divine and human.*

**Third,** *the soul without the body and its corporeal members cannot produce and complete its actions as long as it is in the body.* But *the Son of God — being the eternal Omnipotent God — needed neither angelic nor human nature for completing the actions of His Omnipotence;* rather, before He assumed the human nature, *without it He completed all things which He willed.* But now, after He has assumed it and *in the personal union evected it to the highest sublimity above all the heavens,* even if He were now able even *without the ministry of the human nature to complete all the works of His Omnipotence,* yet — because *He united it and made it His own by hypostatic union* — He wills *nothing further to be done with men without it,* for whose sake He assumed it and promoted it in Himself to the government of the age, as it is written: ***"All things He subjected under His feet"*** (Eph.) — that is (as **Sedulius** rightly interprets) *under the dominion of His humanity, all things I say which are in heaven and on earth and under the earth: Angels, men, death, the devil, the birds of heaven, the beasts of the field, the fishes of the sea, and every name which is named not only in this but also in the future age* (Phil. 2; Ps. 8; 1 Cor. 15).

**"O inenarrable mercy, O immense wisdom, O dignity of human nature! O wretched us, who neglect these things, or as ungrateful and perfidious bastards extenuate them — brothers of Judas rather than of Christ!"** But of these things elsewhere.

**Fourth,** *soul has and makes its actions common with the body by physical and natural communication.* But *the divine and human nature in Christ, united by a more constrained bond and copulation far more august than the soul is united to the body* (as Paul exclaims, 1 Tim. 3: *"Great is the mystery of piety, God has been manifested in the flesh"*), *not by natural or physical communication, but by personal union and by a far more constrained and august communication than by any cogitations we can comprehend* — much less by physical or dialectical or in short human words utter — *they complete common actions,* the *difference of each nature being preserved.*

**Fifth,** *soul and body are finite natures, and their conjunction is finite and by a certain definition and circumscription;* and the *soul, even if it cogitates other things far away, is not outside its own body, as long as the man in this life is surviving.* But the *divine nature in Christ is immense and infinite, repletively everywhere by essence.* And the *human nature in Christ is finite and circumscribed* — yet so, that *in it is and dwells the whole plenitude of the divinity of the Logos, not in part* (since what is eternal and lacks beginning, middle, and end can by no means be divided into parts), but *wholly personally* — *not also as though the human nature were expanded and diffused into infinity, and repletively everywhere by essence, as the divine nature is repletively everywhere;* for on that reasoning *it would no longer be human nature,* but — the *truth of the body remaining* and the *properties of the divine and human natures being preserved* — *in that mode which is hidden to all creatures in this life,* to be reserved to the *intuitive notice and predication of the mystery exceeding the grasp of all men in this life.*

**In the Person of Christ** all these things happen, who *according to His divine nature is so in every place, that He is above and outside every place,* and *according to His human nature also circumscribed reigns through all local places in ages of ages, God and man,* and *is everywhere God and man,* the *difference of each nature remaining preserved,* and *preserves all His promises according to each nature,* and *is present, according to the promises, in the ministry and in the use of the sacraments,* — that is, in the *action,* as He said — *even if every human reason, indeed all the gates of hell, cry back, and oppose the rules of physics to the Omnipotence and truth of the Son of God Omnipotent* — as *dung to the best aromatic.* And so much of the first similitude let it be said.¹⁴⁰

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### The Second Similitude — Fire and Iron

**As fire by its substance fills iron,** and *communicates to the iron its own faculty,* and *is mixed to it everywhere without confusion of substances,* so that *no part of the iron is exempt from fire,* and yet *the fire in turn does not take up the properties of the iron* — **so the Logos, assuming the human nature, shines in it wholly, and the human nature, as if kindled by that light, is united to the Word,** and *is made partaker of the divine virtue and operation, not delivering its own imbecility to the divinity.*

This similitude is doubtless drawn from the *bush ignited* — which wholly burned, and yet *was not consumed,* but *retained its substance and nature* — and *in which bush the Son of God appeared to Moses* (Ex. 3). And *Apoc. 1:* the similitude of the *Son of man* is shown to John *in the species of brass, in a furnace ignited and glowing.*

And this similitude is *very frequent in the ecclesiastical writers.*

**Origen** bk. 2 ***περὶ ἀρχῶν*** says: *"A mass of iron always placed in the fire, in all its pores and veins receives fire, and is wholly made fire, so that neither does the fire cease from it, nor is it itself separated from the fire. Hence we say that the whole mass of iron has been made fire, because nothing else in it except fire is discerned. And if any one wishes to touch and handle the mass of iron, he will feel not the strength of iron, but of fire. So in the nature of Christ the human — the divine fire substantially shines, and the whole plenitude of the divinity dwells in it. And this truly is* ***περιχώρησις.***"¹⁴¹

**The same Basil,** *De nativitate Christi,* says: ***"How is the deity in the flesh? As fire in iron —*** ***οὐ μεταβατικῶς*** — not by passing over or by alteration and conversion, but ***μεταδοτικῶς,*** distributively or communicatively. For the fire does not run out to the iron, but remaining in its own terminus or place it distributes to it its own faculty. But how is God the Logos not filled with corporeal debility? We say: as neither does the fire take up the properties of iron. Iron is black and cold, but ignited it puts on the form of fire, and itself is illustrated and glows — not blackening the fire; and itself is inflamed, not blackening the flame. **So indeed the human flesh of the Lord too was made partaker of the deity — it did not deliver its own imbecility to the deity.**"

**Cyril,** *De Incarnatione Unigeniti* ch. 8, and bk. 1 *In Iohannem* ch. 15, and bk. 4 ch. 15, *repeats the same similitude* — luminously and copiously.

**Damascene** employs the same, bk. 3, ch. 17: *"Ignited iron burns, not because by physical account it possesses a burning energy, but because by the union to fire it has acquired that. So the flesh of the Lord — not according to its own* ***ἐνέργειαν,*** *but on account of the Logos united to it — is enriched with divine operations."*

Just as above, concerning the *first similitude,* it was said that it fits ***κατά τι*** (*in a certain respect*), so it must be repeated of *this second* similitude. **Heat in iron is an accident,** and inheres *separably,* and is *assumed by the iron.* But *two substantial natures* in the one ***ὑπόστασις*** *of Christ* are *inseparably* united, and the *human nature, once assumed by the Logos, is in eternity not deposited.* Iron *assumes* heat. **The human nature does not assume; it is assumed.**

### The Third Similitude — Sun and Light

**Justin:** *"Before I confessed that I could not by mind comprehend the manifest knowledge of this truth, and I do not now regret this modesty, content with the little measure given us. But let us compare the union with an ample Image, becoming a magnificent origin from so great a Father.* 

*Let the Logos be as that first light constituted on the first day. And cogitate the human nature to which the Logos is united to be as the solar light — nor cogitate the sun to be another light than she which was constituted before, but the sun is like a body in which that prior light — which was diffused before on every side — has been coupled together. This light now, in this body, makes for us the spaces of days and nights and of the seasons of the year. For if it were not tied to the body, but was wholly diffused in the whole air, it would not distinguish for us the times, nor would it have such regular motions.*

*As the light is so related to the solar body that the prior light and the sun are no longer divulsed, but the sun is called the one only light — so cogitate: after the Logos is united to the body, they are no longer divulsed; but as the sun is one, and yet the natures are two (one of the light, another of the solar body), so is Christ one, but the natures two — the one divine, the other ours; and the efficacy of the light is His own, which is discerned from the solar body not by act but by reason and cogitation. So also the properties of each nature remain to be discerned, although the natures cannot be divulsed."*

Not simply (***οὐχ ἁπλῶς***) does this similitude fit; and against it many things can be opposed. Nevertheless, of many things it admonishes us. For as the *sun was constituted after the first light,* so the *human nature was created in Christ, since the Logos was before from eternity.* Then, as the *light is in the solar body,* so the *Logos is in His own assumed body by personal union,* so that *no other thing is united to the Logos by hypostatic union.*

**Oecumenius** rehearses this similitude also on Col. 2:

> ***"ὥσπερ τὸ πρωτόγονον φῶς ἐν ἀρχῇ, ἀσώματον καὶ ἄϋλον ὑπάρχον, ἐνεκράθη τῷ ἡλιακῷ σώματι, καὶ γέγονε τρόπον τινὰ ἐνσωμάτωτον, ὑλικὸν καὶ ἐν αὐτῷ ὑπάρχον, οὐ περιέργεται οὔτε κωλύεται καταφωτίζοντα πάντα· οὕτως ὁ ἐν ἀρχῇ ἄσαρκος θεὸς Λόγος, σαρκωθείς, οὐ κωλύεται καὶ σαρκὶ ἡνῶσθαι καθ᾿ ὑπόστασιν, καὶ μὴ συγκεκλεῖσθαι ἐν αὐτῇ, ἀλλὰ τὰ πάντα πληροῖ"*** — namely (as **Justin** rightly says) ***κατὰ οὐσίαν πανταχοῦ, οὐ κατ᾿ ἐξαίρετον,*** just as He is in His own temple properly ***καθ᾿ ὑπόστασιν.***¹⁴²

Let these things suffice concerning the similitudes — of which is true what is said: ***ἐλλαττοῦται πᾶσα ἐικών, καὶ ἅπαν παράδειγμα, τῆς ἀληθείας*** — that is, ***every Image and every example is less and inferior to the truth.*** ***τὰ θεῖα οὐ διασημαίνεται διὰ τῶν αἰσθητῶν*** — as the Greeks rightly spoke. Let it therefore suffice that we have set forth the *right confession;* and, thanks having been given to God, let us henceforth live quietly through *silence and right faith.*
