## ANTITHESIS — The Heresies Against the Divinity of the Son and the Hypostatic Union

**Against the truth of the doctrine of the divinity of the Son, and of the hypostatic union of the natures, Satan has opposed himself in all times.** We shall rehearse a *few and chief heresies,* that the truth may more shine forth, and be dearer and more precious.

### *Simon Magus*

**This one,** while the Apostles were still living, from ethnic, Judaic (for he was a *Samaritan*) and Christian religion flowed together blasphemies and deliria — *as it were the Father of all heresies* — teaching that *there is only one Person in the divinity,* who on Mount Sinai delivered the Law, and appeared to the Jews under the name of Son, and *did not truly, but putatively suffer,* and *under the species of ignited tongues stirred up the Apostles by a new afflatus on the day of Pentecost.*

He is named **Magus** because *by magical prestiges he demented men.* And from him ***Simony*** takes its name — that is, ***gain,*** which is sought in those things which are the office and gifts of the Holy Spirit and the Church. For he willed to give money to Peter and John the Apostles, that he might receive the power and right of *conferring the Holy Spirit* (Acts 8). He named himself ***δύναμιν τοῦ Θεοῦ τὴν μεγάλην*** — the *Great Power of God* — and he saw a statue set up to himself at Rome with this inscription: ***"To Simon the Holy God."*** A harlot whom he led about with himself, named ***Selene,*** he said was the *Holy Spirit* and the *first Mother of all creatures.* At Rome, in the middle of the city, he is written to have finally fallen and perished. See ***Irenaeus*** bk. 1, ch. 10; ***Eusebius*** bk. 2, ch. 23; ***Epiphanius*** Tom. 2, bk. 1.

### *Menander*

**This one** was a disciple of Simon Magus, and his fellow-countryman, teaching that *he himself was the Saviour, and sent from the invisibles for the salvation of men.* He also exercised magic; and whoever learned it from him, he promised him *victory and power even against the Angels,* and *immortality in this life without the discommodity of old age.*

### *Cerinthus*

**He denied that Jesus was born of a Virgin,** but said that Joseph and Mary had a Son who excelled in wisdom and justice; but Christ was *another,* who descended into Jesus *in the species of a dove from above,* and *revealed the unknown Father,* and *perfected the virtues,* and *again flew away from Jesus at the time of the passion.* For *Jesus alone suffered, and rose again.* See ***Irenaeus*** bk. 1, ch. 25.

The Apostle **John,** having by chance encountered *Cerinthus* in the baths, said to his disciples: *"Let us go out, lest we be crushed."* And when John and the disciples had gone out, the building collapsed, and crushed Cerinthus and his associates. Hence the little verses:

> ***Impia Cerinthus sancto convicia Christo***
> ***Dum facit, et stulta garrulitate furit,***
> ***Concidit, et rapido blasphemum contulit ictu,***
> ***Collapsa, subitò facta ruina, domus.***

**"While impious Cerinthus made insults against holy Christ, and raged with foolish garrulity, — the house collapsed, and by a rapid stroke crushed the blasphemer, a sudden ruin having been made."**

The *Ecclesiastical History* (bk. 3, ch. 28) reports that Cerinthus also taught that Christ's kingdom after the Resurrection would be *earthly at Jerusalem,* and would last a thousand years. Whence long afterwards arose the ***Chiliasts.*** **Epiphanius** names Cerinthus ***ἀνόητον καὶ ἀνοήτων διδάσκαλον*** (*"a witless one and a teacher of the witless"*).

### *Ebion*

**He taught that Christ was only a man,** born of Joseph and Mary; or, if He was conceived of the Holy Spirit and born of a Virgin, *nevertheless He was not always God and the Logos and the Wisdom of God.*

***Ebion*** is *the same as* ***πτωχός,*** *poor,* say **Epiphanius** and **Eusebius.** ה , ן ו י ב א = ***πένης.*** *For truly he was poor in the acknowledgement of the glory of Christ.*

### *Basilides, Carpocrates, Saturninus*

These, in the year of Christ 137, augmented the doctrine of Menander *with marvellous stratagems and subtle sophistries* — among whom **Basilides** obtained the first parts. **Irenaeus** bk. 1, chs. 22, 23, 24. They taught that *Christ was a phantom (φάντασμα),* that is, that He *appeared putatively,* was *not a true man, nor assumed flesh;* but *as the First-born of the Father, Nous, He was sent to lead the Jews into liberty,* and *was not crucified — but in His place Simon of Cyrene was mutated into the form of Christ.* Basilides feigned *365 heavens,* per the diction ***ἀβρασάξ,*** which he set in the place of the beginning and of God. See **Epiphanius** Tom. 2, bk. 1.

### *Valentinus*

Out of the earlier heresies **Valentinus** gathered, about the year of Christ 141, *portentous dogmas,* and *feigned various generations of Gods, or Aeons.* He said that Christ *took nothing from the flesh and blood of Mary,* but passed through her *as through a pipe and channel,* His body having been *brought down with Him from above.*

### *Cerdo*

Valentinus's contemporary (***σύγχρονος***) **Cerdo** taught that there are *two Gods* — a *good one,* and an *evil one, the Creator of the world and the giver of the Law and the Prophecies;* and *that He who was preached by Moses and the Prophets as God is not the Father of the Lord Jesus Christ, but another, unknown and good;* and that *Christ appeared only putatively.*

### *Marcion*

**He succeeded Cerdo,** and on account of a rape excommunicated by his father, the bishop, fled to Rome, and embraced Cerdo's blasphemies and scattered them. To this one, asking of Polycarp, ***"Do you acknowledge me, Polycarp?"*** Polycarp answered: ***"I acknowledge you as the first-born of the devil."***

The follower of Marcion's deliria was ***Marcus,*** the author of the ***Colorbasian*** heresy, a magician — who by magical prestiges deceived miserable little women drawn into lust — just as the *Anabaptists of Münster, the Rothmann, the Leyden, the Knipperdolling,* and the like did.

### *Apelles*

**Also embracing the heresy of Marcion,** he said *Christ was the Son of the good God,* and *had a body — not putatively, but in truth — that is, true human flesh, but not from the Virgin Mary or from male seed, but from the four elements of the world;* which flesh, he said, *He rendered back to the world at His death,* and *rose again without that flesh,* and *returned to the heavens.*

### *Marcellina*

**She embraced the doctrine of Carpocrates,** and at Rome seduced many, a female teacher of the Gnostic heresy. *Christ,* she said, *was equal to the other men, but had a purer soul.* She *worshipped Jesus, Paul, Homer, and Pythagoras* as *more excellent than the rest of men.*

### *Montanus* (A.D. 174)

**Montanus the Phrygian** feigned that *Christ was putatively a man,* and that *he himself was the Paraclete promised by Christ.* With him were **Priscilla and Maximilla** as *prophetesses.* His followers are called ***Cataphrygians.***

### *Tatian the Encratite* (A.D. 175)

The disciple of Justin the Philosopher and Martyr — *after the death of his preceptor, he followed the Valentinian heresy.* He had a disciple, **Severus** — whence the ***Severian heretics,*** who *rejected the Old Testament altogether.*

### *Artemon* (A.D. 200); *Theodotus*

**Artemon** taught that *Christ was a pure man* — which at that time also **Theodotus the Currier** scattered, and on that account was excommunicated by Pope Victor. And from this **Paul of Samosata** drew his venom, the renovator of the blasphemies of Artemon.

### *Noëtus*

***Νοητός*** (or rather ***ἀνόητος***) taught that there is *only one Person of the divinity,* and dared even to teach that ***the Father suffered.*** Whence they are called ***Noëtians and Patripassians.*** Noëtus's words were these:

> ***ἕνα θεὸν δοξάζω, ἕνα ἐπίσταμαι, καὶ οὐκ ἄλλον πλὴν αὐτοῦ, γεννηθέντα, πεπονθότα, ἀποθανόντα*** — *"I glorify one God, I acknowledge one, and no other besides Him, born, suffered, died."*

Against this one, in the Asian churches was set forth this confession:

> ***ἕνα θεὸν δοξάζομεν, ὡς οἴδαμεν δικαίως δοξάζειν, καὶ ἕνα Χριστὸν ἔχομεν, τὸν υἱὸν τοῦ θεοῦ, παθόντα καθὼς ἔπαθεν, ἀποθανόντα καθὼς ἀπέθανεν, ἀναστάντα, ἀνελθόντα εἰς τὸν οὐρανὸν, ὄντα ἐν δεξιᾷ τοῦ πατρὸς, ἐρχόμενον κρῖναι ζῶντας καὶ νεκρούς· ταῦτα λέγομεν μεμαθηκότες ἀπὸ τῶν θείων γραφῶν, ὃ καὶ ἐπιστάμεθα*** —

*"We glorify one God, as we know it is right to glorify; and we have one Christ, the Son of God, having suffered as He suffered, having died as He died, having risen, having ascended into heaven, being at the right of the Father, coming to judge the living and the dead. These things we say, having learned from the divine writings, and which we know."*

See **Epiphanius** bk. 2, Tom. 1.

### *Sabellius* (A.D. 257)

**He denied that Christ is the Son of God and First-born,** and taught that *there is one Person of the Father, of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit,* and that *the Trinity is only in* ***ὀνόμασιν*** *(names), not in things or* ***ὑποστάσεσι*** *(distinct hypostases);* and *thus there are three nomenclatures in one hypostasis and substance* — just as the Jews and the like *feign that the three divine persons are not* ם י נ פ *(faces), but* ת ו מ ש *(names)* — that is, *bare appellations* — although *God has patefacted Himself far otherwise,* as *Christ's baptism most evidently shows.*

Against these whom we have rehearsed, most-faithfully fought both the Apostles themselves and their disciples and posterity. Peter withstood Simon Magus; John withstood Cerinthus, Ebion, and the rest; Ignatius likewise; also Polycarp, Justin, Irenaeus, Origen, Clement, Tertullian, and so on.

### *The Origenists*

**They taught that Christ's body, after the Resurrection, became altogether spiritual, invisible, and** ***ἀπερίγραπτον*** (uncircumscribed) **or infinite** — *and that our bodies also would be such.* **Jerome** refutes this and several other errors (Tom. 2, *ad Avitum* and *ad Pammachium*), showing that *Origen thought badly of the resurrection of the body,* since he *denied the resurrection of the flesh,* and *sensed that Christ also, more as a phantasm and putatively, and as an ἡδονόν,* conversed with the disciples after the Resurrection *than that He had true flesh in truth.*

### *Paul of Samosata* (A.D. 261)

**The ambitious bishop of Antioch** *feigned that* ***λόγος*** *in John does not signify a Person distinct from the Father, but only the deliberation of the eternal Father, and the counsel about the creation of the world and about the sending of Christ.* He renovated the blasphemies of Theodotus and Artemon.

Against him the Synod of Antioch was gathered, at which **Gregory of Pontus, Bishop of Neocaesarea** — called ***ὁ Θεόδωρος, θαυματουργός, δεύτερος Μωυσῆς,*** and ***ἱερομάρτυς*** — was present, whose confession against Samosatenus is this:

> *"One God, the Father of the living Word, of subsisting Wisdom, and of His entire Integral Image, the Whole Begetter, the Father of the Only-begotten Son. One Lord, alone from the alone, the Image of the Father, the efficacious Word, the Eternal Son of the Eternal: One Holy Spirit, having substance from God, who through the Son appeared, sanctifying, and through whom God over all and in all is known."*

### *Nepos* (A.D. 264)

**An Egyptian bishop,** who taught that *Christ would reign a thousand years on earth with the pious,* because it is written (Apoc. 20): *"The blessed shall reign a thousand years."* Nepos, however, renovated the heresy of Cerinthus rather than being the first to feign it. Whence they are called ***Nepotians*** and ***Chiliasts,*** to which number also *Polycarp, Papias, Lactantius, and certain others* are referred — extending Christ's earthly kingdom to a thousand years, perhaps *more by a mystery than by truth.* Such wander today everywhere, who feign that *Christ's kingdom has passed away,* and *imagine for themselves a new one,* as though *mentally captive.*

### *Manes the Persian* (A.D. 280)

**Manes** — by name and thing furious — drawing his fanatic opinions from the books of Scythianus and Terebinthus, barbarous and fanatic men, left to him by a Persian widow (whose Manes was both slave and heir) — feigned *two Gods,* the *good* — ***φῶς*** — and the *evil* — ***σκότος,*** the *Creator of the impious and of marriage.* He contended that *the Son did not truly assume the human nature, but only pretended it and putatively,* ***ἡ δόκησιν καὶ φαντασίαν.*** From this Manes are the ***Manichaeans,*** whose sect *for two hundred years widely wandered and propagated in Arabia, Egypt, and Africa* — in which *Augustine* also was engaged for nine years before he was converted. Manes was finally by order of the King of Persia (whose sick son he had killed with the medicines of pharmacies) *excoriated.*

### *Arius* (A.D. 328)

**Of Arius we have spoken above,** when we commemorated certain things concerning the causes of the Nicene Council. Arius renovated the errors of Samosatenus, and *denied the eternal divinity of the Son.* We shall briefly rehearse the chief arguments which he used.¹⁴³

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

## Twelve Arian Arguments and Their Rebuttals

### First Arian Argument

> **God neither suffers nor dies. Christ suffered and died. Therefore He is not by nature God.**

**To the major,** antiquity most rightly answered: *God does not suffer* — as concerns the divine nature, which is *impassible and immortal.* **To the minor:** *Christ suffered* — namely, *in the flesh* (as Peter, 1 Pet. 3, speaks), ***ἡσυχάζοντος τοῦ Λόγου*** (*"the Word resting,"* as **Irenaeus** speaks), *that God the Logos might be able to be crucified and to die in that nature which was able to suffer and to die.*

**Augustine** *in agone Christiano* ch. 10: *"Let us not hear those who on this account wish to compel us to number the Son of God among the creatures, because He suffered. It is one thing to be a creature, and another to take up a creature. The only-begotten Son, the virtue, wisdom, and Word of God, through whom all things were made, took up a human creature — which He deigned to raise up when fallen, and to renew when grown old; nor in her by the passion was He Himself changed for the worse, but rather changed her for the better by the resurrection."*

**Basil:** *"Him whom the universes do not contain, impassibly by the flesh He embraced death, that by His own passion He might give to us impassibility."*

**Athanasius** bk. 9 *ad Theophilum:* *"We do not fear that God according to the man was born, and the same according to the man God suffered, God died — but we glory to say God was born, suffered, and so on, according to the man."*

**Proclus of Constantinople** *ad Occidentales:* *"We confess that one of the increate and ineffable Trinity, God the Word, was made man in the flesh which for us from us He assumed — suffering not by the deity. One of the Trinity was crucified — in the flesh."*

**This whole response** afterwards *bore the doctrine of the Communicatio Idiomatum.* ***God was born, suffered, died*** — namely ***in concreto.*** But of this doctrine we shall speak a little below in the controversies of Nestorius and Eutyches.¹⁴⁴

### Second Arian Argument

> **The Father is greater than I. Therefore the Son is not by nature God.**

**The Athanasian Symbol answers:** *"Equal to the Father according to the divinity, less than the Father according to the humanity."*

**This response is given** according to ***ἰδιοποιίας,*** which today is called the *doctrine of the Communication of the Idioms.* It is also wont to be answered according to ***κοινοποίησιν*** — that is, from the doctrine of *the office of the Person of Christ.* ***"The Father is greater than I"*** — not by reason of essence, but by reason of my office: because the Father sends me; I am sent to the ministry of humiliation. Christ therefore is considered in this saying not as *giver of goods together with the Father and Holy Spirit,* but as *the Person sent* — and (as from Augustine we showed above on the Pauline saying to the Philippians) *simultaneously exinanited in the form of a servant.* And this response about the *Person sent* **Athanasius** himself rehearses in bk. 3 *ad Theophilum.* **Basil,** however, takes the same saying *of the Father as cause of the Son, and as generating Person* (bk. 4 *contra Eunomium*). And we have treated of this matter more prolixly in bk. 1 of the *Institution of the Christian Religion,* to which we send the studious.

### Third Arian Argument

> **The honourer is less than the honoured. The Son honours the Father. Therefore He is less than the Father.**

**We deny the major,** because *honour is a name of order,* and *does not take away equality of essence.* The essence is the same; the Persons differ by *relations, properties, and office.* **To the minor:** The Son honours the Father *both because He is Father,* and *because He is sent by the Father,* to whom He subjects *Himself and His Church, or His whole Kingdom.* But the Father also in turn *honours the Son:* John 8: *"The Father seeks my glory, and glorifies me."*

### Fourth Arian Argument

> **A created nature is not eternal. The Son is created (Prov. 8: "God created me"). Therefore He is not from eternity, but is** ***πλάσμα ἐκ μὴ ὄντων.***

**Answer to the minor.** In the Hebrew (Prov. 8) is ***Kanah*** (הנק), which word does *not signify* ***"He created me,"*** but ***"He possessed me,"*** and ***"He begot me."*** *For* ***Kanah*** *the Greek voice* ***γεννάω*** *and the Latin* ***gigno*** *or* ***genero*** *seem to have their origin.* Others answer according to the *doctrine of the Communication of Idioms:* *"the Son is created according to the human nature,* which in Christ is a creature," and (as **Athanasius** *ad Theophilum* bk. 11 most piously says) ***"simultaneously is something greater and more glorious than every creature, on account of the union."***

But this phrasis — *"Christ is created,* or *is a creature"* — was *rejected by the orthodox churches on account of the Arians.* For the Arians speaking thus willed that Christ was *not the eternal God, consubstantial (ὁμοούσιος) with the Father.* Wherefore, *even if the explication is added — "according to which nature" — nevertheless* ***ambushes must be avoided,*** which minds given to depraved opinions always construct by *speaking ambiguously and not openly,* as today too *we have experienced in the doctrine of the Supper.*

And it is worth here in passing to write down concerning ***Arius the fraudulent,*** *playing with ambiguities and deceiving the magistrate,* a history worthy of memory. **In feigned repentance after his damnation,** Arius took two books — one which contained his blasphemous opinion (which he had written with his own hand and secretly bore with him), the other written by another hand *on the sentence of the Nicene Council* — and thus approached the Emperor Constantine, and delivered to him this *other* book written by another hand *on the sentence of the Nicene synod,* swearing that *he thought so and not otherwise concerning religion, just as he had written with his own hand.* By this fraud he so deceived the Emperor that Constantine ordered Arius, without delay, to be received into the Church by Alexander, the highest Bishop — who by *his prayers poured out to God, brought Arius to destruction:* on the next day, as Arius was processing with court retinue and pomp to the Church, his belly seizing as he went, *he was casting forth his intestines, and by the stroke of an angel was made lifeless.*

**And not much less today are the artifices, and marvellous frauds, and the** ***Phormion-manners and gestures*** **of those who,** *disputing craftily about material heavens, about Christ's body enclosed in the heavens, about the Supper of the Lord, and about the presence of an absent body and the absence of a present body, and by similar tricks — deceive themselves and others.*

### Fifth Arian Argument

> **To do is to create. The Father made Christ (Heb. 3). Therefore the Logos is a creature.**

**To the major.** *"To do" does not always signify "to create,"* but *"to constitute and ordain"* — as *Theodosius made Arcadius king,* that is, *constituted, elected, ordained, created, and confirmed as king.* *"He who knew no sin, He made sin"* (1 Cor. 5) — here it is said not of the essence but of the office: God ordained Christ to be a victim for sin. And Heb. 3: *God made Christ high priest,* that is, *ordained.* For he speaks not of essence but of office. Likewise Acts 2: *"This Jesus whom you crucified, God made Lord and Christ,"* that is, *constituted and ordained,* ***κατέταξε καὶ κατέστησε, οὐκ οὐσίωσε.*** For he speaks of Christ the Man and of the exaltation of the human nature in Him, and simultaneously of the office of the whole Christ. As all sayings that speak of the *office of Christ according to each nature* *simultaneously illustrate — indeed presuppose — the exaltation of the human nature in Christ.*

### Sixth Arian Argument

> **Subjection shows inequality. The Son subjects Himself to the Father (1 Cor. 15). Therefore He is inferior to the Father.**

**Cyril responds to the major:** *"Subjection and obedience does not take away equality of essence or of power."* **To the minor,** we answer: *Paul speaks of office, not of essence.* Christ's *kingdom* is eternal, but the *mode differs in this life and after this life.* In this life the Church is gathered *through the ministry of the Gospel.* In eternal life there will be *no need of ministry,* but *God will immediately be all in all;* and then the *Son will hand over the kingdom to the Father* — that is, will lead the resuscitated Church into the sight of the Father — *that the divinity may reign immediately,* not through ministry: *and the Son shall subject Himself to the Father who subjected all to Him* — namely, *not by reason of essence but by reason of office,* because this *Person, sent to be King and Head of the Church,* will *be under the sending divinity,* which at length *will be Head immediately, and will be all in all, without any medium of ministry,* whose Head now is *the Person sent, or the Son.* ***ὑποταγήσεται*** — *He shall be subjected* — that is (as the Greeks best interpret): ***τότε δείκνυται ἀληθῶς καὶ θεοπρεπῶς ὑποτεταγμένος, ὅτι ἐπλήρωσε γὰρ πατρικὸν βούλημα, παραδιδοὺς τὴν βασιλείαν, πάσης στάσεως καὶ τυραννίδος ἠλευθερωμένην*** — *"Then He shall truly be subject, or show Himself subject, because now He has fulfilled the paternal will, leading the Kingdom or the Church liberated from every evil."*

### Seventh Arian Argument

> **Christ cries out on the cross: "My God, why hast Thou forsaken me?" Therefore He is not by nature God.**

**Answer according to the rule:** other sayings speak of *Christ reigning* — as *"What the Father does, the Son also does"* — others of the *humbled and suffering Christ.* And *one is the status of submission, another the status of glorification.* And *"the Word resting,"* Christ suffered and died. But these things do not hinder the equality of essence and power; but *show the obedience of that time,* and the *atrocity of our sins,* the *severity of divine justice,* and the *magnitude of God's wrath against the sins of the human race* — and are elsewhere more copiously shown. **Epiphanius** says: *"Christ cries out from the nature of the humanity, not from the nature of the divinity."*

### Eighth Arian Argument

> **God cannot be exalted. Christ is exalted. Therefore He is not by nature God.**

**We responded above in the explication of the Pauline saying to the Philippians.** And here we consider **Athanasius's** saying (bk. 8 *ad Theophilum*): *"God is not changed, but glorified in Himself the nature which He assumed. But the human, mortal, and infirm flesh and nature He advanced to divine glory — the Lord Himself saying of the assumed Son of Man: 'Now is the Son of Man glorified, and God is glorified in Him: God has glorified Him in Himself.'"*

### Ninth Arian Argument — the Omniscience, Omnipotence, Omnipresence Cluster

> **Omniscience, Omnipotence, Omnipresence or Ubiquity are properties fitting God alone. In Christ there is not omniscience (Mark 13: "Of that day and hour also the Son does not know"); nor omnipotence (Matt. 20: "It is not mine to give to you to sit at my right hand and at my left"); nor omnipresence (John 16: "I leave the world; I am no longer in the world"). Therefore in Christ there is not the divine nature.**

**Answer:** those sayings are to be received *of office,* not of the divine essence.

**Of that day the Son does not know** — namely, *insofar as He is sent to this office of teaching the Gospel in this life* — in which office it is required that the *future judgement be asseverated,* but *it is not required that the day and hour be predicted.*

**"It is not mine to give,"** and so on — namely, *in this my office, since I am sent to obedience in teaching and suffering.* Elsewhere He says: *"All things which are the Father's, are also the Son's; what the Father does, the Son also does."*

**"I leave the world"** — namely, *now that the office of my humiliation is expedited.* From the *reason of living of this age* — labourious and exposed to the senses — *I depart to the Father into the glorious and celestial reason of living.* *"I go to the Father,"* because *I have completed the work and office of redemption* — the *exinanition removed, the infirmities laid down, and all miseries removed* — *exalted with my flesh to the highest excellence, glory, and power of the Father.*

**"You do not always have me with you"** — namely, in the *physical mode of corporeal presence locally,* as before the Resurrection I was with you in all convivium and familiarity. *"I shall, however, be with you unto the consummation of the age,"* not only by the *universal presence of my divinity, and by inhabitation and spiritual efficacy in you,* but also *by my flesh and blood, or by the substance of my body, according to the truth of my promise and Testament in the Supper* — not, indeed, in *any gross mode of attingentia of this age,* but *in a supernatural and celestial mode,* since I *have passed out of the world to the Father into a supernatural and celestial mode of living, which eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor has it entered the heart of man* — that is, *by your reason, or by localities and physical rules, geometric dimensions, dialectical argumentations, and like infirm curiosities, it cannot be comprehended in your labourious world,* whose infirmities I have laid down and left. I am *with the Father,* and *have all things under my feet,* and *the whole world to me as God and man — my divine and human natures — is subject.* And whatever I say and promise, *I do* — even if in your world you do not yet understand *how it can be done, you who have not yet with your eyes seen my life and majesty of power.*¹⁴⁵

*(Further Arian arguments continue; the transition to the Communicatio Idiomatum proper begins after the Arian catalogue is complete.)*

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

## The Post-Arian Heresies — Through Eutyches to Modern Antitrinitarians

### *Donatus* (A.D. 331)

**The African bishop of Carthage** — whence the *Donatists* or *Donatians* — taught that the *Son is less than the Father,* and the *Holy Spirit less than the Son;* and *scattered many other things consonant with Arian blasphemy,* and *infected almost the whole of Africa and Judaea.* **Augustine** at length expelled this heresy from Africa.

### *Macedonius* (A.D. 345)

**By the Arians** violently exalted to the episcopal see of Constantinople — with over three thousand men slaughtered at his introduction into the church, the pious and sincere Bishop Paul having been driven out — he taught that *the Son indeed is equal, and similar in essence, to the Father,* but *the Holy Spirit has nothing common with the Father and the Son.* Whence they are called ***Macedonians*** and ***Pneumatomachians.***

Against these, by order of *Theodosius,* the Council of Constantinople was gathered, at which **Gregory of Nyssa, Amphilochius, Nazianzen, Cyril, Meletius,** and 150 other orthodox bishops were present — damning **Macedonius,** the *master of the plumary art* (*Ein Federmacher*), the *proud one,* and **Eunomius** and other similar men — and adding to the Nicene Symbol these words:

> ***"I believe in the Holy Spirit, the Lord and Life-giver, who proceeds from the Father and the Son, who together with the Father and the Son is adored and glorified, who spoke by the Prophets."***

### *Photinus* (A.D. 355)

**The Bishop of Sirmium in Illyricum,** renovating the opinions of Ebion, Sabellius, and Samosatenus, taught that *there is only one Person in the divinity,* and that *the Logos is not a Person distinct from the Father,* and *did not exist before the ages,* but *Christ began to be from Mary,* and *in Christ there are not two distinct natures.*

Against this one was convoked the *Council of Sirmium,* at which the Emperor Constantius himself was present. The Fathers (in whose number also was **Ossius, Bishop of Cordoba**), *for love of tranquillity and to make concord,* mutated the vocable ***ὁμοούσιος*** into the vocable ***ὅμοιος,*** pretexting this reason: that the *former word was not in Scripture* — though yet the *vocable Iehova is equally attributed to the Son as to the Father.* Of which temerity at length they repented — especially **Ossius** — when by this account the way was laid open for the Arians to *palliate their own blasphemies.* Nevertheless were also set forth decrees worthy of consideration against the Arians:

- **If anyone says** the Son of God is from Mary *by prescience,* and was not *before the ages begotten of the Father* and *with the Father,* through whom all things were made — *let him be anathema.*
- **If anyone** says the Son born of Mary is *a naked or mere man,* let him be anathema.
- **If anyone** understands that saying — *"I am God the first and the last, and beside me there is no God"* — which was said for the *destruction of idols* and of those who are not gods — to *take away the Only-Begotten God before the ages* in a *Judaizing manner,* let him be anathema.
- **If anyone,** hearing that saying — *"The Word was made flesh"* — thinks that the *Word has been changed into flesh,* or that the *Word suffered conversion in assuming the flesh,* let him be anathema.
- **If anyone** says that this — *"Let us make man"* — was said *not by the Father to the Son,* but *by God to Himself,* let him be anathema.
- **If anyone** takes this — *"The Lord rained from the Lord"* — *not of the Father and the Son,* but says *He rained from Himself,* let him be anathema. For the *Son the Lord rained from the Father the Lord.*
- **If anyone** says that *Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are only one Person,* or *three Gods,* let him be anathema.

### *Aetius* (A.D. 358)

**Deacon of Antioch,** sophistically, and with new tincture and peculiar species of erudition, from *Aristotle's Categories* renovated the dogmas of Arius, ***segregating himself from the consortium of the Arians*** — as though he by no means sensed with them — *that thus the rest he might more conveniently deceive* — just as today many *by a new sophism and pigment renovate the old deliria of Carlstadt, Zwingli, and the like,* and yet are unwilling to be called *Zwinglians or Sacramentarians,* and *permit many to be deceived by themselves.*

### *Eunomius*

**Aetius's disciple** and notary, *playing with the calliditas of words,* propugned the Arian heresy, contending that *the Son is dissimilar to the Father in all things,* because *by no means can a creature be similar to the Creator.* First *cunningly he dissembled his venom* until he had *deceived many;* at length coming forth, he was removed from office. Against him wrote **Basil** and **Nazianzen.** From him the *Eunomians* are called.

### *Eudoxius*

First Bishop of Germanicia in Cilicia, then Bishop of Antioch by force, later of Constantinople by force, he *denied the divinity of the Son,* and *widely scattered the dogmas of Arius,* and *sent Arian doctors everywhere to the Churches.*

**And this Arian age was similar in many modes to our age:** both as to *the calliditas and traps of words and phrases* by which the simple are deceived; and as to *the crafty actions, stratagems, lies, tinctures, lightness, and inconstancy;* and as to *the exiles of the orthodox cast out by the Arians.* And dissensions gradually were augmented, the Orientals *denying the consubstantiality of Christ with the Father,* the Occidentals *asserting the same,* which we name ***ὁμοουσίαν*** — *coessentiality.* And to such an extent grew the discords that, *all being despaired of,* the Orthodox concluded that *nothing further was to be done with the Arians about religion,* but that *they were to be held as obdurate, abject, and condemned* — which will also sometime need to be done in our Churches, provided that the *chair be not infected and the youth be rightly instituted.*

### *Acacius* (A.D. 364)

**Bishop of Caesarea,** learned and eloquent, said the Son is *similar,* ***ὅμοιος,*** *not* ***ὁμοούσιος,*** to the Father; the ***ὅμοιος*** he explicated *of will, not of essence.* Since on this account a Synod was at Seleucia, and *altercations arose in many words about* ***ὅμοιος,*** the legate of Emperor Constantius arose, and *said that he would no longer be present with those who endlessly quarrelled,* and *was unwilling to return into session.* But Acacius, a *prestige-artist,* used the word ***ἀσεβής*** for *not worshipping another* (which otherwise signifies *impious*), saying:

> *"The Father worships no one:* ***ἀσεβής.*** *The Son worships the Father:* ***εὐσεβής.*** *Therefore they are dissimilar."*

**A response was needed** to the profanity of the vocable, which was also *exploded by the whole people's clamour.* But as to the argument itself: *the Father certainly glorifies the Son and seeks the Son's glory. The Son honours the Father, because the Father is Father* — *this honour does not take away equality of essence.* And since the Son is *the Person sent,* He *subjects Himself to the Father,* as was said above on the Arian arguments.

### *Apollinaris* (A.D. 365)

**Rhetor and Lector of Laodicea,** often admonished by the orthodox bishop *to shun the consortium of Epiphanius the Ethnic Sophist,* when he *would not obey* was *deprived of communion.* In the desire of vindication, therefore, he excited a heresy — teaching that *the Trinity's* ***ὁμοουσία*** *is true and eternal,* but *the human nature in Christ lacks a rational soul, in whose place is the divinity of the Son itself.* Hence the ***Apollinarists,*** ***οἱ ψυχοφθόροι*** (*soul-corrupters*), against whom to refute at that time in the Athanasian Symbol these words are set: ***"Perfect God, perfect man, subsisting of a rational soul and human flesh."***

### *Nestorius* (A.D. 432)

**The Constantinopolitan bishop,** *by nature eloquent, self-taught,* arrogant, temerarious, young — *called* ***ἀνθρωπολάτρης*** (*the cultivator of a mere man*) — taught that *Mary is not* ***θεοτόκος*** *(bearer of God), that God was not born of a Virgin, that God was not crucified by the Jews.* Hence he was wont to say: *"Do not glory, O Jew, because you crucified not God but a man."* Likewise: *"God is not δίμηνος καὶ τρίμηνος"* (*"of two months' or three months' [gestation]"*).

Against him a *Synod was convoked by Theodosius the Younger at Ephesus,* over which **Cyril** presided, and *Nestorius was damned* — who in exile, when his tongue had been consumed by worms, is reported to have died *crushed by the earth,* many groans and lamentations being uttered before the earth ruptured and covered and pressed him down.

**What Nestorius's dogmas were** — that he wished Christ to be *only a man in whom there are divine gifts,* as in a ***θεοφόρῳ*** (*God-bearer*), *not truly God* — the decrees of the Ephesine Synod show. Nestorius's dogma in reality was this: *that the Logos was present to the man Christ by* ***assistance or society,*** as He was present to Elijah and to the other Prophets; and *the two natures in Christ were not united by hypostatic union.* And the wise judged that *he willed to lie in the seed of the Samosatene dogma* — for he was also a *grand-nephew of Samosatenus.* The chief decree thus speaks against Nestorius: ***"If anyone dividing the ὑποστάσεις in Christ after the union, and saying the Logos was joined to the man by society or power, not by natural union — let him be anathema."*** The ***natural union*** is what we name ***hypostatic.*** For before *Eutyches abused the vocable "nature,"* the orthodox safely used it, as we showed above.

**Nestorius therefore divided the hypostases,** *as though there were two of Christ, or two persons — God another, and man another Christ.* Then he *feigned only a society of the Logos with Christ the man.* Afterwards he was detected as plainly impious, *sensing that Christ was only a man.* Thus is it wont to happen — that *from grade to grade every heresy pedetentim and insidiously grows and ascends,* unwilling to burst forth at the first attempt, until *plainly detected* it can no longer conceal itself — as examples today everywhere testify.

Hence at the same time was handed down the doctrine — Scholastic indeed, but *founded in the word of God,* and most-necessary and most-useful *for judging controversies and retaining the true sentence* — ***on the Communication of the Idioms,*** when it was asked concerning these propositions speaking of Christ: ***"God is man born; God suffered; God was crucified; God died"*** — that it might be known *that this Person is Christ God,* and that *the Logos was not merely present to Him as to Elijah or to the other Prophets.* But of this form of speaking we shall say a little later, since a few of the heresies which remain must yet be added.¹⁴⁶

### *Eutyches* (A.D. 449)

**Archimandrite or Abbot in a monastery near Constantinople,** *rude,* knowing that Nestorius had been damned because he had *separated the natures* and *fabricated two persons, as it were,* fell into the *other extreme* — and affirmed *the divine had receded with the human nature into one and the same composite,* and *no longer are the natures distinct;* but that *just as there is one Person, so also one nature.*

Against him was convoked the ***Chalcedonian Synod.*** I name this one especially, since on account of Eutyches the *first Ephesine synod which had been instituted was plainly infamous and* ***λῃστρική*** (*brigandish*), completed *with words and bloody blows* for Eutyches. For thus do cruel heretics often *receive, greet, and attack the orthodox,* when they perceive them *to be destitute of the truth.* For *every desertion of the truth becomes ferity.*

The Emperor **Marcian** himself was present at the Chalcedonian Synod, and *Eutyches was damned for the confusion of the two natures,* plainly similar to *Schwenckfeld* in our age *confusing the natures in Christ,* and *denying that the human nature is a creature.* Before the union indeed Eutyches said the natures were *diverse,* but *after the unition and concretion he taught that there was one nature made.* Moreover he feigned that *Christ's body fell from heaven,* and that *there in the heavens the Word of God was clothed with it, and passed through the Virgin as through a channel* — and *renovated the deliria of the Manichaeans.* Likewise: *Christ's flesh was not of the same nature as ours; the deity suffered,* and so on.

**The summa of the Chalcedonian Synod** *Cedrenus* rehearses in these words: *"That sacred Synod pronounced our Lord and God Jesus Christ perfect God and perfect man, the same consisting of two natures, without any confusion or division; and that the two natures are* ***in no*** *reason of* ***διαιρέσεως*** *(separation or division), but of* ***διαφορᾶς*** *(discrimen)."*¹⁴⁷

### The Post-Chalcedonian Anti-Nestorian and Anti-Eutychian Literature

Against Nestorius, Eutyches, and the like, many decrees of the orthodox were set forth, which are *most worthy of assiduous reading.* And the *acts of the Chalcedonian Council are extant in the book of the Councils,* to which we send the pious reader — for it would be too prolix to rehearse everything. The *edict of the Emperor Justinian* is extant, and the *right confession of faith,* and the *condemnation of the heretics.* And the *anathematismi* written by **Cyril** are known. And the *labours of Basil, Nazianzen, Epiphanius,* and others are extant.

There is extant besides the book of **Anicius Manlius Severinus Boëthius,** patrician and Roman consul, *De duabus naturis, et una persona Christi, adversus Eutychen et Nestorium* — in which are explicated the *vocables of* ***οὐσίας, ὑποστάσεως,*** ***persona, subsistentia*** (which is ***οὐσίωσις***), and the like — and the *absurdities of Nestorius, Eutyches, and the Manichaeans are shown and confuted,* and the *Catholic faith concerning the one Person and two natures* is briefly expounded.

Extant is the work of ***Cassian the Hermit,*** completed in seven books *against Nestorius* and many other heretics — Ebionites, Sabellians, Arians, Eunomians, Macedonians, Photinians, Apollinarians, Pelagians, Marcionites, and similar — in which work he shows that *one and the same is Christ and the Word of God,* or *one Person of God the Word and of the human nature assumed from the Virgin,* and *no disjunction or division can be made between God the Logos and Christ the man,* because *the whole God is in Christ, and the whole Christ the man is in God.* Whence Cassian says: *"Whatever was of double nature has become of one virtue, and the name of humanity in Christ the divinity has wholly claimed to itself, and we do not separate the flesh from God, but we believe the whole — which Christ is — to be in God; for the nature of the flesh has been translated into spiritual substance, and that which was of man has become wholly of God,"* and so on. By these words Cassian does not teach *the equalising of the natures* or *the Eutychian and Schwenckfeldian confusion,* but *one and the same Person and one Christ,* and *simultaneously the glory and majesty communicated to the human nature in Christ from the conjunction of the Word* — the *human nature not abolished, but by its corporeal infirmity absumed.* For he always confesses *one Person and two natures,* and *magnificently predicates the glorification of the human nature in Christ,* not attributing improper things to it (for *what of Christ is not said as it is — even if honour be seen — is an injury,* as he himself says, bk. 1, ch. 1), but *retaining the testimonies of Scripture, the most-constrained personal union, and the evection of the humanity in the Logos to the highest sublimity above all the heavens at the right of the virtue and majesty of God Almighty.*

Extant is the ***Little Book of Faith of Maxentius*** *on the mystery of the Lord's Incarnation, against Nestorius and the Acephali* — of whom below. And Maxentius rehearses the summa of the Chalcedonian Council's decrees:

> *"If anyone does not confess two natures in Christ, and Christ as one of the Trinity even with His own flesh, and the Word by nature God made man, and two nativities in the one Son of God — the eternal and the other made in the last times, and Mary not so called for honour's sake only but properly and truly the Mother or Bearer of God, and Christ as having suffered in the flesh — namely, God — let him be anathema."*

And two dialogues of the same [Maxentius] are worthy of reading.

**Chiefly, however, celebrated are the Epistles of Leo, Bishop of Rome — especially the tenth, to Flavian** — diligently to be read, which the universal Synod of Chalcedon preached against Eutyches, so that *he who reads that one Epistle can think himself to have read the decrees of the whole Council which pertain to the Eutychian controversy.* First it fights against those *who constitute two persons or two Sons.* Then against those who *assert the divinity of the only-begotten Son passible.* Then against those *who take away the properties and introduce a confusion of the natures and deny the communion of the actions.* And **Leo** sets down these words which we retain as a rule:

> ***"Each nature acts, with the communion of the other, what is proper — namely, with the Word operating what is of the Word, and the flesh executing what is of the flesh."***

Fourthly he fights against those who *assert the assumed human nature has been changed and made a celestial substance,* so that *it is no longer human nature.* But *the denial of true flesh is the denial of the corporeal Passion.*

Extant is the book of **Gelasius, Bishop of Rome,** ***De duabus naturis in Christo, adversus Eutychen et Nestorium,*** where also the consensus is shown from the testimonies of the orthodox Fathers collected — just as **Leo** in Ep. 97 *ad Leonem Augustum contra blasphemias Nestorii et Eutychetis* adduces the testimonies of the Fathers on the one Person of the deity and the flesh. And they are worthy of diligent consideration — both the *sentences on the one* ***ὑπόστασις*** which Leo alleges, and *the ones on the two natures* which Gelasius rehearses.

**Among other things Gelasius confers the Sacrament of the Body and Blood of Christ with the mystery of the two natures in Christ.** *As the bread is and remains bread, its nature and property remaining, and yet is the image of the body — not of an absent one, since by the operation of the Holy Spirit it passes into the divine nature (namely, according to the words of the institution, "This is my body") — so one is Christ integral in two natures.* And these words *both against the Transubstantiators, and against new Nestorians* — or ***ἀσωμάτοι*** — *(those who deny the body or the corporeal presence of the body in the Supper)* — are to be observed. For Gelasius confers the *two natures in Christ distinct* with the *bread and body in the Supper,* and with the *wine and blood — sign and thing-signified — conjoined or united by* ***sacramental union, truly, substantially*** — so that *he who denies the substantial presence of the body and blood does the same in the Supper as if someone in the mystery of the Incarnation should deny the presence of the divinity of the Logos in the assumed nature.*

Extant are the *five books of* **Vigilius the Martyr, Bishop of Trent** (*stoned by the barbarian idolators at the age of forty*) — *against Eutyches and others sensing wrongly of the unity of the Person of Christ, of the two natures and their property* — which books we by no means disapprove, but on the contrary against the Eutychians we *make much of them and rightly preach them,* *even if others abuse them.*

**We are not ignorant** that many things from the Fathers, especially from Vigilius, are today *heaped up* — *that Christ has departed from us in His humanity, or in His body and flesh, and has left the world by corporeal presence, and is with us only in His divinity, not by His body and blood in the Supper,* and so on — which about the *local mode of the presence of the human nature* we altogether know to be true. For since the ancients, speaking of the Supper of the Lord, *never and nowhere thence weave consequences against the substantial presence, exhibition, and reception of the body and blood of Christ in the Supper,* but *distinctly and with great asseverations affirm that Christ in the use, or in the action, of the Supper is present not only by His divinity but also by the substance of His body, and is received by the mouth of those receiving,* and *is conjoined and united with us as if someone were to super-pour molten wax onto another* (as the ancients were wont to speak). And moreover they gravely reprehend those *sensing that Christ dwells in us only by Spirit, or by the divinity alone;* and *teach that Christ in the Supper so gives to us His body and blood* that *by corporeal union, and by natural participation* — that is, by the very *nature or substance of His body* — *He is conjoined and united to us who still are dwelling in this world on earth,* *not indeed by any gross and physical mode of attingentia of this age* (much less — as *incarnate devils and Cyclopes* with *bestial mouth* speak — by a *Cyclopic and Capernaitic mode of presence and manducation*), but by a ***supernatural, spiritual, and celestial mode, truly however, not by faith only, or habitude, efficacy, and divinity*** (since Christ did not say, *"Eat, this is my divinity,"* or *"the body by spirit and faith only present"*), *but in reality.* And thence they draw arguments in the gravest disputations — as that *Christ being true man has a true body, since He gives us His body;* likewise that *the resurrection of our bodies to life is certain and undoubted, because they are united with the body and blood of Christ,* and so on.

All these things and each, since the ancients affirm, teach, and write — therefore we understand their sentences on the *locality or corporeal location of Christ's body somewhere and in heaven* simply and rightly *not of an omnimodal absence,* and such (indeed) that *even if Christ Himself said and promised that by His body given for us and by His blood shed for our sins He would truly be present, nevertheless this could not be believed and could not be true* — but *of the mode of presence and absence — local, corporeal, carnal — which is according to the reason of this age.* Thus the Fathers themselves declared their sayings; thus certainly all of antiquity sensed and taught.

But that the words of Vigilius and others about the *local circumscription of the body* against establishing Christ's presence in the Supper are adduced — we simply respond that *they fought against the Eutychian confusers of the natures in Christ,* and *willed to show the differences of the natures and of the essential properties,* not *therefore denying* that also *those things are true and in reality happen* which Christ moreover said and promised of His own body — even if to our physical judgement they do not seem to cohere with the physical properties of the body. ***"God with His body does whatever He wills, and yet the body remains,"*** says **Augustine.** Let us grant, therefore, that *God can do something* which we confess *we cannot investigate.* By this our response, I do not doubt that the pious are content. And so we do not throw away the words of Vigilius against the Eutychians, but — when they are extended to the doctrine of the Supper (to which neither Vigilius nor other Fathers, when they thus spoke, had regard) — we rightly and candidly explicate them, and *say that they do not pertain to the Supper,* concerning which the manifest words of Christ Himself are extant.

The words of Vigilius are these:

> *"One Son of God, and the same made Son of man, has beginning by the nature of His flesh, and does not have beginning by the nature of His divinity; He is created by the nature of His flesh, and is not created by the nature of His divinity; He is circumscribed by place by the nature of His flesh, and is not contained by place by the nature of His divinity; He is even less than the Angels by the nature of His flesh, and is equal to the Father according to the nature of His divinity; He died by the nature of His flesh, and did not die by the nature of His divinity. This is the faith and Catholic confession which the Apostles handed down, the Martyrs strengthened, and the faithful now unto this day guard."*

**These words of Vigilius we do not disapprove, but we wish them to be rightly understood.** Vigilius speaks *against the Eutychians* (as was said), who *confuse the natures in Christ,* and *place only one nature.* Then he speaks of *Christ as made, created, circumscribed, diminished, dead according to the nature of His flesh* — that is, ***of the state in which Christ on earth visibly walked,*** and *completed the work of redemption in extreme exinanition,* per that: *"Thou hast diminished Him even below the Angels."* Therefore Vigilius says: *"Less than the Angels by the nature of His flesh"* — which words otherwise are by no means true *of the glorification and exaltation of the human nature in Christ,* which even the Angels adore. But if others insist that Vigilius speaks of the *nature of the flesh per se, not of the glorification of the flesh,* we insist in turn that *in the same mode Vigilius is to be received of the local circumscription of the body,* such as by itself is *the nature of the flesh* — *not of the prerogatives and highest exaltation of the body,* much less of the *power of God, who with His own body can do whatever He wills and promised.*

Let these sayings also be in view:

**Augustine:** *"It is not of our fragility to discuss the secrets of the heavens, but is of our faith to have sublime and honest sensations about the dignity of the Lord's body."*

**Philip Melanchthon:** *"No one shall persuade me that Augustine so binds the body of Christ to one place that he affirms it cannot be elsewhere — especially since Scripture nowhere affirms that Christ is so in one place that He cannot be elsewhere. For what other thing can be brought forth besides the judgement of human reason why we should enclose Christ in one place? But the judgement of reason must yield to Scripture."* The same: *"I would rather die than affirm that the body of Christ cannot be but in one place. Argue those who say this constantly, publicly and privately, when there shall be occasion."* The same, *to Oecolampadius:* *"That is an opinion unworthy of Christians, that Christ has so occupied a certain part of heaven that in it as though enclosed to a prison He sits."*

**Bucer's** in the *Retractationes:* *"Nothing can be firmly affirmed of it — whether the Lord is with His body circumscribed by some place of heaven, as Augustine and some other Fathers write. For all our matters must lean on the certain word of God."*

**I have rehearsed these things more copiously,** to show that *against the Eutychians Vigilius senses with us,* and *we with him — the same;* and Vigilius by no means contradicts our doctrine *on the corporeal presence and manducation of the body of Christ in the Supper according to the words of the institution.*¹⁴⁸

**Extant** are three dialogues of ***Theodoret*** — ***ἄτρεπτος, ἀσύγχυτος,*** and ***ἀπαθής*** — written against the Eutychians, *most worthy of reading concerning the one Person and the two distinct natures in Christ.* Some things must be read with judgement — as when he discourses of the *mode of seeing God in eternal life,* and when at times *improperly using the name Person* he says *God did not suffer but the man* (Dial. 3, fol. 55). For even if some judge that *he retained something of Nestorianism,* nevertheless he himself elsewhere sufficiently declares — understanding that *the deity did not suffer, but Christ suffered in the flesh, the Word resting.* For the saying of Irenaeus he himself alleges (Dial. 3, fol. 69). Certainly Theodoret writes most rightly, *"Two natures are in Christ born of a Virgin — the divine eternal and the human assumed — each retains its own properties, nor is there to be made a confusion of the natures."* But judgement must always be brought in reading.

**Extant** are the *three books of Fulgentius, Bishop of Ruspe* (A.D. 500), *to Thrasimund the Arian King of the Vandals in Africa,* in which he shows from the sacred Scriptures: (1) the *true and eternal divinity of Christ;* (2) the *true hypostatic union of the two natures in Christ;* (3) the *assumption of a rational soul also* in the mystery of the Mediator, and *the perfection of the man Christ.* And thus, *with names suppressed,* he *gravely and happily confutes* the Jews, Manichaeans, Arians, Apollinarians, Photinians, Nestorians, and Eutychians.

**From Fulgentius** (that I may add this in passing) — in the *Marburg Colloquy of 1529,* Zwingli objected to Luther the words on *local humanity,* that Christ's body is in a certain place, and is somewhere *contained locally.* Luther responded in these words: *"Fulgentius does not speak of the Supper, but so speaks against the Manichaeans and other heretics denying the humanity of Christ, proving that Christ had a true body, because He was in a place, which is proper to a body. But of the Supper Fulgentius says that the body and blood of Christ are offered, that is, are eaten and drunk in the Supper. And to Monimus he writes: 'The cup which we drink is the communication of the blood of Christ. And the bread which we break is the participation of the body of the Lord.'"*

**Extant** also is the *dialogue of Rusticus the Deacon* defending the decrees of the Chalcedonian Council against the Eutychians and Acephali, on the two natures in one Person of Christ, and showing that in Christ there is ***aliud et aliud, non alius et alius*** (see [[note-73]]). He also shows the *dissimilitude of the union of soul and body in a man, and of the divine and human nature in Christ* — of which dissimilitude above we have treated. This dialogue needs *accurate judgement* both for its phrases and for the *arguments of human reason adduced by the Acephali,* which Rusticus for the most part *also refutes by reason in turn.*

**Extant** is a little book — titled *Peregrinus* — written by **Vincentius of Lérins, the Gaul,** *against Arians, Nestorians, Apollinarians,* and other heretics, in which the *unity of the Person of Christ* and the *two natures in Christ* is also diligently treated.

But I shall cease from rehearsing the names of the many writers who withstood the Nestorians and Eutychians and similar heretics — and I shall add a few names of the heretics who *proceeded from the same school of Nestorius and Eutyches.*

### *The Quaternists*

**They taught a Quaternity in place of the Trinity,** on account of the human nature assumed by Christ — and thus feigned *two Christs,* as **Nestorius.** Rightly **Maxentius** says: *"If anyone does not confess Christ as one of the Trinity with His own flesh, let him be anathema."* And **Cyril** to Nestorius, Ep. 2, as **Rusticus the Deacon** also alleges these words: *"If we confess Christ as one Lord, we adore also the one and the same Word and Man, without the phantasy of incision, since His body is not foreign to the Word — with whom also He sits with the Father, not as two Sons sitting-together, but as the One about the union with His own flesh."*

There is indeed cited the sentence of Rusticus that *the human nature in Christ is not numbered with the Trinity* — whence afterwards certain insane persons of the kind, not without blasphemy, said the human nature of Christ is something *outside the deity, as other things are.* But he wishes nothing else, save that there be *no quaternity,* and that the *human nature of Christ is not coessential to the divinity,* as in the Trinity *all three Persons are coequal, coeternal, coessential.* The human nature is not numbered with the Trinity, since *it is not mutated into the divine essence.* But it is another thing to speak of Christ's Person, in which are two natures, *the whole Christ, who is one of the Trinity, God and man, His assumed nature not excluded* — as we shall say below on the glorification of the human nature in Christ.

### *Acephali; Synchitae*

**After the Chalcedonian Synod many impugned its acts,** called ***Acephali,*** because the author of that faction was uncertain — or (as Nicephorus commemorates) because they were *not under bishops,* and thus *lacked a certain head.* And the Emperor Zeno himself was in the *Acephalist heresy,* as also his successor Anastasius. And Peter Gnapheus, *cruel against the orthodox* (for such heresies *thirst for blood,* and *without public tumults never dote*), inflamed and confirmed Zeno.

They are called ***Synchitae*** from the *confusion of the natures,* since they taught the same with Eutyches — that *there is only one nature in Christ.* Whence they are also called ***Monophysites.***

### *Deuterius* (A.D. 510)

**An Arian bishop at Byzantium** baptizing Barbas said: *"I baptize thee in the name of the Father, through the Son in the Holy Spirit."* The bath at these words *dried up.* Barbas fled terrified, and was converted to the true faith. Thus **Nicephorus.**

### *Mahomet* (A.D. 623)

**He denied that in Christ there are two natures,** and taught that *Christ is a Legislator, a man and human wiser than others, not the Redeemer, not the Saviour.* The name *Mahomet* signifies *desirable.* For the devil *strove to transfer to Mahomet* that of the Prophet — ***"There shall come the desired,"*** **Mahamed,** *"of all nations"* — which is said of Christ — to *obscure the glory and benefits of Christ.* And *occasion for so great blasphemies was given by the horrid sects among Christians* — chiefly the Arian, Nestorian, and Eutychian — *from which the Mahometan or Turkish dogmas arose.* For from the *sink of all heresies the Alcoran is conflated.* Nor is it doubtful that *public and private confusions* will similarly arise from the theological contentions of our age. May *the Son of God clemently have mercy on us,* and *preserve the seed of the true Church* according to the promise: ***"I have left for myself a poor people on earth, invoking my name."***

### *The Monothelites* (A.D. 629)

**The Eutychians divided into various sects** — Agnoëtae, Tritheitae, Theopaschitae, Aphthartodocetae, and several others — when they had by various motions for 180 years miserably distracted the whole East, *came forth with a new face* and taught that in Christ *there is only one will and operation,* ***μίαν θέλησιν καὶ ἐνέργειαν.*** Into this heresy the Emperor Heraclius was *most insidiously dragged by Athanasius, Patriarch of the Jacobite Catholicos.* When the contentions grew, Heraclius set forth an edict (as in the courts often is wont to happen when thence *theological contentions are to be composed*) *that both parties be silent, and neither speak of one will and action, nor of two wills and actions.* In this mode the politicians thought there would be *an end to the contests,* and *did not so much withstand the Eutychians* (whom rather they confirmed) as *make the highest injury to the orthodox,* and *more inflamed the minds of the Eutychians* saying that *both wills had been lost by the orthodox.* Such counsels are today too often stirred up in courts — which are both *varied,* and *bend religion to political tranquillity,* which for the most part *they prefer to celestial truth.*

But the Monothelite heresy occupied almost the whole East, until the Saracens *utterly extirpated all faith in Christ from those places.* Against the Monothelites a synod at Byzantium was convoked by *Constantine the Bearded,* in which the Monothelites were damned, and it was shown that in one Lord Jesus Christ our God are *two natural wills and two natural faculties of acting.* And before this Constantinopolitan Synod, which was numbered sixth among the Ecumenical, *Pope Martin* also sent decrees against the Monothelites to the Eastern and Western Churches. And extant among other things is also this decree:

> *"If anyone does not confess properly and truly two wills and operations of the same and one Christ God, coherently united, divine and human, and thence in each of His natures the same voluntary and efficacious of our salvation existing according to nature, but says one nature, or one will, or one operation, let him be condemned."*

And **Zonaras** Tom. 3 p. 70 rehearses the decree of the sixth Ecumenical Synod: ***οἱ μίαν λέγοντες θέλησιν καὶ ἐνέργειαν ἐπὶ τῶν δύο φύσεων τοῦ Χριστοῦ, μίαν ἄντικρυς καὶ φύσιν κηρύττουσιν*** — *"Those who affirm one will and action in the two natures of Christ, plainly proclaim also one nature."*

**Peter Lombard,** Master of the Sentences, bk. 3 dist. 17, considers three grades in Christ's human will: (1) *the appetition of food and drink,* as on the cross He said *"I thirst";* (2) *the affections proper to the nerves in the laceration of the body, and the sense of grief and torment,* of which He preaches Himself in Ps. 22; (3) *the internal affections of the heart,* of which He says: *"My soul is sad even unto death,"* and *"Not as I will, but as Thou wilt."* But a fourth grade of will is *proper to the divine nature in Christ:* *"No one knows the Father except the Son, and to whom the Son wills to reveal."* This *willing is of the divine nature,* which with the Father *both chose the Church from eternity and is efficacious in her.*

### *The Monophysites* (A.D. 746)

**Most pertinaciously they denied the properties of the natures in Christ,** even if they said they confess Christ God and man. They sensed the same as the Eutychians, and at length said *one nature in Christ, not two.* These heresies and blasphemies followed by *public penalties, Turkish tyranny, and the Pontifical kingdom* — in which they most contended concerning *primacy,* and the *scholastic disputations prevailed which obscured the truth of doctrine,* and *drove Christ into Egypt, as it were into exile,* until at length in the *temple* — that is, *in the Scriptures* — the truth and Christ Himself would be found again *by the ministry of the great Luther, the Prophet and last Elijah.*

**Nevertheless the devil did not rest,** but in our times excited ***Servetus the Spaniard*** to attack the three Persons of the one divine essence, and to *deny the divinity of Christ,* and to *renovate the blasphemies of Samosatenus.* Servetus was *burned at Geneva in 1553,* and *plainly obdurate.* Following him were the Antitrinitarians, Tritheists, and similar *trumpets of the devil,* in *Russia, Poland, Transylvania, Hungary,* and elsewhere:

- **Georgius Blandrata,** the Italian, physician to the former Queen of Hungary;
- **Valentinus Gentilis** the Italian, punished at Verona;
- **Paulus Alciatus** the Italian;
- **Franciscus Lismaninus** the Corcyrean monk, former confessor to Queen Bona;
- **Gregory Paul,** former minister of the Church at Cracow;
- **Johannes Cazanovius** the Knight;
- **Peter Stator** the Gaul;
- **Franciscus Davidis** in Transylvania;
- **Peter Gonasius or Goniundzi** the Lithuanian;
- **Bernhardus Ochinus;**
- **Gribaldus;**
- **Lelius Socinus;**
- **Simanus;**
- **Menno;**

and several others — of whom some are *openly Arians* (and so far as *to go into the Mahometan camp,* as **Sylvanus** with the Counts of the Palatinate whom he took with himself was intent on the same), others *feign three divine essences and are Tritheists;* others *retain the Samosatene dogmas and teach that Christ began in the womb of Mary, and yet is Son of God because conceived by the Holy Spirit and received all power because He is Saviour of the human race, and is to be invoked because that divine glory was gifted to Him from God — but not the eternal God, but made God at a certain time.*

**The Anabaptists** similarly *doted in Frisia, Westphalia,* and elsewhere concerning Christ. Likewise **Schwenckfeld, David Georgius.** And *everywhere in Germany also many fanatics wander* — partly Nestorians, partly Eutychians. Arians also *betray themselves in many places.* And since *security is extreme, and the petulance of minds great,* and the *period is almost fully turned around,* the *penalties are to be feared which we see are at the doors.*

**We pray, however, our Lord Jesus Christ the Son of God,** that — *since everywhere it draws toward evening* — *He may be and remain with us,* and *leave us a seed,* lest we be *equal to Sodom and Gomorrah;* and *may He conjoin the minds of the pious rightly-sensing and rightly-invoking God,* lest by mutual wounds we *destroy ourselves,* but that we may *be and remain one in Him.*¹⁴⁹

<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
