# *Doctrina de Communicatione Idiomatum*

## The Foundation

**Most firmly must it be held** that ***in Christ Incarnate there are and remain two perfect and integral natures*** — the *eternal or divine,* and the *human assumed* — both as to essence and as to the essential idiomata of each nature. And these two natures do not subsist ***ἰδικῶς καὶ ἀνὰ μέρος*** *(separately and by part)* so that *as many Persons as there are natures* — but are *united,* **not by confusion, or by conversion, or by alteration, or by equalisation, but by hypostatic union** — by which *from these two natures, whole, integral, and perfect,* is constituted ***one and singular*** ***ὑφιστάμενον*** — the *difference of the natures by no means being taken away on account of the union,* but rather *the property of each nature being preserved, and coming together into one Person* — as the *Chalcedonian Synod* spoke, ***ἑκάστης φύσεως εἰς μίαν ὑπόστασιν συντρεχούσης.***¹⁵⁰

Then it must be diligently seen *what things follow from the difference of the two natures, and from the discrimen of the essential idiomata or natural properties, and from the hypostatic union.*

**It is established, and certain,** that the ***essential idiomata of one nature are not attributed to another nature in abstracto considered,*** but *are communicated to the Person in concreto.* For the *essential idiom of one nature cannot be made or be the essential or natural idiom of the other nature,* or *common to both,* so that *separately, distinctly, and formally it inheres in each nature according to itself.* For by this reason *the difference of the essential properties would be taken away,* and *the confusion of the natures and of the idiomata would arise.*

**It is established, moreover, and certain,** that ***the propositions, enunciations, or predications about those things which follow from the hypostatic union of the two natures, are not all of one and the same kind,*** but *there is a great difference among them,* and *they must necessarily be distinguished.*

### The Ancient Vocables — Irenaeus, Damascene, Theodoret

The doctrine of the *Communication of the Idioms* was handed down by the ancients. **Irenaeus** called it ***ἰδοικείωσιν;*** others ***τρόπον τῆς ἀναδόσεως καὶ περιχωρήσεως*** ("mode of exchange and interpenetration"). Later ones most-conveniently would call it ***κοινωνία τῶν ἰδιωμάτων*** — which we usually define as ***the predication in which the property of one nature is attributed to the other nature in the Person,*** the rule being preserved, which is: ***"The deity is not affected by the injury of its own body, just as the flesh is glorified by the majesty of the deity."***¹⁵¹

**The scholastics,** uncertain of the reality itself, define the *Communication of the Idioms* only *of the form of speaking* — in which the *property of one nature is said of the whole Person of Christ in concreto,* and it is signified that *in Christ are two natures,* not only so that one is *society and separable* (as in Elijah and other saints God is present *by society, as helper, separably*), but so that *the Logos assumed the human nature by a marvellous union, inseparable and personal.*

**Even though from the ancients it can be shown** that the vocable ***κοινωνίας*** was accommodated *to many propositions* — not only to those which the definition of the scholastics just rehearsed embraces, but also *to personal offices or* ***ἀποτελέσματα,*** and to the *exaltation of the human nature in Christ* (as above in the definition of Person we showed concerning ***ἰδιοποιία*** and ***κοινοποιία*** [[note-66]] and [[note-67]]) — nevertheless, *lest a* ***λογομαχία*** *be raised and dissensions augmented,* we understand ***Communicatio Idiomatum*** at present properly *of those propositions in which the properties of one nature are attributed to the Person,* per the rule of **Theodoret:** ***κοινὰ τοῦ προσώπου γέγονε τὰ τῶν φύσεων ἴδια*** — ***"the properties of the natures have become common to the Person."*** Scholastically this is called the ***Communicatio Idiomatum.***

### Abstract vs Concrete

***Abstracta*** signify *the natures themselves and their idiomata, considered per se.* ***Concreta*** signify ***the very*** ***ὑφιστάμενον,*** *in which those natures and their idiomata subsist and are sustained* — which ***ὑφιστάμενον*** receives its *denomination* either from *one of the natures* or from *both* — as: ***God, Man, Christ, Emmanuel,*** ***θεάνθρωπος.***

Now let it be piously and diligently inquired *which propositions properly pertain to the Communication of the Idioms* — which **Cyril** understands by the vocable ***τῆς ἰδιοποιίας*** — *which is attributed to the Person* — so that we may not give occasion to errors and greater dissensions by *confusion of propositions,* *as though all these were of the same kind,* when they must *necessarily be distinguished.*

### By No Means — Abstract Predications

**By no means** is it said *in abstracto:* ***"The divine nature or the divinity is human,"*** or *conversely.* Nor is it said of the *idiomata:* ***"The invisible and impassible in the Logos has been made visible and passible through the union."*** Nor should there be *confusion of abstracts and concretes.* For it is not said: ***"The Logos is the humanity;"*** ***"The humanity is the Logos, God, Creator;"*** or ***"The man is the divinity;"*** ***"The flesh is God"*** (though *ecclesiastical writers have sometimes so spoken improperly*), and so on.

### But in Concreto — Rightly

But ***in concreto*** it is rightly said: ***"The man Christ is eternal God, is Creator of all things, is Omnipotent with the Father and the Holy Spirit."*** ***"God is man; God was made man, born of the Virgin; God suffered; the Son of God died."*** These and similar things ***befit the Person according to one only nature, not according to both.*** Therefore the ancients in the explication of such propositions used ***distinctive particles,*** as:

- **John 3:** ***"The Son of Man descended from heaven"*** — namely, *according to the divine nature which in Christ is incarnate,* not *according to the matter of the body,* as the Manichaeans willed. **Cyril** says: *"He descended — namely, through the dispensative unity."*
- **1 Cor. 2:** ***"The Lord of glory was crucified"*** — namely, *in His own human nature,* as **Peter** says (1 Pet. 3 and 4): ***"mortified in the flesh,"*** ***"suffered in the flesh."*** And **Paul** himself adds the distinctive particle, ***"according to the flesh"*** (Rom. 1): ***"The Son of God was born of the seed of David according to the flesh, and was declared Son of God according to the Spirit."*** And: ***"From the Jews is Christ according to the flesh, who is God blessed forever."***¹⁵²

Let us now take examples in order from the *Second Article of the Apostolic Symbol.*

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

## The Communicatio Applied to the Clauses of Article II

### *Qui conceptus est de Spiritu Sancto* — *"Who was Conceived by the Holy Spirit"*

*"God took up the human nature"* is *rightly said.* *"God took up a man"* is *improperly said* (see Maxentius's *Dialogue*).

Therefore it is rightly said: ***"God, the Son of God, the Logos, was conceived by the Holy Spirit"*** — at a certain time, namely *as regards the Incarnation, or the assumption of the human nature,* which assumption was made *by the virtue and efficacy of the Holy Spirit* — not as of the Father [begetting], but as of the *Effector,* who *not from His own substance, but from the Virgin Mary's substance, made and constituted the body of Christ.*

### *Natus ex Maria Virgine* — *"Born of the Virgin Mary"*

Not according to the *divine* nature, but according to the *human* — and yet not in the *regular mode of nature,* but (as the Church sings) ***beyond the series of things, by a mystical breath, without male seed.*** Whence excellently **Ambrose** says (bk. 9 *In eos qui initiantur mysteriis*): ***"What do you seek the order of nature in the body of Christ, since beyond nature is His very birth from the Virgin?"***

### *Passus, Crucifixus, Mortuus, Sepultus* — *"Suffered, Crucified, Died, Buried"*

**1 Pet. 4:** ***"Suffered in the flesh."*** **1 Cor. 13:** ***"Crucified out of infirmity."*** **1 Pet. 3:** ***"Mortified in the flesh."*** For the *divinity did not suffer, was not crucified, did not die.* But *God the Logos, who was made flesh, also suffered, was crucified, and died,* as Paul says: ***"They crucified the Lord of glory."*** And He suffered *according to the assumed nature,* or (as **Jerome** wrote) *according to that which was able to suffer.* For since it was not possible for the very Word to die — being immortal, the Son of the Father — He *assumed to Himself a body which could die,* as **Athanasius** says (*De Incarnatione Verbi*). Hence there is extant the decree of **Damasus:** ***"If anyone says that in the passion of the cross the Son of God bore the grief in the divinity, and not in the flesh and rational soul which He assumed in the form of a servant, let him be anathema."***

**And this too must be known: there is a huge discrimen between these propositions:**

- ***"John the Baptist suffering, God suffers"*** (or *"Paul, or other saints, suffering, God suffers"*)
- and ***"Christ suffering, God suffers."***

The Baptist suffers, as a man, many griefs of soul and torments of body. And true is that saying (Zech. 2): ***"He who touches you touches the apple of my eye."*** For *relation,* or the *contempt and contumely inflicted on the Baptist as on the slave and legate of God,* pertains to *the Lord sending* — even if the Baptist takes the wound in his own body, and not God. Therefore this proposition, ***"God suffers, the Baptist suffering,"*** is ***figurate,*** and is *true by metaphor.*

But when we say ***"Christ suffering, God suffers,"*** *there is subject no figure or metaphor* — because ***this Person, who is by nature God, suffers and dies*** — and because *God in His own body which He assumed and united to Himself, or in His own human nature in which the whole plenitude of the divinity dwells personally.* Therefore Paul says (1 Cor. 2): ***"They crucified the Lord of glory."*** Likewise: ***"God has acquired the Church for Himself by His own blood."*** And 1 Pet. 1: ***"We are not redeemed by corruptible things..."***

**And even though we do not have God passible in His own nature,** and we do not say ***God suffered*** in the sense that ***He received the transfixions of the nails in His own nature,*** nevertheless **that body which was made the proper body of the Only-Begotten of God bore the sufferings** — and (as **Athanasius** *ad Epictetum* speaks) ***the Word God is in a certain manner said to have sustained the nails,*** because *He was in it at the time when it suffered,* and *vindicated to Himself as His own the things which were proper to the body,* which — being *the temple of the Word* — was *full of the deity.* Certainly *without the passion the impassible God did not perfect our salvation.* And ***He too Himself suffered,*** because *when He could have prevented it, He did not prevent;* and *did not exert His own power, nor did He do what He could have done in that passion of His own proper body, but rested in His humanity.*¹⁵³

### *Descendit ad Inferos* — *"Descended into Hell"*

**1 Pet. 3:** ***"In the Spirit going, He preached to the spirits in prison."*** Antiquity so spoke: ***ἡ θεότης σὺν τῇ ψυχῇ ἐπορεύθη ἐπὶ τὰ καταχθόνια*** (*"the deity with the soul went to the underworld"*). And **Augustine** says: ***"The soul of Christ and the deity of Christ were in hell, where Christ showed to the devils His presence and power, and conquered the devil and the kingdom of devils, and liberated us from the power of the infernal, and led out and reported the triumph from hell."***

**It is madness** to say that ***"He was buried,"*** and ***"He descended to hell,"*** signify the same thing in this passage. Here rather the devil *in reality experienced that of Hos. 13:* ***"I shall be thy pestilence, O hell."*** And this part was of the *Triumph and Victory of Christ.*

### *Tertia die resurrexit à mortuis* — *"On the Third Day He Rose from the Dead"*

We therefore say ***in concreto:*** ***God has been raised up and lifted from the dead*** — namely (as in Acts 2 it is written), *according to the flesh,* or *according to the human nature,* or rather *by His own body.* ***"The man rose again, since a man died — and a man was raised, but resuscitating was God,"*** says **Ambrose.**

He rose *without corruption of the human mass,* and *through the sepulchre, not with the seal or stone moved,* as *pious antiquity has always spoken.* For **He rose with a body subtle and light — surpassing and penetrating all things — and yet a true and human body.**

### *Ascendit ad Caelos* — *"He Ascended into Heaven"*

**God has been lifted up into heaven** ***according to the human nature*** — the *form circumscribed, and the location of the body,* by *visible motion,* so that by a *visible interval He gradually departed farther from the sight of the Apostles.* In this *visible, manifest, corporeal, and sensible form* He no longer converses with us on earth, but *in heaven so appears to the Angels and to the blessed* (Apoc. 14; Acts 1), and *in that shall again come from heaven with glory to judge* (1 Thess. 4).

**But He ascended into the heavens:**

**1.** *Not by natural motion* (which is that a heavy body is carried downwards), but by a *visible* motion — and *marvellous and proceeded from divine power.*

**2.** *Nor did He ascend that He might be captured, contained, or comprehended by a material and physical heaven, or by any created place wheresoever it be,* so that *He could not be elsewhere where He wills to be* — even if *heaven itself received Him with joy in the ascension,* and *yielded to Him ascending and penetrating all clouds and heavens by His own body* (Heb. 5).

**3.** *Nor did He ascend so that henceforth by His body He would plainly be separated from us,* and *would not be able to be with us any longer,* even though *He promised* that ***by His body and blood He wills to be present with us in the Supper on earth where the Supper is administered according to the words of the institution.***

But (as we speak with the Scripture's words) He *ascended above all the heavens* (Eph. 4), *where no physical place can be cogitated;* He *penetrated the heavens* (Heb. 5), *against the physical rule of the impossibility of the penetration of dimensions;* He is *made higher than the heavens* (Heb. 7); He *ascended to the Father* (John 20); He has *entered into the sanctuary, into heaven itself, before the face of God* (Heb. 9).¹⁵⁴

### The Cluster of Questions Here — The Sea of Questions

**Here confluences a sea of questions,** of which the chief are these:

- Is Christ ***σῶμα περίγραπτον***? — *in one certain place of heaven?*
- Likewise on the sayings: ***"I leave the world;" "You do not always have me."***
- Is Christ *everywhere* even by His *humanity?*
- Is Christ *in the Supper* not only by His divinity and Spirit, but also *by the substance of His body and blood?*

### Question — Christ is *σῶμα περίγραπτον*

***By the communication of the idioms it is said:*** ***"God the Logos is circumscribed"*** — namely, *according to the nature of His flesh,* since He has a true human body. And ***"the man Christ is ἀπερίγραπτος"*** — *according to His immense divinity.* But ***περίγραπτος*** we understand *of the true substance and property of the human body,* not only *of the physical circumstances* such as are in the reason of this present age.

### Question — Christ by His Body Is in a Certain Place of Heaven, by Corporeal Location Somewhere

**Physically, according to the mode of a true body, Christ by His own body is** ***περίγραπτος,*** and *when He wills, in heaven He so appears to the Angels and to all the blessed.* But let us **beware and flee two things:**

**1.** Let us not *feign a physical place of heaven where Christ is by His own body* — otherwise *we shall have Christ nowhere,* since *physically speaking* we *cannot define or show a certain place or a certain "where,"* — and *what to us seems upward, to the Antipodes seems downward,* and *conversely.* Wherefore **Augustine** rightly says: ***"Neither where nor how Christ's Lord's body is in heaven is anxiously to be inquired"*** — *especially since Scripture says that Christ ascended even above all the heavens,* and *was made higher than all heavens and places.*

**2.** Let us *not repugn the truth of Scripture,* which affirms *two things.*

**The prior** shows *whither Christ by His body has arrived, and where He is in the heavens* — namely, ***at the right of God Almighty, in the glory of the Father*** — not only *in a place, physically speaking, somewhere in heaven, where and when and how He wills to be,* but also ***above the places of places, and outside every place.***

**The posterior** shows that ***Christ by His body can also be — in another than physical and natural, circumscribed and visible mode — wheresoever He wills to be, without injury and corruption of His true and human body*** — namely, in a mode *known only to divine power and will,* according to the *manifest promise and institution* — *as in the Supper* — even if that mode *does not seem to cohere with corporeal location and the physical properties of the body,* to human judgement. For ***one little word of Christ exceeds all physical rules and all the laws of nature*** — and *he who does not believe this cannot be a Christian.* We have treated this matter above in the mention of Vigilius [[note-148]].

### The Acts 3 Question — *"Christum oportet caelum accipere"*

Here is asked the place of Acts 3: ***εἰσοδὸν οὐρανὸν δέξασθαι*** — whether ***Christ ought to accept heaven, or heaven ought to accept Christ*** — thus indeed, that *Christ by His own body would neither will nor be able to be elsewhere until the last day than somewhere in heaven,* and *therefore also would not be in the Supper,* even if the *dispositive words of His own Testament clearly affirm* that ***He is present by His body and blood.***

For into this sense the words of Peter are cited thus: ***"Christ must be received, contained, comprehended by heaven."***

**Although with those who used this version — with that sentence by which they say Christ has a true body, and was received from heaven at the Ascension — we have no strife** (lest we seem to establish a ***λογομαχία***) — nevertheless **suspicion can never be lacking** from *the cunning speaking ambiguously,* when we can *speak properly, perspicuously, and without any ambiguity.* Let it be shown what is the *true sentence of Peter's words.* Then, *how it must be understood that Christ was received by the heavens.* Then, that *this inverted version is ambiguous, new, and cunningly used for the absence of Christ's body in the Supper.*

**The sentence of Peter's words** — and the *context of Peter's sermon,* and the *article on Christ's Ascension into heaven* which the sacred letters explain, and the *collation of similar sentences* — lay it open without ambiguity.

***Christ*** (says Peter) ***suffered out of infirmity according to the Prophecies.*** ***But now He has received heaven as glorious Triumphator and Victor, and Author of life, and Lord of heaven.*** He has received *heaven,* that is, *the possession of heaven, the dominion, and the glory,* being *evected through the exaltation, unto the time of judgement,* when *He shall no longer dominate through means in the midst of enemies,* but *will manifestly reveal His glory even on earth, and will be God all in all.*

But He has *not so received heaven that He might be captured by material and physical heaven,* and *nowhere else, even if He willed, by His body could be, than in a certain place of heaven — which nevertheless will perish.* This is *plainly an idle phantasy, most alien to Peter's sentence.* ***Christ has received heaven, and is in heaven,*** just as ***God is in heaven,*** and is *also by His body no longer in time, but in eternity itself, in which God is.* Therefore, says Peter, ***undertake penitence and be converted to Christ,*** who is *such a Lord, Ruler, and Possessor of heaven,* and *has all under Him and subject to Him.*

**The article of Ascension** is explicated by Scripture itself — which treats *not only of physical localities and omnimodal separation,* but *describes the highest exaltation also of the human nature in Christ, evected above all the heavens to the right of God,* as Christ Himself says: ***"I ascend to my Father."***

**Equivalent sentences are:** *Christ ought to receive heaven — that is, the possession and dominion of heaven — until the times of restitution.* Luke 24: ***"Christ ought to suffer, and thus enter into His glory."*** 1 Cor. 15: ***"He ought to reign, until He puts all enemies under His feet."*** Luke 19: ***"He went away to receive for Himself a kingdom, and to return."*** 1 Pet. 3: ***"He went into heaven, is at the right of God, with Angels, powers, and virtues subjected to Him."*** Apoc. 5: ***"The slain Lamb received virtue, riches, wisdom, fortitude, glory, benediction."*** Dan. 7: ***"The Son of Man came unto the Ancient of days"*** (per that: *"I ascend to my Father"*), ***"and He gave Him power, honour, and kingdom. His power (which He received given to Himself as Son of Man) — an eternal power which shall not be taken away, and His kingdom, which shall not be destroyed."*** Matt. 26: ***"All power is given to me in heaven and on earth."*** And: ***"He received the name which is above every name"*** — namely (to use the words of **Calvin** and **Beza**) ***"according to the flesh, or according to the human nature with respect to the flesh."*** For he speaks of *power received in time,* not of the *divine nature,* which has *that eternal power from eternity to eternity.*

**That the cloud received Christ from the eyes of the spectators, and Christ was raised into heaven by His body and received by heaven** — this is to be understood *of the history of the Ascension,* which was done and was visible. **But not on that account is it to be collected:** *"Christ was received into heaven, therefore He is so in some physical place of heaven with His body — even if He said and promised, that is, even if He wills — He cannot be elsewhere."* For **this consequence is what the** ***Utopists*** **weave.**

**It is also certain that it is not equitable that the effort of those be praised who by inverting and reverting, bending and reflecting the sayings of Scripture — chiefly those which in Scripture itself have their own manifest declaration and immediately pertain to the articles of faith — construct** ***ambiguities,*** as though from *the Sibyl's leaves and the oracles of Apollo* — such as: ***"Aio te, Aeacida, Romanos vincere posse"*** (*"I say that you, Aeacides, can conquer the Romans"*).

***εἰσοδὸν οὐρανὸν δέξασθαι.*** Here indeed those ***οἱ ἐπαμφοτερίζοντες*** (*"those who look both ways"*) ask: *Has Christ received heaven — or has heaven received Christ?* **Egregious indeed and outstanding is this wisdom, and worthy of laud is the solicitude!** As though the Holy Spirit *plays with uncertain and flexible sayings,* and does *not Himself interpret Himself* by these sentences: ***"He ascended into heaven, He penetrated the heavens, He ascended above all the heavens, that He may receive the heavens"*** — that is, ***the possession and kingdom of the heavens*** — ***and sit at the right of God,*** as Paul says: ***"He ought to reign, until He puts all enemies under His feet;"*** or (as Peter understanding the same, says) ***"He ought to receive heaven, until the time of the restitution is present."***

And how great petulance were it, if one should feign an *amphibologram* in these: ***"Christ ought to penetrate heaven, and possess it"?*** Likewise: ***"Christ ought to break the devil, and conquer death."*** Here certainly no one is so temerarious as to say: ***"Christ ought to be penetrated by heaven, to be conquered by death,"*** and so on. Wherefore we are most-certain of the certain sentence, which the Scripture itself begets and shows.

**On Castellio** true is the judgement of Beza — that his audacity in *converting the Bibles* was ***sacrilegious and impudent,*** and that he was wont ***not to accommodate himself to the words, but the words to his own sense.*** And it is **Castellio** whom in the version of Peter's words the rest have followed, sensing that Christ is so *contained by His body by heaven* that He *cannot be elsewhere.*

### But — Is This a New Phrase? Justin, Nazianzen, Syriac

**But this phrasis is not new,** they say. For **Justin Martyr** says that *Christ is detained in heaven.* Then **Nazianzen** says that *Christ is captured by heaven.* Then the Syriac version says: ***"The heavens capture Christ."***

**Justin,** however, by no means says that *Christ is detained by any physical place of the material heaven,* nor does he put the *material heaven firmer than Christ the possessor of heaven, even according to the flesh.* But he says: ***ὁ πατὴρ κατέχει τὸν υἱὸν*** — *"the Father has the Son who ascended into the heavens with Himself"* — ***κατέχει,*** *detains* — but *where and how?* ***At His own right, βασιλεύοντα, reigning, until He subjects the enemies under His feet,*** or (as he himself speaks) ***πατάξῃ, He strikes.*** Justin therefore does not will that the *flesh of Christ is so detained in heaven that it can nowhere be elsewhere.*

**Nazianzen** — whose authority long after the fact was at last (by *good auspices,* it seems) found by these versioners and *boastfully brandished as in a triumph* — plainly does not prove the exposition of Peter's words which they assert. For Nazianzen thus says: *"The Arians object to us these sentences:*

**1.** *Christ ought to reign, until,* and so on.
**2.** *Christ* ***ὑπὸ οὐρανοῦ δεχθῆναι*** *— to be excepted, or received, or taken up by heaven — until the times of the restitution of all things.*
**3.** *Christ ought to sit at the right, until He obtains victory over His enemies.*

*Therefore, say the Arians, there will at some time be an end of Christ's kingdom.*"

**Nazianzen responds** with these very sayings (which certainly not even the Arians themselves understood of the *local inclusion of Christ in heaven* — for if they had done so, they would have had a specimen more cunning of their own opinion, and could have argued thus: *"Christ is and reigns in a certain physical place of heaven; but this heaven will perish, and the physical place will perish, and all physical places such as are now will perish, since heaven and earth shall perish; therefore Christ's kingdom also shall perish, indeed Christ Himself shall no longer be anywhere,"* and so on) — with these very sayings, I say, Nazianzen shows that ***the infinite duration of Christ's kingdom is contained.*** Nazianzen therefore understands by these words the ***kingdom and dominion*** of Christ, not the *temporal or local commoration of Christ in the heavens.* For he says that ***these two are the same:*** **(1) Christ does not leave the heavens, nor departs from the heavens;** **(2) Christ does not make an end of reigning.**

**The Syriac version** thus reads: ***ר ק י ב ל ו ן*** ***ל ד ה ל א ׁ ָ י ַ מ ַ ש ְ ל א ֹ ל ְ ו*** — *"which it is necessary that the heavens receive"* — that is, *"it is fitting for the heavens, and it behoves the heavens, that they receive Him, cede to Him, open themselves to Him, and obey Him, and be subjected"* — just as subjects receive their Lords — which is ***δέξασθαι βασιλέα καὶ ἄρχοντα,*** as in **Plutarch** this phrase often stands.

**I add to these versions also the words of Oecumenius,** who says: *"Oportet — pro, oportuit — Christum caelum accipere,"* was said just as that in 2 Pet. 1: ***"Accipiens" — pro, "Accepit potestatem à Deo Patre honorem et gloriam."*** ***ἐνθρονιζόμενος γὰρ, καὶ εἰς τὴν πατρῴαν ἀπαραιτήτως εἰσδεχόμενος οὐσίαν, οὐρανὸν ὑπεδέξατο, ὡς ὑποδοχεῖον ἢ ἀπεσταλμένον, καὶ αὐτὸν οὐρανὸς οὐκ ἐδέξατο*** — that is, ***"Inthronised, or received onto the royal throne, and admitted without refusal to the paternal seat — He received heaven and subjected it to Himself as a receptacle,"*** that is, ***"as a throne (just as heaven is called the place of the dwelling of God Himself); heaven did not accept Him, but He Himself accepted heaven,"*** and so on. Thus **Oecumenius,** whom I allege *that we may see the sentence of the whole antiquity is not that this place of Peter must be read and interpreted in an inverted word-order.*

**In the New Testament** (that we may add this in passing) the vocable ***δέχομαι*** is employed for *to accept, except, receive, and perceive something or a person* — as: [treatment continues in the next tranche, together with the full doctrine of Ubiquity of Christ's human nature].¹⁵⁵

*(This concludes the core Doctrina de Communicatione Idiomatum. The next tranche opens the specific* An Christus sit omnipraesens *question of the Ubiquity of the human nature — for which the mandatory Greek JPG cross-check applies especially, since Selnecker's Chalcedonian-formula Greek and his Cyril/Damascene citations are at their densest.)*

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

## Completion of the *δέχομαι* Vocable Study

The vocable ***δέχομαι*** is employed for *to accept, except, receive, and perceive* something or a person — as:

- ***ὁ δεχόμενος ὑμᾶς, ἐμὲ δέχεται*** (Matt. 10): *"He who receives you, receives me."*
- ***δέχεσθαι παιδίον*** (Matt. 18); ***δέξασθαι Χριστόν, δέξασθαι Παῦλον*** (Gal. 4): *"As an Angel of God you received me, as Christ Jesus."*
- **Or of things:** ***δεξάμενος τὰ παρὰ Ἐπαφροδίτου*** (Phil. 4): *"Receiving the things you sent, from Epaphroditus."*
- **Or of the word of God:** ***ἐδέξαντο τὸν λόγον*** (Acts 17).
- **And the grace of God:** 2 Cor. 6: ***μὴ εἰς κενὸν τὴν χάριν τοῦ Θεοῦ δέξασθαι ὑμᾶς***.
- **Or consolation:** ***παράκλησιν;*** likewise ***κοινωνίαν τῆς διακονίας τῆς εἰς τοὺς ἁγίους δέξασθαι*** (2 Cor. 8).
- **And the helmet of salvation to receive** (Eph. 6).
- **Or the kingdom of God:** ***δέξασθαι βασιλείαν τοῦ Θεοῦ*** (Mark 10) — just as ***Plutarch*** uses ***δέχεσθαι τὴν ἀρχήν;*** likewise, *place and city* — that is, *to receive the right, administration, and empire of the city,* ***ἀναδέχεσθαι τὴν πόλιν*** (Plutarch, *Fabius*), just as ***δέξασθαι τὸν οὐρανόν*** = ***to receive heaven,*** that is, *the kingdom of heaven, the possession and administration of heaven, not as a minister or citizen, but as Lord, Prince of heaven* (Acts 2:65).

**Let this be the summa of what we have said on the place of Peter:** *Even if by the passive form of speaking Christ is said to have been received by heaven, nevertheless the pious and erudite never so understood this as though Christ by His body could not also be elsewhere, wheresoever He wills to be present; but Christ so ascended into heaven and was visibly received by heaven, that He might sit at the right of God Almighty, and be able, wheresoever He wills, to be present by His own body* — namely, *where He said and promised that He wills to be present.* This on the place of Peter we ought to have admonished, to which what we above indicated concerning Vigilius the Martyr [[note-148]] must be added, and joined by accurate repetition.

## On the Sayings *"Relinquo mundum;" "Me non semper habetis vobiscum,"* and So On

Of these sayings we have treated above in the confutation of Arius's arguments [[note-145]]. Now I shall add **Bucer's** words:

> *"The Lord willed to teach by this testification of His going from the world to the Father nothing else than that He is passing from the reason of living of this age — laborious and exposed to the senses — into the glorious and celestial reason of living, which is proper of the resurrection and of the future age. This reason of the future life is such that as no eye has seen and no ear has heard, so no man's heart is able to enter — that is, cannot be comprehended by our reason."* Thus Bucer.

**John 16:** ***"Exivi a Patre, et veni in mundum; iterum relinquo mundum, et proficiscor ad Patrem."*** Here the *antithesis* shows that ***the return is opposed to the coming,*** or ***the highest glorification (which is the departure from the world) to the exinanition (which was the coming into the world).*** Rightly therefore also **Calvin** admonishes, saying: *"That Christ denies He will be always with the disciples, must be referred to the kind of presence to which carnal cult and sumptuous honour is fitting."*

**Rightly we say:** ***"Christus reliquit mundum"*** — per the *Communication of the Idioms,* according to His humanity, which ***τοπικῶς*** (locally) is not in the world — that is, *in the reason of this world or of our age,* which is *by the natural and earthly mode of the presence of familiarity.* For thus Christ Himself wills to be understood, when after the Resurrection to the disciples (to whom He exhibited Himself present to be seen and touched), He says (Luke 24): ***"These things I spoke to you when I was still with you"*** — that is, ***when before my Resurrection I conversed with you in familiar manner.***¹⁵⁶

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

## *An Christus sit Omnipraesens etiam sua humanitate* — Is Christ Omnipresent Also by His Humanity?

**Christ is everywhere according to the divine nature.** He is *not everywhere according to the substance of His body* in a *physical, created, and natural mode,* or in the *finite and circumscribed mode of a body.* Of *Omnipresence,* however, we have treated above in the refutation of Arius's arguments [[note-145]].

For **Christ can be also by His own body — wheresoever, howsoever, and whensoever He wills to be — and truly is there where He said and promised that by His body and blood He wills to be;** and yet ***has and retains a true body.***

**But we speak in this question — in which we say Christ according to the substance of His body is not everywhere** — of ***ἰδιοποιήσει,*** which is properly the *consideration of the natural and habitual idiomata,* in which the *essential properties of the natures* are considered, and by the *distinctive particles* is made the declaration ***according to which nature.*** But by no means do we speak of ***κοινοποιήσει*** and ***μεταποιήσει.*** For what pertains to the *offices and benefits of Christ* — which are to be understood *according to each nature* — the *natural properties of the human nature are not simply considered,* but ***these foundations are held in the view and minds of the pious:***

**First,** that ***the Son of God can, together with His assumed body, be and do whatever and wherever He wills, without injury or annihilation of the body.***

**Second,** that ***the human nature in Christ, from the union with the Logos, has above its own strengths and natural properties*** — not only *created gifts surpassing the gifts of all other creatures,* but also *is made a hypostatic organ,* ***through which, in which, and with which*** *are exercised, offered, exerted, and exhibited to the human race the works and actions of the divine and eternal Omnipotence* — so that ***God the Father does nothing with the human race except through the Son, and the Son does nothing with the human race without His assumed nature, but with it, in it, and through it does and completes all things which He does and completes with men, on account of whom He assumed the human nature.***

**Third,** that ***this same human nature of Christ has been exalted above all creatures to the Omnipotent, eternal, and uncreated right of God*** — *not only in a physical place, but in the Logos above all the heavens, and above all the places of places* — ***having all power in heaven and on earth, all things present to His eyes, all things in His hands, all things subject to His feet, no thing being excepted except Him who subjected all to Him.*** And this is properly ***μεταποίησις,*** as **Gregory of Nyssen** speaks.

**Fourth,** that ***all things which Christ in His own word and Testament says, affirms, ordains, and promises, are true, truly are done, truly so stand,*** *whether they cohere with the natural properties, quantities, and physical qualities, or do not cohere.* For ***the word is not the physical word, but is the Word of the Word*** — that is, ***the Word of the Son of God Omnipotent and truthful,*** *greater, stronger, and more potent than every creature, physical, and reason.* ***"The word of the Lord is in virtue; the voice of the Lord in magnificence — she is the mistress of all nature."*** Whoever loses this word ***truly loses the Word, the Son of God.***

### The Presence of Christ with the Church According to Office

**What pertains to the office of Christ:** Christ is present with His Church *according to each nature,* as He says: ***"I shall be with you unto the consummation of the age"*** — the *whole and the total,* by the *mode which is not physical and local* by which Christ was in the womb of the Mother, was on earth visibly conversing, hung on the cross by His body, lay in the sepulchre — as the Angel says: ***"He has risen; He is not here"*** — but ***by that mode which is plainly hyperphysical, divine, and incomprehensible*** — ***true, however, by that reason which is known and possible to Me alone,*** and *which suffices for My promise and My office.* For ***I satisfy My promises according to each nature — as I am God and as I am man.*** ***Anathema on him who divides Christ.***

### Luther's Rule on Christ's Modes of Presence

**Let the rule often repeated by Dr. Luther be held:**

> ***"Christ has many modes by which He can be by His own body — wherever, whenever, and howsoever He wills to be — whether in one, or in many, or in all places, or outside all places — more than we in this life can understand and attain by our own reason."***

This rule, taken from Christ's word, confirms the *Omnipotence and truth of Christ,* and *refutes the blasphemies and deliria of all devils and heretics, Sacramentarians, and similar fanatics;* and is ***immovable, certain, unconquered, and infallible,*** and *by no one — save him who petulantly contradicts the expressly-set-forth Christ and His word — can be denied.*¹⁵⁷

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

## *Christus est in Cœna Præsens* — Christ Is Present in the Supper

**Christ is present in the Supper — not only according to His divine nature and by His Spirit, but also *with His human nature,* or *by the substance of His body and blood.*** And this ***not according to the rule of physical property*** — ***κατὰ τὸν κανόνα τῆς ἰδιότητος φυσικῆς*** — but **according to the truth of the words and promises of Christ, and according to the divine will and power.**

We therefore say: **Christ is in the Supper — both whole and totally** — that is, ***according to each nature*** — *not simply through that Communication of the Idioms which is* ***ἰδιοποιΐα*** *(and which hitherto has been discussed), but also through* ***κοινοποίησιν*** — that is, *by reason of office, which is of the whole Person according to each nature.* For the *office of Christ is to preserve the promises just as they sound.*

Whence piously and excellently **Ambrose** says:

> *"It is clear that beyond the order of nature the Virgin generated; and this which we make [in the Supper] — the body — is from the Virgin. Why do you seek here the order of nature in the body of Christ, since beyond nature is the Lord Jesus's very birth from the Virgin? Truly of the flesh of Christ which was crucified, which was buried — truly therefore is the Sacrament of that flesh. The Lord Jesus Himself cries: **This is my body.** Before the benediction of the celestial words, another species is named. After the consecration, the body of Christ is truly given. He Himself has said it — His blood. Before the consecration, it is called the blood of Christ. Thou sayest: Amen — that is, ***it is true.*** What the mouth speaks, let the intent mind confess; what the discourse sounds, let the affection perceive."*

### Luther on the Sacrament — the Repetition of the Doctrine of the Concordia Domini

**Luther** in the *Repetition of the Doctrine on the Supper of the Lord:*

> *"Of the sacrament of the Body and Blood of Christ, we have never taught, nor do we yet teach, that Christ from heaven or from the right of God descends and ascends — neither visibly nor invisibly. For we firmly retain the article of faith: 'He ascended into the heavens, He sits at the right hand of God, thence He will come.' And to the divine Omnipotence we commend how His body and blood in the Supper are given to us and exhibited, where by His own mandate we come together and the institution is preserved. We do not think, nor make mention, that there is made either an ascent or a descent, but most-simply we retain His words: 'This is my body; this is my blood.'"*

### Melanchthon on the Question of Christ's Body in the Sacrament

**Philip [Melanchthon]:** *"There falls a question: How can Christ be in the Sacrament by His body, since a body cannot be simultaneously in different places? I answer: Christ said He wills to be present; therefore He truly is in the Sacrament, and indeed corporeally. And no other cause of this corporeal presence is to be sought — save this: the words so sound; wherefore the thing so stands as the words sound. And what pertains to the body — Christ can, when He wills, be everywhere, in all places. Therefore another is the reason of Christ's body, and of our bodies. But of Ubiquity we ought not to dispute; here far another thing is being done in the Supper. And the scholastic doctors also speak nothing of Ubiquity, but retain the simple sentence of the corporeal presence of Christ."*

### The Pious Antiquity on the Reality of Christ's Body from the Supper

**Pious antiquity** — as from **Athanasius** and others is established — against the *Manichaeans* and similar *fanatics* alleged the *words of the Supper on the truth of Christ's body:* Christ gives us in the Supper His true body and blood — not an *imaginative or significative or phantastic* one. Therefore Christ has a true body, and not an imaginative or phantastic one.

We therefore say: If Christ has a true human body, then He gives it to us in the Supper as He said. But Christ has a true human body. Therefore He gives it to us in the Supper as He promised. And this is the sentence of pious antiquity.

**Conversely:** If Christ does not have a true body, He does not give one. But He does not have (per the *Manichaeans* and ***δοκηταί***). Therefore He does not give. Likewise: If Christ does not give a true body in the Supper as He said and promised, He does not have a true body. But Christ does not give a true and substantial body of His own to those eating the bread of benediction (per the ***μυστηριομάχους***). Therefore also He does not have a true body. And this *absurdity* is necessary to follow from the sentence of those who *deny the corporeal presence of Christ in the Supper,* and *the true manducation of the body,* according to the words: ***"Take, eat, this is my body."*** For to these the ***Manichaeans*** substitute contrary words: ***"This is not my true body, but an imaginative, phantastic, figurative one."***

**Let us close with the words of Epiphanius:** *"He who does not believe that He is truthful, as He said, has fallen from grace and salvation."* Let those also who deny the doctrine of the corporeal presence and manducation be mindful of the examples of **Berengarius, Carlstadt, Zwingli,** and many similar men. **Berengarius,** dying, said: *"Today my Lord Jesus Christ shall appear to me — either unto glory, as I hope, on account of my revocation and penitence; or unto damnation, as I fear, on account of the corruption of others whom I could not lead back into the way of truth."* ***"Let those tremble"*** (says **Gerson**) ***"and beware, who to the similitude of demons rejoice to draw others with themselves to destruction, especially young men and boys — such I know not if they could ever sufficiently repent. Their proper damnation ought to have sufficed them. What of the evil examples of great and lettered men?"*** Thus Gerson. Let us proceed now in the Symbol.¹⁵⁸
