
# *Sedet ad Dexteram Dei Patris Omnipotentis* — He Sitteth at the Right Hand of God the Father Almighty

The prior Article on the Ascension is as it were the antecedent, of which this is the consequent and the fruit. For as Christ descended from the heavens and was made man, that in His self-exinanition He might be humiliated below all Angels and men, so now He hath ascended into the heavens in glorious triumph, that above all Angels and men He might be exalted to the right hand of God in supreme sublimity. Wherefore these two Articles — *He ascended into the heavens,* and *He sitteth at the right hand of God* — so cohere that they cannot be separated. For He ascended in order that He might sit at the right hand of the Father.

And when it is now asked of Christ, who ascended into the heavens, *where* He is in the heavens, Scripture pointeth us to no fallen and physical place — neither in the East nor West, nor North nor South, nor in some region under us with respect to the Antipodes — but leadeth us to *the right hand of God the Father Almighty.* Christ is in the heavens. But where? *At the right hand of the Father.* So Scripture always speaketh, *1 Pet. 3:* ***"He is at the right hand of God, gone into heaven, with Angels, authorities, and powers subject unto Him."*** And *Acts 2:* ***"For David is not ascended into the heavens; but he himself saith, The LORD said unto my Lord, Sit thou at my right hand."***

Whence **Augustine** saith: ***"He ascended into the heavens, that is, He placed the condition of our nature, which He assumed of the human mother, above the heavens at the right hand of God the Father."*** The same: ***"By 'right hand' understand the power which that Man, received of God, hath received — that He may come to judge, who first had come to be judged. For this He hath ascended into heaven."*** And again: ***"The Man received by Christ, sitting at the right hand of the Father, reigneth."***

The Ascension of Christ therefore is not a simple transition, procession, change of place, ascent from a lower to a higher chamber, or a local going-up from a place of the earthly element into a celestial place — but is also the supreme glorification, exaltation, and access to the possession of the spiritual and eternal kingdom, and of the dominion given Him by the Father, that He might rule and moderate all things by His virtue.

## Three Things to Be Asked Here

Here therefore three things must be asked. **First,** what it is *to sit at the right hand of God.* **Second,** what kind of predication is *Christ sitteth at the right hand of God* — and what is the diversity of the propositions (*Christ is Creator,* or *God was born and suffered,* or the like which we have hitherto had) from *Christ sitteth at the Father's right hand.* **Third,** what and in what things doth the *glorification of the human nature in Christ* consist.

### 1. What It Is to Sit at the Right Hand of God

Paul (*Eph. 1*) explaineth the phrase *to sit at the Father's right hand* thus: ***"God the Father established Christ at His right hand in the heavens, above every principality, and power, and virtue, and dominion, and above every name that is named, not only in this world but also in that which is to come; and hath put all things under His feet, and gave Him to be head over all the Church."***

*The right hand of God* signifieth no *place* (as Calvin and Beza rightly speak) but the *power* which the Father hath conferred upon Christ, that in His name He may administer the empire of heaven and earth. The right hand of God is eternal, and lacketh all circumscription: wheresoever He reigneth and hath dominion, there is the right hand of God, filling heaven and earth. And it is great folly to define the right hand of God by the circumscription of place.

**To sit at the right hand of God is supreme glory, majesty, excellence, dominion, virtue, and power over all things.** To sit at the right hand of God is to be consort of the divine and supreme empire above all things, and to hold the supreme authority of ruling.¹⁵⁹

### 2. Of What Kind This Predication Is

But what kind of predication this is, and whether it is like the earlier propositions which are explained through the *communicatio idiomatum* — *Christ hath been exalted to the right hand of God, and sitteth at the Father's right hand* — must be accurately inquired.

Certain men, without any consideration (as men puffed up by vain science are wont, secure), rush forth saying that *Christ is and sitteth at the right hand of God the Father through the communicatio idiomatum,* which they will have to be a *verbal or dialectical predication,* the properties of both natures being retained. But being asked *according to which nature,* they answer *according to the divine.* And so sacrilegiously they exclude the human nature from that supreme glory and excellence to which Christ, after His Resurrection and Ascension according to the human nature, hath been exalted (*Acts 2; Heb. 1:8; Eph. 1; Phil. 2*). Let us therefore dismiss these as babblers ignorant of what they believe and speak.

Certain others say: *He sitteth at the right hand of GOD through the communicatio idiomatum, according to the human nature — resting to wit from all miseries in some corporeal or natural and finite place.* This opinion, plainly *fanatical,* Calvin himself and Beza refute — affirming that *the right hand of God is everywhere,* and that *Christ truly sitteth at the right hand of God* — that is, *that to Christ hath been handed over all power, so that in the flesh in which He had been humiliated He should reign in the stead of the Father and be second to Him.*

Let us however hear how *pious and learned Antiquity* hath spoken.

**Athanasius:** ***κάθου ἐκ δεξιῶν μου· τοῦτο εἰς τὸ κυριακὸν σῶμα λέλεκται· τὸ σῶμά ἐστιν ᾧ λέγει, κάθου ἐκ δεξιῶν μου.***

*"'Sit thou at my right hand.' This hath been said unto the body of the Lord. The body it is unto which He saith, Sit at my right hand."* And Athanasius addeth the reason, saying that *according to the divine nature Christ is beforehand in heaven and on earth, and filleth all things, and is not placed anew upon any throne,* ***καθέζεται.*** These words of Athanasius are also cited by **Theodoret,** dial. 6, fol. 41.¹⁶⁰

**The same [Athanasius], against the Arians:** ***ὁ κύριος τῆς δόξης οὐ δοξάζεται, ἀλλ᾿ ἡ σὰρξ τοῦ κυρίου τῆς δόξης, αὕτη λαμβάνει δόξαν, συναναβαίνουσα εἰς οὐρανόν· ὅσα οὖν λέγει ἡ γραφή, ὅτι ἔλαβεν οὗτος καὶ ἐδοξάσθη, διὰ τὴν ἀνθρωπότητα αὐτοῦ λέγει, οὐ διὰ τὴν θεότητα.***

*"The Lord of glory doth not acquire glory; but the flesh of the Lord of glory — she receiveth glory, ascending together into heaven. Whatever things therefore Scripture saith, that this One hath received and hath been glorified (namely in time, the union of the two natures being made), it saith them on account of the humanity, not of the divinity."*

And when it saith: ***"God super-exalted Him and gave Him the name which is above every name"*** — He speaketh of the temple of His body. For it is not the Most High who is exalted, but the flesh of the Most High that is exalted. And to the flesh of the Most High hath been given the name which is above every name. For neither did the Word of God receive the beginning of a name, but *His flesh with Him hath been made partaker of the divine name.*

**Gregory of Nyssa:** ***τῇ δεξιᾷ τοῦ Θεοῦ ὑψωθείς. τίς οὖν ὑψώθη; ὁ ταπεινὸς ἢ ὁ ὕψιστος; τί δὲ ταπεινόν, εἰ μὴ τὸ ἀνθρώπινον; τί δὲ ἄλλο παρὰ τὸ Θεῖον ἐστίν ὁ ὕψιστος; ἀλλὰ μὴν ὁ Θεὸς ὑψωθῆναι οὐ δεῖται ὕψιστος ὤν· τὸ ἀνθρώπινον ἄρα ὁ Ἀπόστολος ὑψωθῆναι λέγει. ὑψωθῆναι δὲ διὰ τὸ Κύριον καὶ Χριστὸν γενέσθαι· οὐ τὴν προαιώνιον ὕπαρξιν ὁ Κύριος διὰ τοῦ ἐποίησε παρίστησιν ὁ Ἀπόστολος, ἀλλὰ τὴν ἐκ ταπεινοῦ πρὸς τὸ ὑψηλὸν μεταποίησιν, τὴν ἐκ δεξιᾶς τοῦ Θεοῦ γεγενημένην. σαφηνίζεται γὰρ διὰ τούτου τοῦ ῥήματος τὸ τῆς εὐσεβείας μυστήριον· ὁ γὰρ εἰπών, τῇ δεξιᾷ τοῦ Θεοῦ ὑψωθείς, φανερῶς ἐκκαλύπτει τὴν Ἀπόρρητον τοῦ μυστηρίου οἰκονομίαν, ὅτι ἡ δεξιὰ τοῦ Θεοῦ ἡ ποιητικὴ τῶν ὄντων τῶν πάντων, ἥτις ἐστίν ὁ Κύριος, δι᾿ οὗ τὰ πάντα ἐγένετο, καὶ χωρὶς οὗ ὑπέστη τῶν οὐδέν, αὕτη τὸν ἑνωθέντα πρὸς αὐτὸν ἄνθρωπον, εἰς τὸ ἴδιον ἀνήγαγεν ὕψος διὰ τῆς ἑνώσεως.***

*"'Exalted at the right hand of God'* (Acts 2). *But WHO was exalted — the humble, or the Most High? What is humble, save that which is human? What else, besides that which is divine, is the Most High? But for God to be exalted, being Most High, is no need. Therefore it is the human that the Apostle saith to have been exalted — exalted namely because the Lord was made Christ. The Apostle by the 'made' asserteth not the eternal existence of the Lord, but designeth the transformation for the better, from the humble to the excelling — namely, that which is made at the right hand of God. For through this very expression 'He made' is declared the mystery of piety. For He who saith, 'Exalted at the right hand of God,' manifestly revealeth the ineffable dispensation of the mystery: that the right hand of God — the creatrix of all things that are, which right hand is the Lord Himself, through whom all things were made, and without whom nothing of what is subsisteth — this hand hath led up the man united unto her into her own sublimity through the union."*¹⁶¹

**Chrysostom on Psalm 1:** ***ὁ δεσπότης τὸν ἄνθρωπον, τὴν φύσιν τὴν σὴν, θρόνον ἐκάθισε βασιλικόν, ἄνω πάσης ἀρχῆς, καὶ ἐξουσίας καὶ δυνάμεως, καὶ παντὸς ὀνόματος ὀνομαζομένου. τί ταύτης ἴσον λέγοιτ᾿ ἂν τῆς τιμῆς, ἢ ἀπαρχὴ τοῦ γένους ἡμῶν ὁ τοσαῦτα προσκεκρουκότα, ἡ λιμωμένη ἐν ὕψει τοσούτῳ κάθηται, καὶ τοσαύτης ἀπολαύει τιμῆς;***

*"The Master hath placed man, thy nature, upon a royal throne, above every principality, and powers and virtues, and every name that is named. What can be said equal to so great an honour, save that our first-fruit — which had so often stumbled, and had been famished — is set on so lofty a height, and enjoyeth so great honour?"*

And: ***ἡμετέραν γὰρ φύσιν ἐκάθισεν ἐπὶ τῶν χερουβὶμ καὶ πάσης τῆς ἀγγελικῆς δυνάμεως. τὸ ἔσχατον μέρος τῆς λογικῆς κτίσεως ὁ ἄνθρωπος ἦν. ἀλλ᾽ οἱ πόδες ἐγένοντο κεφαλή, καὶ εἰς τὸν θρόνον ἀνηνέχθησαν τὸν βασιλικόν· τὸ σῶμα γυμνὸν ἐκ ναυαγίου, λαμπρᾷ περιβάλλει τῇ στολῇ, καὶ πρὸς τιμὴν ἄγει τὴν ἀνωτάτω.***

*"For our nature He hath seated above the Cherubim and every angelic power. The last part or work of the rational creation was man; but now the feet have been made the head, and led up unto the royal throne; and the body which came naked from the shipwreck the Lord hath vested in bright robes, and led up to supreme glory."*

***ποίῳ χρήσομαι λόγῳ; ποίοις φθέγξομαι ῥήμασιν ἀγνοῶ· ἡ φύσις ἡ σαθρά, ἡ φύσις ἡ εὐτελής, ἡ πάντων ἐλάττων ὑποδεχθεῖσα, πάντα νενίκηκεν, ὑπερέβαλεν ἅπαντα· σήμερον ἀνωτέρα γενέσθαι πάντων ἠξίωται, σήμερον ἀπέλαβεν ὅπερ ἐπὶ χρόνον πολὺν ἐπεθύμησαν ἄγγελοι· σήμερον ἀρχάγγελοι τῶν ἐπὶ πολὺ ποθουμένων θεαταὶ γίνεσθαι ἐδεῶντο, καὶ τὴν φύσιν τὴν ἡμετέραν ἐν τῷ θρόνῳ τοῦ βασιλέως τῇ δόξῃ τῆς ἀθανασίας ἐκλάμπουσαν κατενόησαν. τίς οὖν ἦν ὁ ἀκούσας τό, κάθου ἐκ δεξιῶν μου; τίς ἦν; ἡ φύσις ἐκείνη, πρὸς ἣν εἶπε, γῆ εἶ, καὶ εἰς γῆν ἀπελεύσῃ.***

*"What discourse shall I use? With what words shall I utter it, I know not. Nature putrid, nature abject, less than all — received up — hath conquered all, hath surpassed all: this day she is counted worthy to be made supreme above all; this day hath she received what for a long time the Angels desired; this day were the Archangels beseeching to be spectators of those long-desired things, and beheld our nature shining upon the throne of the King in the glory of immortality. Who therefore was He that heard, 'Sit at my right hand'? Who was He? That very nature to which He said: 'Earth thou art, and unto earth shalt thou return.'"*¹⁶²

**Vigilius, lib. 5, cap. 3:** ***"God exalted Him, and gave Him the name which is above every name, that in the name of Jesus every knee should bow — of the heavenly, the earthly, and those below."*** And again: ***"'Exalt the head' (Ps. 110). 'Made better than the Angels, by how much He hath by inheritance obtained a more excellent name than they.' No one is so out of his mind that he understandeth these things according to the nature of divinity. These things in the Son of God agree with the nature of His flesh — according to which He hath been exalted, honoured, crowned, and hath obtained the dignity of a better name — He who according to the nature of the Word hath needed none of these things."***¹⁶³

*"Such things,"* saith Chrysostom (Theodoret also citing), *"have all been said not of God the Word, but of what is according to the flesh"* — as also *"Given to me is all power in heaven and on earth"* (Matt. 28), and *"Glorify me with the glory"* &c. (John 17), which **Athanasius** willeth to be understood ***ἀνθρωπίνως*** — that is, according to the human nature: the same **Cyril** always admonisheth. *"For to the Word,"* saith Cyril, *"no adventitious glory befitteth — ἐπίκτητος δύναμις καὶ δόξα — nor empire extrinsically granted."*

**Oecumenius on Heb. 1, from Photius and Chrysostom:** ***κάθου ἐκ δεξιῶν μου. Ἀνήνεγκε τὴν ἀπαρχὴν τῆς φύσεως τῆς ἡμετέρας τῷ πατρί, καὶ τὸ δῶρον ἐθαύμασε ὁ πατήρ, καὶ διὰ τὴν ἀξίαν τοῦ προσενέγκαντος, καὶ διὰ τὸ ἄμωμον τοῦ προσενεχθέντος, ὡς οἰκείαις αὐτὸ δέξασθαι χερσί, καὶ θεῖναι πλησίον οὗ δῶρον, καὶ εἰπεῖν, κάθου ἐκ δεξιῶν μου. ὁ τόπος τῆς καθέδρας τὸ ὁμότιμον σημαίνει. Μέγα μὲν οὖν, καὶ οὐ μόνον ὑπὲρ τὴν ἀνθρωπείαν φύσιν, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὑπὲρ ἅπασαν τὴν κτίσιν, ἀνθρωπίνως καὶ αὐτῷ εἴρηται· ὡς γὰρ Θεὸς ὁ Υἱός, αἰώνιον ἔχει τὸν θρόνον· εἰς τὸν αἰῶνα τοῦ αἰῶνος. οὐ γὰρ μετὰ τὸν σταυρὸν καὶ τὸ πάθος ταύτης ὡς Θεὸς τῆς τιμῆς πρώτως ἠξιώθη, ἀλλ᾿ ἔλαβεν ὡς ἄνθρωπος ὅπερ εἶχεν ὡς Θεός. ὡς ἄνθρωπος ἀκούει, κάθου ἐκ δεξιῶν μου· ὡς δὲ Θεὸς αἰώνιον ἔχει τὸ κράτος.***

*"'Sit at my right hand.' — He hath borne up to the Father the first-fruits of our nature; and the Father admired this gift both on account of the dignity of Him who offered, and on account of the innocence of that which was offered; and receiving it in His own hands, He set it down in the place next to Himself, and said, 'Sit at my right hand.' The place of the seat signifieth equality of honour. This is great indeed, and not only above human nature, but above the whole creation. Yet these things have been said of Him humanly. For as God the Son hath an eternal throne, unto ages of ages. Not after the cross and the passion was He first, as God, deemed worthy of so great an honour — but He received AS MAN what He had AS GOD. As man He heareth, 'Sit at my right hand'; as God He hath eternal power."*¹⁶⁴

*I shall recite also the words of my preceptors, which I heard dictated at Wittenberg, together with a thousand others, two-and-twenty years ago:*

***"Because in Christ are two natures, divine and human, know that the nature exalted is that in which He was crucified. For the Son of Abraham and of David according to the flesh is exalted according to the flesh — and is not only Son of Abraham and David, but also Lord, sitting at the right hand of the Father, having all things subject unto Him. He hath not been exalted according to the divine nature, in which from eternity unto eternity He is always equal to the Father. And the name which is above every name is not given to the divine nature in Christ, because that nature hath that name from eternity — namely, glory, power, and divine majesty — not by the largesse of grace, but by nature. But this name hath been given to the form of a servant, that is, to the human nature, the Son of Man of Mary, who had it not before, nor could He have it. So Christ in John 17, when He prayeth that this name, this glory and power may be given Him, prayeth not that it be given to the divine nature, which hath had it always from eternity and never lost it; but that it be given to the form of a servant. 'He gave these things to man, not to God,' saith Augustine. And this is the name of which Isa. 42 is written: 'I am the Lord, this is my name, I will not give my glory to another.' This Son alone hath this name. 'God made Him Lord and Christ,' Acts 2. Therefore we adore this Son together with the Father and the Spirit, with ONE adoration, together with His unstained flesh. For the flesh is adored in the one hypostasis of the Word. So Augustine also saith: 'I therefore adore the Lord's flesh, nay, the perfect humanity in Christ, because it was received of divinity and united to deity.'"***¹⁶⁵

These things I recite with the best faith. And the same are extant more copiously, in almost the same words, expounded in the first Tome of the works of *D.D. Maior* on chapter 2 to the Philippians.

The summa of all these things pious Antiquity hath embraced in these rules:

- **Cyril,** lib. 2 *de recta fide ad Reginas:* *"To have received glory, power, and kingdom over all is to be referred to the conditions of humanity."*
- **Leo,** *Epist.* 81 *ad Palestinos:* *"Whatsoever Christ received in time, He received according to the man, to whom are conferred those things which He had not."*
- **Hilary:** *"That which is from time hath received the glory of that which is without time."*
- The same **Athanasius, Ambrose, Augustine, Epiphanius,** and all the orthodox repeat this against the Arians and their like, and out of the sacred letters show *that glory is required not of the Word, but of the flesh* — to use Hilary's words.

We have shown therefore that *to sit at the Father's right hand* is *the mystery of assumed flesh* (as **Cyprian** speaketh), and *pertaineth to the glorification of humanity by reason of the body* (as **Ambrose** speaketh). From these pious, learned, and orthodox testimonies of antiquity it may now be judged concerning this predication — *Christ sitteth at the right hand of God the Father almighty.*¹⁶⁶

### 3. This Predication Is Not Similar to the Communicatio Idiomatum Properly So Called

This predication is *not* similar to the earlier ones — *"Christ God hath suffered"* — that is, *through the communicatio idiomatum according to the human nature,* because divinity is impassible; *"Christ man hath created heaven and earth"*; *"Before Abraham was, I am"* — that is, *through the communicatio idiomatum according to the divine nature,* which is from eternity and hath created all things.

But here, when we say *"Christ sitteth at the right hand of God,"* the predication holdeth itself otherwise. In the former propositions, what is proper to one nature is attributed to the person in the concrete, and this is properly named in the schools *communicatio idiomatum.* But in this proposition, *"Christ, Man, sitteth at the right hand of God the Father in the glory of the Father"* — that which is *proper to God* (namely, eternal glory, majesty, kingdom, divine power) is given and attributed to the human nature in Christ *truly, without confusion or equalization of natures and idiomata.* For although the eternal glory of God is an idioma proper to the divine nature — which is given to no creature — yet that Light, and Glory, and ***Λόγος*** shineth, dwelleth, and exerteth itself in its own assumed nature, and with it, and through it: as Paul saith, *"In Christ the man dwelleth all the fulness of divinity."*

The flesh of Christ is by no means ***τῆς οὐσίας τῆς Τριάδος, οὐδὲ ὁμοούσιος τῇ Θεότητι καὶ τῷ Λόγῳ, καὶ οὐ ταὐτὸν ἐν ποιότητι φυσικῇ Θεότης καὶ ἀνθρωπότης*** (*"of the substance of the Trinity, nor consubstantial with the Godhead and the Word; nor is Godhead and manhood the same in natural quality"*) — as **Cyril** speaketh. Nor doth the divinity of the Word effuse essential idiomata outside itself into the assumed nature, or make them equal to the essential attributes of divinity so that, separated from them, they should inhere formally and per se in the humanity — for God is always the head of Christ the man, and the human nature of Christ is beneath God the Creator. But (that we may speak with **Athanasius** according to Scripture) as ***τὸ λογικόν*** is not something separated from its body when it doeth aught, so **God the Λόγος** is not separated from His assumed nature, nor from the temple of His body in which He dwelleth ***σωματικῶς*** — and which He hath exalted in Himself to the Father's right hand — but ***δι᾿ αὐτῆς, ἐν αὐτῇ, καὶ μετ᾿ αὐτῆς*** (*through it, in it, and with it*) exerteth His power and glory, and maketh His human nature above its own condition — ***ὑπὲρ φύσιν, ὑπὲρ ἔννοιαν, ὑπὲρ νοῦν καὶ ὑπὲρ αἴσθησιν τῶν ἀνθρώπων*** — perfect: ***ἀποτελεῖ.***

Certain speak thus: *Christ sitteth at the right hand of the Father, reigneth over all, hath the name that is above every name, and all power in heaven and on earth, through the communicatio idiomatum — because divine glory and power (not merely created, such as is that of Angels and blessed men, but uncreated and eternal, which is the property of the divine nature) is communicated to the human nature in Christ,* ***οὐκ οὐσιωδῶς*** *(as Eutyches and Schwenckfeld raved), but* ***ὑποστατικῶς*** — *so that Christ God reigneth over all and through all places of places, for ever and ever, not without His human nature, but in it, with it, and through it.*

But this phrase and predication of *communicatio idiomatum* doth not agree with the definition set forth above, where it was shown that the *communicatio idiomatum* properly so called is a predication in which what is proper to one nature is attributed to the person in the concrete. For to obtain kingdom above all things, and to be in ineffable glory at the right hand of God, befitteth no creature at all, but is of the divine nature alone. And yet is attributed to the human nature in Christ — not in name only, but truly in the person of Christ — as the testimonies of Antiquity, greater than every exception, do show.

More broadly, however, this form of speech is extended to the *glorification of the human nature in Christ,* which hath not only created gifts, but hath *all power in heaven and on earth communicated and given to it,* and hath received *divine glory,* and hath not only its own essential idiomata or natural properties, but also many things given and communicated to it *out of the hypostatic union with the Logos, and out of glorification.* For here it is said not of the person only, *"The Son of Man hath been exalted, reigneth, vivifieth, remitteth sins, hath all things subject to Him"* — but ALSO *the human nature in Christ hath been carried above every name, and all things are put under the feet of Christ; Christ's flesh is vivifying; His blood cleanseth us from sin.* Wherefore I have admonished above that *propositions are to be distinguished which are not of one and the same genus.*

**Damascene** also nameth this glorification ***κοινωνίαν τῶν αὐχημάτων τῶν θείων*** (*"communion of the divine excellences"*). And **D. Luther** saith: ***"'All power is given me in heaven and on earth' — namely, through the communicatio idiomatum. For to Me the Man, Jesus of Nazareth, hath been communicated and given that power which I had not before, and yet the Λόγος from eternity hath had, and hath from eternity."*** And elsewhere he calleth this glory ***"exaltation real."*** Whence hath sprung the form which some use, *communicatio realis* — which form however was in no way understood by the purer doctors of the Church as the reason of those propositions in which what is proper to one nature (be it property, action, or passion) is attributed to the person in the concrete, and befitteth the person according to the other nature alone. But they understood by it *the glorification of the human nature in Christ,* whereof not only are its essential idiomata predicated, but at the same time is explained *what hath come to it beyond and above its physical properties, and hath been given and communicated by the union and by glorification.*¹⁶⁷

Since however a most hateful contention hath arisen about words — and the scholastic doctors will have only those predications, in which what is proper to one nature is attributed to the person in the concrete, to be referred to by the appellation ***κοινωνίας ἰδιωμάτων*** — plainly, to shun the ***λογομαχία*** in which manifest injury hath been done to many rightly-sensing men, we judge that *there ought to be no fighting over words.* For once things are rightly settled, the reason of words will be easy afterwards. And the things are these: *that Christ with His flesh, sitting at the right hand of God the Father in the glory and throne of majesty higher than the heavens, reigneth, and hath all power in heaven and on earth truly given and communicated to Him according to the flesh* (as pious antiquity rightly spake), *and this not in name only, but* ***τηρήσας ὑπὲρ τὴν φύσιν καὶ ὑπὲρ ἰδιώματα φυσικά*** (*"reserved above nature and above natural properties"*). If the scholastic doctors do not impugn this, it is well. Let them by all means name the *glorification of the human nature,* and separate it from that customary *communicatio idiomatum* of which was said above — or let them name it otherwise — provided that they confess and approve the *thing itself.*

For this cause therefore certain learned and pious men have established *three quasi-genera,* or three classes of propositions, to be rightly discerned:

- **First class:** propositions to which the appellation of *communicatio idiomatum* properly belongeth.
- **Second class:** names of the *office* and of ***ἀποτελεσμάτων*** *personalium,* which befit the person not according to one nature only, but according to both.
- **Third class:** propositions on the *glorification of the human nature in Christ,* which hath ***ὑπὲρ τὴν φύσιν*** many gifts, glory, power, and divine virtue — as hath now been sufficiently shown.¹⁶⁸

**Vigilius**, lib. 2, cap. 4, saith: ***"Many of the orthodox have divided themselves into two parties, not by diversity of sentence, but by profession of the voice — so that what they think in the same way, they will not pronounce in the same way; being led by an idle and altogether superfluous fear, so that it may rightly be applied to them what the Prophet said (Ps. 52 [53]): 'There they trembled where there was no fear.' Hence many are reputed Eutychianists when they are not; and many are thought Nestorians when they are not. And yet you see them, with certain doleful insults worthy of every lamentation, accuse each other with the names of heretics. 'Thou,' saith the one, 'art a Eutychianist,' 'and thou art a Nestorian' — forgetting altogether the apostolic sentence which saith* (Gal. 5:15) *'See lest, while ye accuse one another, ye be consumed one of another.' I beseech you, when ye are sound in sense, why are ye insane in your voices?"*** And such also is our age today, and such are the mores. *O tempora, o mores!*

But those who deny that anything hath been given and communicated to Christ the man which surpasseth the proper conditions of the human nature, and who *enclose the humanity of Christ in some physical place,* so that elsewhere and in the Supper He cannot be with His body according to His own will and promise — and who *prescribe laws to Christ, what and how and to what extent He both willeth and can* — these the Church holdeth not merely for Nestorians, but seeth and lamenteth to be *authors of impiety no longer to be borne in the Church of Christ.* But omitting these, let us now return to the subject.¹⁶⁹

Some, in these words (*"Christ sitteth at the right hand of God, and hath all power given Him in heaven and on earth"*), admit no *communicatio idiomatum,* but simply say that those words must be understood *in respect of the flesh which hath been made consort of the divine empire and of supreme glory — not in name only, but truly and really.* For Christ, according to divinity, is Himself the right hand of God. According to humanity, He is at the right hand of God.

Some further explain *"He sitteth at the Father's right hand"* of the *office according to both natures.* For *to sit is to reign,* and to perform and execute the *royal and priestly office.* This, they say, is of the *whole person.*

Both explanations, of antiquity and of the later authors, agree in one sentence: that *the human nature of Christ is so raised into the supreme sublimity at the right hand of the virtue and majesty of God, that it is excluded neither from kingdom, nor glory, nor office, nor cultus, but is Lady and head of all creatures, and hath all things subject to Him: Angels, Men, Heavens, Sun, Moon, Stars, birds of heaven, sheep, oxen, cattle of the field, fishes of the sea, and all the works of God's hands (*Ps. 8, 1 Cor. 15*).*

## Of the Six Glories of the Human Nature in Christ

We have spoken of the *communicatio idiomatum* properly so called, and added those things which require judgment in discerning propositions that are *not of one and the same genus,* and in whose difference the word of God is to be consulted — the word which attributeth ***ὑπὲρ φύσιν*** many things truly given to the human nature, which cannot and ought not to be referred to that customary mode of the *communicatio idiomatum.*

In what things, however, the glorification of the human nature in Christ consisteth, may be gathered from the things which have hitherto been said.

### First Glory — The Hypostatic Union

The **first glory** of the assumed human nature is the *wonderful and ineffable Union,* by which inseparably the divine nature and the ***Λόγος*** are coupled with it into one person.

### Second Glory — One Adoration

The **second glory and majesty** of the human nature is that with *one λατρεία,* that is, with *one and the same adoration,* the Λόγος incarnate is adored and worshipped together with His assumed flesh or humanity, by reason of the hypostatic union: not ***ἐν μέρει,*** apart, separately, or in some part, or partly as man and partly as God, nor as one adored together with another; but *ONE Christ Jesus is adored with one adoration together with His own flesh — not by co-adoration, but simply by one adoration* — as Creator according to the divine nature, and as Redeemer, who took upon Him the property of Man.

The union is inseparable. Inseparable therefore is also the *invocation:* as we are baptized *not into the divine nature of Christ alone, but also into His death,* which by reason of the human nature is attributed to the Λόγος; and as we are justified *not by the divine nature alone in respect of the name of Christ, but in the Blood of Christ* (*Rom. 5*). Nor let this phantasy hinder us: *"The human nature is a creature, and is not Omnipotent per se, and cannot be everywhere."* This is true of the human nature *adored separately.* But we do not adore the flesh of Christ separately, but adore *Christ God and Man in both natures.*

And it pleaseth to add a few sayings of pious Antiquity.

**Athanasius and Cyril, anathematism:** ***"If any say that the flesh of our Lord, as of a man, is not adorable, or not to be adored — and not to be adored as the flesh of the Lord and of God — let the holy and Catholic Church anathematize him."***

**Athanasius, oration 5 against the Arians:** ***"When we adore, we do not set aside the Λόγος from the flesh. For we know that the Word was made flesh. Who therefore will say, 'Depart from the flesh, that I may adore Thee'? And how would the body of the Lord not be adorable, when the Λόγος extending His bodily hand hath healed the fever-stricken, when uttering the human voice hath raised Lazarus, when with hands stretched forth upon the cross hath cast down the Prince of the air?"***¹⁷⁰

**Cyril of Alexandria on John 6:** *"Confessing the Word of God united to flesh according to substance, we adore one Son and Lord Jesus Christ."* And dial. 6: *"According to the humanity He received this — that every knee should bow unto Him."*

**Cyril to Nestorius:** *"He who saith, 'On account of the Assumer I venerate the Assumed, and on account of the invisible I adore the visible — the received with Him who received' — this man divideth into two Christs Him who is one, establishing in part the Man separately, and in part likewise the God. He evidently divideth the Unity — which is not one co-adored with another, but is understood as ONE Christ Jesus, Son of God only-begotten, to be venerated with one cultus together with His own flesh."*

**The same [Cyril] to Theodoret:** *"If the same is at once God and Man, He is adored as one with one adoration; and God is not co-adored and co-appellated."*

**Theodoret, dial. 2:** *"The body of the Lord, glorified with divine glory, is adored by the celestial powers."* And: *"That body, dignified with session at the right hand of God, is adored by every creature."*

**The same, on 1 Ephesians:** *"That He who is God should sit together with God is not admirable. But that our nature — taken up from ourselves — should be partaker of the same honour with Him who assumed: this it is that surpasseth every miracle."*

**Gregory Nazianzen, ad Cledonium:** *"If any adore not this Crucified, let him be anathema, and let him be reckoned with the Jews the deicides."*

**The same, in the 2nd Epistle ad Cledonium:** *"We adore the Son of God, born of the Father and of the holy Virgin Mary — not as two sons, but as one and the same in deity and honour inseparable. And with these if any, either now or hereafter, agree not, he shall plead his cause before God on the day of judgment."*

**Ambrose, De Spiritu Sancto, lib. 3, cap. 12:** *"The Angels adore not only the divinity of Christ, but also the footstool of His feet. And if they deny that in Christ even the mysteries of the Incarnation are to be adored — in which as it were certain footprints of divinity are expressed, and in which we take note of certain paths of the celestial Word — let them read that the Apostles too adored Him rising in the glory of the flesh."*

And again: *"The Prophet saith that earth is to be adored (*Ps. 99, 'Adore the footstool of His feet'*) — namely that earth which the Lord Jesus took up in the assumption of the flesh. Thus by 'footstool' is understood earth; by 'earth' the flesh of Christ, which even today in the mysteries we adore, and which the Apostles adored in the Lord Jesus."*

**Augustine, on Psalm 99:** ***"'Adore the footstool of His feet, for it is holy.' I turn myself to Christ, because Him I seek here; and I find how, without impiety, earth is adored — without impiety the footstool of His feet is adored. For He hath taken up earth of earth, because flesh is of earth; and of the flesh of Mary He received flesh. And because He walked here in this very flesh, and HATH GIVEN THAT VERY FLESH UNTO US TO BE EATEN unto salvation — and no one however eateth that flesh unless he first hath adored — it is found how such a footstool of the Lord's feet may be adored; and NOT ONLY DO WE NOT SIN BY ADORING, BUT WE SIN BY NOT ADORING."***¹⁷¹

**The same, on John 14, of the words of the Lord, Sermon 58:** *"If Christ is not by nature God, but a creature, He is by no means to be worshipped, nor as God to be adored. But they will reply to this and say, 'Why is it that thou adorest His flesh — which flesh thou deniest not to be a creature — together with the divinity, and servest it no less than the deity?' — But I adore the Lord's flesh — nay, the perfect humanity in Christ — because it was received of divinity and united to deity, that I may confess not one and another, but one and the same God and Man, Son of God. In sum, if thou separatest the Man from God, I never believe Him, nor serve Him: even as if one should find lying the purple or the royal diadem, would he try to adore it? But when the King hath been vested in these things, he incurreth the danger of death, if any shall contemn to adore them with the King. So in Christ the Lord — the humanity not naked or alone, but united to divinity, that is, ONE Son of God, true God and true Man — if any shall contemn to adore, he shall die eternally."*

**Epiphanius, in Ancoratus:** *"That which is of Mary — what dost thou allege? Wilt thou adore the Savior in the body, or wilt thou not adore? — 'Why should I adore?' — For if I have not adored, I have not had life. — 'Therefore dost thou adore a creature, the body itself?' — Great is the folly of those who say such things:* ***πολλὴ φρενοβλάβεια.*** *Let no one say to the Only-begotten, 'Put off the body, that I may adore Thee'; but let him adore together with the body the Only-begotten — which body hath been buried and raised."*

**Theophylact from Chrysostom, on Matt. 28:** *"Because human nature — before condemned — but now united to God the Word personally — sitteth in heaven and is adored by the Angels: therefore He rightly saith, 'All power is given me.'"*

**Damascene, lib. 3, cap. 8:** *"We adore Christ perfect God and perfect Man with one adoration, together with His unstained flesh — not saying His flesh is inadorable. For He is adored in the one hypostasis of the Word. Nor therefore do we offer veneration to a creature. For we adore not as naked flesh, but as united to deity — the two natures being reduced into one hypostasis of the Λόγος. I fear to touch the coal, on account of the fire coupled with the wood. I adore both natures of Christ my God on account of the deity united to the flesh. For I add not a fourth person in the Trinity, but I confess one person of the Word and of His flesh."*

Whence the *sounder Scholastics* also said: *"As with the same fear iron ignited is feared, and fire in iron, so with one adoration the Λόγος is adored together with His assumed flesh."*¹⁷²

Examples of this adoration in the New Testament are infallible:

- The Magi come from the East, and adore the infant Jesus born, lying in the manger.
- In the ship they adore Him, saying: *"Truly Thou, who art here with us, art the Son of God"* (*Matt. 14*).
- The blind man adoreth Him (*John 9*).
- *"Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on us,"* cry the lepers, and others; and we daily.
- Even unclean spirits fall down and cry: *"Jesus, Son of the Most High God"* (*Mark 3 and 5*).
- The disciples adore Him raised (*Matt. 28*).
- *"When He was borne into heaven, they adored Him"* (*Luke 24*).
- *"All the Angels adore Him"* (*Heb. 1*), and *"minister unto Him"* (*Matt. 3*) — namely ***μετὰ σαρκός*** (*"with the flesh"*), as **Oecumenius** elegantly explaineth this adoration: that is, the Angels adore the flesh or humanity of Christ; and *"every knee is bent in the name of Jesus"* (*Phil. 2*), ***εἰς ἐνανθρώπησιν*** (*"unto the Incarnation"*), saith **Basil**.

These things I recite more prolixly on account of the blasphemies of our times, in which there are some who dare to say and to write that they do *not* adore *the flesh of Christ even in the very hypostasis of the Λόγος,* or *the humanity in the deity* — because a creature must not be adored.

We adore not humanity separately, or subsisting *per se* — nor are we ***ἀνθρωπολάτραι*** (*worshippers of a mere man*) — but we adore *the whole Christ, Λόγος, perfect God: not according to Himself in divine nature only, but according to the human nature assumed, made flesh* — to use Cyril's words to Theodosius. For we adore Christ *Redeemer, Propitiator, Mediator, Justifier, Savior, King, Pontiff, Head.* Such however is Christ not according to divine nature only, but according also to the assumed human nature — which must not simply be looked upon with bovine and carnal eyes as a creature, but must also be considered as *united to the divinity of the Λόγος, and raised above every creature, and Lady of all creatures at the right hand of God in the glory of the Father, in the assembly and throne of the Holy Trinity.*

From these therefore it must be judged concerning the proposition *"Christ is to be adored"*: Is it through *communicatio idiomatum properly so called,* that is, *according to divine nature only*? Far from it. — Is it through *that communicatio idiomatum more broadly used, of the divine glory of Christ communicated also to the human nature in Christ*? Altogether so. — But to shun the ***λογομαχία,*** we omit these forms, and simply say: *Christ is to be adored ACCORDING TO BOTH NATURES* — because our faith, whose nearest fruit is Invocation, looketh not only to the divine nature but also to the human nature of Christ, and adoreth *the whole Christ,* and addresseth Him — *God and our Brother, Emmanuel,* and ***θεάνθρωπον*** — which whoever doth not do, can by no means be Christian, but must be dismissed to the Turks and pagans.¹⁷³

It is patent from these things also, that many things are attributed to the human nature in Christ which do not befit its conditions considered per se separately. *Adoration* certainly is a cultus owed to God Almighty alone. But it is owed also to *Christ the man,* not only according to divine, but also to human nature. And this glory of the human nature in Christ is *supreme and admirable and ineffable* — to us, moreover, it is a *supreme consolation,* who approach not only unto the divine essence, but also unto our own flesh in Christ — that is, unto Christ God and Man — and invoke one and the same together with the Father and Holy Spirit, with one adoration.

### Third Glory — Communicatio Idiomatum in the Concrete

The **third glory** of the human nature in Christ is also this: that properties of the divine nature are attributed to Christ the Man in the concrete, and reciprocally the properties of the human nature are similarly attributed to Christ God in the concrete — this on account of the hypostatic union, according to the doctrine of the *communicatio idiomatum properly so called:* as, *"Christ the Man, our Brother, is Omnipotent, eternal, infinite, immense, everywhere-present, Creator of heaven and earth."* — *"The Son of God was born of Mary, for my sins suffered and was crucified, and died."*

### Fourth Glory — Vivifying Flesh, Cleansing Blood

The **fourth glory** of the human nature of Christ is that the Son of God with it and in it hath performed the work of the Redemption of the human race — and that the humanity hath been made partaker of the victory and triumph of the Son of God — and that the death and Passion of Christ, which He Himself sustained in His own human nature, is *one λύτρον* for the sins of the whole human race. For *the flesh of Christ is vivifying,* and *the blood of Christ cleanseth us from all sin.* For it is the flesh and blood of the *only-begotten* of God, and therefore hath vivifying life, which is not only a *created quality,* but is otherwise *a property of divinity.*

Whence **Athanasius,** apud Theodoretum, dial. 2, saith: *"When Christ saith, 'the Father hath given the Son life,' it must be understood that life hath been given to the flesh."*

And **Cyril,** lib. 4 *Thesauri* cap. 13: *"The Son is said to receive life from the Father, not inasmuch as Λόγος, but inasmuch as man."*

And, in *John,* lib. 2 cap. 114: ***"When He had said that life had been given, He addeth, 'because He is the Son of Man,' so that we may understand all things to have been given Him as a man, who — being a creature — hath nothing of Himself. But the only-begotten Son is not a partaker of life, but is life naturally, as the Father is. Life however is understood not simply as one who liveth, but as one who vivifieth others. But vivification is a possession proper to the divine nature. And yet the body, because it hath been made proper to the living Word, and hath been united to Him — for this reason, and rightly, as having the vivifying operation of the Word Himself, is said to have received vivific life. If the flesh be understood alone, she can vivify nothing at all — she who standeth in need of a vivifier herself. But when ye shall have searched into the mystery of the Incarnation with laudable care, and shall have known the Life dwelling in the flesh — although the flesh in and of herself can do nothing at all, yet ye shall believe her to be vivifying. For because she is joined with the vivifying Word, all is made vivifying. For not to the corruptible nature of the flesh hath the Word of God joined Himself, descending, but the flesh hath been raised up to the virtue of the better. Although therefore the nature of the flesh, as it is flesh, cannot vivify, yet she doth so, because she hath received the whole operation of the Word. For it is the body not of just any man — whose flesh cannot profit — but of very Life itself the body, in which the fulness of deity dwelleth corporeally: this can do so. For the nature of the flesh hath the Son of God joined to itself, who substantially is Life. The nature of the flesh, in and of itself, cannot vivify. For what more would it have than the nature of deity? Nor is it understood alone in Christ, but it hath the Λόγος joined to it. This is substantially Life itself. When therefore Christ calleth His flesh vivific, He doth not attribute to it, as unto Himself or unto His proper Spirit, the power of vivifying. For on account of Himself the Spirit vivifieth: unto whose virtue the flesh, through conjunction — not through essential or physical transfusion of the idiomata of divinity into the humanity, nor through equalization of the essential attributes of both natures, but* ***τῆς ἑνώσεως οἰκονομίᾳ*** *('by the dispensation of the union,' as Athanasius saith; or as Cyril saith, dial. 6, 'not by property of nature, but by the union of the person,' or 'through the ineffable concurrence of the Λόγος with the humanity,'* ***ἐκ τῆς τοῦ Λόγου πρὸς τὸ ἀνθρώπινον κοινωνίας καὶ ἑνώσεως***) *— hath ascended. But how this cometh to pass, we can neither with the mind understand nor with the tongue utter, but with silence and firm faith we receive it. He vivifieth also the body of Christ, because it is the body of Life itself, retaining the virtue of the incarnate Word, and full of the power of that One by whom all things are and live. And forasmuch as the body was delivered for us unto death, through the same body doth the Son of God again send life into us. He vivifieth us as God — not, however, in this alone, that we are partakers of the Spirit, but also through the flesh of the Son of Man, which for this cause He setteth before to be eaten — because He hath filled this with life. And whoso eateth this, liveth. But the Word cannot be eaten,"*** &c.

These things — which Cyril in divers places (lib. 4 cap. 23–24 and elsewhere) hath, and which we have here transcribed — are set down that *the figment of those who cry out horribly that the flesh of Christ profiteth nothing may be confounded.*¹⁷⁴

### Fifth Glory — The Names of the Office (κοινοποιία)

The **fifth glory** of the human nature in Christ is that *the names of Christ's office* do not befit divine nature only, but also human — and are names of the *office of the whole person according to both natures.* For it is ***ἐνέργεια θεανδρική*** and ***ἀποτέλεσμα,*** as *Damascene* nameth it — or ***κοινοποιία,*** as *Cyril* speaketh — that Christ is *Redeemer, Justifier, Mediator, Savior, Shepherd of our souls, King, Supreme Priest or Pontiff, Head of the Church, Gatherer, Governor, Defender, and Preserver of the Church,* and therefore *hath all power in heaven and on earth,* and is *Executor of all the promises,* &c. For these offices befit the person *not according to either nature alone, but according to both.* And although these actions per se are of divine and Omnipotent nature, and do not befit any creatures, yet they are done *by the Son in and with the assumed nature, and through it,* and therefore are attributed to *the whole person according to both natures.*

And here again the juniors are to be admonished, to distinguish propositions:

- *"Christ is Mediator."* — Is it through the *communicatio idiomatum* properly so called? Far from it. For not according to one nature only is *Christ our Redeemer, Justifier, Mediator, Savior.* Here the form of ***ἰδιοποιΐα*** is not employed: it is ***κοινοποίησις,*** embracing names of the office.
- *"The Son of God is dead."* — This is ***ἰδιοποιΐα,*** the *communicatio idiomatum* properly said.
- *"The Son of God is Mediator."* — This is ***κοινοποιία,*** not according to one nature only, but according to both — befitting the person as *placating, victorious, hearing, and exhibiting the promises of the new testament.*

And this doctrine hath been explained most gravely by *D. Philip [Melanchthon] the preceptor,* at the end of the *Corpus Doctrinae: How Christ is Mediator, and Why the Mediator is θεάνθρωπος* — whither we send the pious reader eager for truth.¹⁷⁵

### Sixth Glory — The Fourfold Post-Resurrection Glorification

The **sixth glory** of the human nature of Christ is fourfold:

**1.** That *not by the customary order of nature,* but *conceived of the Holy Spirit and born of the Virgin Mary,* Christ became Man *beyond the ordinary series of things* — not of virile seed, but of a mystic breath.

**2.** That He is *free from all sin* — ***ἄμωμος, ἄσπιλος,*** ***ἀναμάρτητος*** — and holy and pure by nature.

**3.** That He is *anointed,* and hath the Holy Spirit *not by measure,* as the other saints, but *the plenitude of all the gifts of the Holy Spirit.*

**4.** That *after the Resurrection the human nature in Christ hath been glorified and clarified,* so that not only is it *exempt from all miseries,* but *can penetrate dimensions when it wills,* nothing standing in the way; and in the Ascension it hath been carried *above all heavens to the right hand of God;* and (as we may use the words of pious antiquity, ***Athanasius contra Arianos,*** *orat. 2 and 4*) *hath advanced into divine glory,* so that it hath *all power in heaven and on earth without confusion of natures* — which power, before it was assumed by the Word, it had not — and is now *in the assembly and council of God, entered into the fellowship of the Holy Trinity, exalted above all creatures, and Lady of all creatures — having all things subject to it: celestial, terrestrial, and infernal.*¹⁷⁶

# *Inde Venturus Est Iudicare Vivos et Mortuos* — Thence He Shall Come to Judge the Living and the Dead

This too is a name of *office,* pertaining not only to the divine nature — of which it is written *"The LORD shall judge the world of the earth,"* and *"He is Judge"* (*Ps. 18, 75*), and again *"Judge of all is God"* (*Heb. 12*) — but also according to the *human* nature, as *John 5 and 6* showeth: *"The Father hath given the Son power to execute judgment, because He is the Son of Man."*

That Form shall be seen — the Son whom He hath united to Himself through the sacrament of the Incarnation. That very Form shall be Judge which stood under the judge; that very Form shall judge which was judged — so that the impious, the pagans, the tyrants, the heretics, and their like may see His glory, majesty, and presence conspicuous to all and each. And such shall the Judge appear as He can be seen both by those whom He is about to crown, and by those whom He is about to damn.

*This glorious Day we long for, we expect, we desire — and to it we cite all the enemies of the Church, and the blasphemers against the Son of God, at whose gentleness they now rage.* **Come, Lord Jesus, Amen.**¹⁷⁷

# The Third Article of the Symbol

***Credo in Spiritum Sanctum Dominum et vivificum, qui ex Patre Filioque procedit; qui cum Patre simul adoratur et glorificatur; qui locutus est per Prophetas.***

*I believe in the Holy Spirit, the Lord and Giver of life, who proceedeth from the Father and the Son; who together with the Father is adored and glorified; who spake by the Prophets.*

## The Divinity and Person of the Holy Spirit

The work of *creation* (*Job 33*, ***"The Spirit of the Lord hath made me"***; and *Ps. 32*, ***"By the Spirit of His mouth is all the virtue of the heavens made firm"***); His *Omnipresence* (*Ps. 139*, ***"Whither shall I go from Thy Spirit?"***); His being ***καρδιογνώστης*** (*Wisd. 1*, ***"The Spirit of the Lord hath knowledge of the voice"***); His *vivifying of things* (*Gen. 1*, ***"The Spirit of God vivified with His motion the created things"***); and the *cultus of faith,* the *testimonies of Scripture,* and the *confession of the Church* — all proclaim to us that the Holy Spirit is eternal God together with the Father and the Son.¹⁷⁸

## The Five Benefits of the Holy Spirit

The benefits of the Holy Spirit are these:

**1.** To *teach.*  
**2.** To *renew and sanctify.*  
**3.** To *help* — by *witnessing of Christ,* by *giving,* by *consoling,* by *praying.*  
**4.** To *seal the promises.*  
**5.** To *be the pledge of our salvation and inheritance.*¹⁷⁹

## And One Holy Catholic and Apostolic Church

***Et unam sanctam Catholicam et Apostolicam Ecclesiam.***

This Church is *wheresoever the voice of the Gospel soundeth,* according to the Prophetic and Apostolic writings; and is shown *by the true consent in the doctrine of the Gospel, as touching the foundation,* and *by the right understanding and lawful use of the sacraments,* and *by the due obedience in ministry according to the Gospel.*¹⁸⁰

**Epiphanius:** ***πιστεύομεν εἰς μίαν καθολικὴν καὶ ἀποστολικὴν ἐκκλησίαν.*** *"We believe,"* saith he, *"in one Catholic and Apostolic Church, and in one baptism of penitence, and in the resurrection of the dead, and in the kingdom of the heavens, and in life eternal."*¹⁸¹

For the ancients used the preposition ***In*** *not* because the faith relieth on the Church or on that visible assembly (as when it is said, *"I believe in God"*) — but to show that souls *acquiesce* in the doctrine which the Church professeth according to Scripture, and are *certain* concerning the resurrection and life everlasting.¹⁸²

## I Confess One Baptism

***Confiteor unum baptisma.***

**Cyril of Jerusalem:** *"Baptism is the liberation from captivity, the remission of sins, the death of sin, the regeneration of the soul, the shining garment, the holy and indelible signaculum, the chariot to heaven, the delight of paradise, the reconciliation of the celestial kingdom, and the charisma of the adoption of sons."*

**Augustine, *Epist.* 23:** *"The water which exhibiteth outwardly the sacrament of grace, and the Spirit operating inwardly the benefit of grace — loosing the bond of guilt, reconciling the good of nature — regenerateth man in one Christ, who was generated of one Adam."*¹⁸³

## The Remission of Sins

***Remissionem peccatorum.***

This remission of all sins is *the Justification of man before God,* which is wrought *by faith alone in Christ,* applying to itself the merit and benefits of the Son of God, out of the mere grace and mercy of God — *without the works of the Law* — which works avail nothing unto justification: not as *merit,* nor as *cause co-operating and helping,* nor as *part,* nor as *form,* nor as *adjunct* of faith in the effecting of justification, nor as *medium* of application or of conservation, nor as *condition* so necessary that without its presence and access there could not be, or come to pass, justification. For the *foundation and cause* — the *first, middle, and last cause* — of justification is *one and sole:* the *obedience and satisfaction of Christ.* For we are justified *without our own works,* whether *antecedent* or *present* or *consequent.*

And yet it is true that — as fire and heat, as water and moisture, as body and soul in the living man, as life and motion (indeed, as *Christ and the Holy Spirit*) cannot be separated — so justifying faith and works (as effects wrought by Christ through the Holy Spirit) cannot be torn asunder. The *efficient and formal cause* of faith is Christ Himself. The *formal cause of works* is faith, which is never idle, though it justify always without works. It is one thing to be *made* just, and another thing to be *declared* just.¹⁸⁴

## The Resurrection of the Flesh

***Carnis resurrectionem.***

We believe *the resurrection of this very flesh.* For this corruptible must put on incorruptibility. Therefore Job saith: ***"In my flesh I shall see the Lord — clothed round with my skin. I myself shall see Him; mine own eyes shall behold Him, and no other. This hope hath been laid up for me in mine own bosom"*** (*Job 19*).

## And the Life Everlasting

***Et vitam aeternam.***

Here shall be the end of all evils, and the beginning and eternal continuation of all consummated goods, and the last and supreme Good: the *sight of God,* the *converse with God,* the *eternal familiarity with the Angels and with all the blessed;* *life, purity, exultation, and gladness in God, who shall be all in all,* and *unto whom shall be given up all praises without end.*

This eternal God — the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, and Jesus Christ, and the Holy Spirit — we implore, that He heal our wounds and mercifully gather to Himself an eternal Church among us; that He avert and hinder the corruptions of doctrine and repress the attempts of fanatical men; and that He grant that all our Churches and schools, and their doctors, disciples, and citizens, be and remain *one in Him.*

***"Sanctify us, O Lord, in Thy truth. Thy word is truth."***

To Thee, one only God — Father, Son, and Holy Spirit — eternal, immortal, strong, holy, merciful, just — be praise, glory, honour, power, and might, world without end. **Amen.**

***θεῷ δόξα καὶ χάρις.***  
*To God be glory and thanks.*¹⁸⁵

*Leipzig, on the day of the Holy Angels [29 September], cIɔIɔ.LXXV [1575].*

# Nikolaus Selnecker — Biographical Sketch

*(Reused from the companion Institutio volumes for the reader coming to Selnecker for the first time, with a paragraph appended on this volume's place in his dogmatic-catechetical output.)*

**Nikolaus Selnecker** (also spelled *Selneccer* or *Schellenecker*) was born on **5 December 1530 at Hersbruck,** a small Franconian town near Nürnberg. His father, a jurist, brought the family to Nürnberg while Selnecker was still a child, and it was there that Selnecker received his first musical education — a formation that would define one strand of his later life-work as a paradigm-Lutheran hymn-writer.

At **Wittenberg** from 1550 he studied under **Philipp Melanchthon** — whose *Loci communes* method Selnecker would later adopt and adapt across all his systematic-dogmatic writings — and took his *Magister* in 1554. From 1557 he served as *Hofprediger* to Kurfürst August of Saxony at Dresden, an office he held until 1565 when the *Crypto-Calvinist controversy* at the Dresden court forced his departure. He became **Professor of Theology at Jena** in 1565, then at **Leipzig** from 1568 — with interruptions during the second phase of the Crypto-Calvinist controversy 1570–1574 (when the strict-Lutherans were briefly displaced) — and finally returned to Leipzig from 1574 as *Superintendent* and *Pfarrer* at St. Thomas, an office he held (with a single year at Braunschweig-Wolfenbüttel 1571) until his death. It was in the early years of this final Leipzig period that the *Symbolorum Exegesis* here translated was composed — first published in 1575, in an *editio princeps* whose Dedicatory Epistle he re-issued *verbatim,* two years later, at the head of the enlarged 1577 imprint that stands as the English text-base of the present volume.

Selnecker's **confessional-Lutheran significance** rests on his role as one of the **six co-authors of the *Formula of Concord*** (1577). The six — Jakob Andreä (Tübingen), Martin Chemnitz (Braunschweig), David Chytraeus (Rostock), Andreas Musculus (Frankfurt/Oder), Christoph Körner (Frankfurt/Oder), and Selnecker himself (Leipzig) — worked between 1576 and 1580 to bring the inner-Lutheran controversies of the second half of the sixteenth century to a paradigm confessional settlement: the *Solida Declaratio* of the *Formula of Concord.* Selnecker's specific contribution was the *Solida Declaratio* Article XI (**Predestination**), whose anti-double-predestination Lutheran formulation — *God has predestined to salvation but not to damnation; the reprobate are lost by their own contumacy* — became the confessional-Lutheran position against both the Reformed *decretum absolutum* on one side and the Semi-Pelagian *praevisa merita* framework on the other.

Selnecker's **hymnody** is his second lasting legacy. Author of ***"Ach bleib bei uns, Herr Jesu Christ"*** (*EG* 347 — the canonical *Ausgangs-Choral* still sung in confessional-Lutheran and Reformed hymnody worldwide) and of dozens more, Selnecker's ***Christliche Psalmen*** (1587) is one of the great confessional-Lutheran hymn-collections of the whole *Concord*-generation.

Selnecker died at **Leipzig on 24 May 1592,** and was buried in the *Paulinerkirche.* His epitaph, composed by his son, closed with the confessional-Lutheran memorial-verse ***Christus mihi vita, mors lucrum*** (Phil. 1:21).

**On the place of the present volume in Selnecker's output.** In addition to the great systematic *Institutio* in two parts (Pars I 1573/1579, Pars II 1579), Selnecker composed a long series of catechetical works — a *Catechesis minor Graecolatina* modeled on Luther's Small Catechism, a *Catechesis maior,* the *Examen ordinandorum,* pedagogical catechetical questions for pupils at home. The present *Symbolorum Exegesis* is the mature meeting-point of these two strands: it takes up, one by one, each clause of the three ancient Creeds — that is, the same clauses which Luther's Small Catechism explains in one sentence apiece — and unfolds them into full dogmatic expositions worthy of the *Institutio.* The volume is, in this sense, the mature Selnecker's own most complete statement of the *systematic-catechetical intersection* that his entire teaching life had been aiming toward.

**English gloss.** *An Exposition of the Creeds — of the Apostles', of Nicaea, and of Athanasius — faithfully repeating the perpetual doctrine of the Church of God concerning the chief Heads and Articles of the Christian Faith: above all concerning the eternal Divinity of the Son of God, the Hypostatic Union of the two natures in Christ, the Majesty of the Son of Man, the Communication of the Idioms, and the Supper of the Lord, and so on. Against the recent blasphemies of these times. Written and edited by the author Nicolaus Selneccerus, D[octor], Electoral Professor in the Academy of Leipzig.* — **Augustine, Letter 7 [epigraph]:** *"He loves himself too perversely who wishes others also to err, that his own error may lie hidden."* — *Leipzig, from the press of Johannes Rhamba, 1577.*¹
