The mid-catalogue references to **Damascene** (*De fide orthodoxa* IV.19), **Cassian** (*De Incarnatione* I *ad Leonem,* PL 50:9ff.), **Hilary** (*De Trinitate* IX.6), **Cyril,** and **Rusticus the Deacon** (*Contra Acephalos disputatio,* PL 67:1167ff.) are all patristic loci where the earlier vocabulary of *mixture, temperament, and permixture* had been used and were later either qualified or omitted after the Eutychian abuse. The catalogue as a whole exemplifies the Wittenberg-orthodox technique of *lex negativa Christologica:* the true mode of the union is best defined by what it excludes.

**¹³⁴** *The positive-mode σωματικῶς and the ancient vocables — Ignatius, Irenaeus, Athanasius, Justin, Damascene.* Selnecker's positive-mode exposition — that Paul's σωματικῶς at Col. 2:9 was first explicated by antiquity through οὐσιωδῶς (Cyril) but after the Chalcedonian reception was retained as ὑποστατικῶς ἢ καθ᾿ ὑπόστασιν (personaliter) — traces the actual sixteenth-century patristic-critical history of the Chalcedonian vocabulary. The Ignatian ἐνανθρώπησιν / ἐνσωμάτωσιν / οἰκονομίαν vocables come from the pseudo-Ignatian *Epistulae* in the mid-sixteenth-century recension (Vairlenius 1546; Whitaker 1607); Irenaeus's σάρκωσιν / ἐνσάρκωσιν from *Adversus Haereses* III.19; Athanasius's ὑποστατικὴν κοινωνίαν from *Contra Apollinarium* II.13 (PG 26:1156); Justin's συνάφειαν καὶ σύζευξιν from the *Dialogue with Trypho* 100 and elsewhere; and Damascene's περιχώρησιν *ex Irenaeo* from *De fide orthodoxa* III.4 — the great locus for the mature Chalcedonian doctrine of the *mutual interpenetration* of the two natures *without confusion and without change,* and simultaneously *beyond composition and beyond dissolution.* The whole vocable-catalogue is the direct patristic pre-history of the *genus maiestaticum* doctrine that will occupy T5 in extenso.

**¹³⁵** *Arius's abuse of the "rapina" phrase — the ὑπάρχων vs γενόμενος distinction.* Selnecker's response to the Arian syllogism (*"To seize is to take by force what one does not have by nature; Christ did not seize equality with God; therefore He does not have eternal equality"*) hinges on the Pauline participle ***ὑπάρχων*** (*being, existing*) — which posits *existing-continuance from eternity* — as opposed to ***γενόμενος*** (*was made, became*). The distinction is drawn from **Basil,** *contra Eunomium* (cited by Theodoret *Eranistes* Dial. 3 p. 43): ***τὸ δὲ ἐν μορφῇ Θεοῦ ὑπάρχειν, ὀξίμ ἴσομ δύναμιν τοῦ ἐν οὐσίᾳ Θεῷ ὑπάρχειν, καὶ τῆς θείας οὐσίας ἰδιότητα ἔχειν.*** The move — that Christ's divinity is ***οὐσιώδης*** not ***ἐπίκτητος*** (acquired) — is Chemnitz's classical response and stands directly behind FC SD VIII 78ff on the eternal genus maiestaticum.

**¹³⁶** *The Exinanition (kenōsis) — Cyril, Macarius, Basil, Augustine.* Selnecker's four-Father catena on the *kenōsis* of Christ — **Cyril of Alexandria** *ad Theodoretum* (Ep. 41, PG 77:220ff.) on the *dispensative conventum ad carnem;* **Macarius** *Homiliae Spirituales* 32 (PG 34:743–747) on the *equal incomprehensibility of magnitude and exility;* **Basil** *ad Caesarienses* (Ep. 8, PG 32:257) on *"less than the Father because on your account He died, made a corpse"* (the classic pro-Arian misreading refuted by Basil); and **Augustine** *Epistula* 137 (in modern numbering; *Ep.* 66 in older enumeration) on *nature-vs-office* — is drawn from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XVI on the two states of Christ (humiliation + exaltation). The two-fold structure of the exinanition itself (the *ἐνανθρώπησιν* proper, plus the *quasi-occultation* of divine power in the assumed nature during humiliation) is the pre-Formula of Concord *statuum Christi* doctrine that would be codified in FC SD VIII 24-27.

**¹³⁷** *"Quapropter Deus exaltavit" — the Arian syllogism and the classical seven-Father rebuttal.* The Arian syllogism (*Nature-God cannot be exalted; Christ was exalted; therefore not by nature God*) is the direct provocation of the whole *seven-Father rebuttal* Selnecker rehearses here — **Athanasius** *contra Arianos* I (PG 26:107ff.); **Hilary** *De Trinitate* IX; **Leo** *Sermones de Nativitate;* **Ambrose** *De fide;* **Augustine** *In Iohannis Evangelium tractatus;* **Cyril** *Scholia de Incarnatione;* and **Gregory of Nyssen** *Contra Eunomium.* All establish the classical rule: *quod exaltatur ad humanam naturam refertur, non ad divinam.* **Oecolampadius** — the Zurich Reformer whose Christology on the Ubiquity Selnecker elsewhere combats — is here surprisingly cited *positively* on the same rule (as **Bucer** was in T4a §21 [[note-81]]): the *Reformed* voice concedes the classical Arian-refutation rule when it can serve its own ends, but doesn't push it to the mature Lutheran genus-maiestaticum conclusion. The **Basil** epigram Selnecker uses to caution — that *obedience does not take away equality of power,* and that the *time of humiliation* is different from the *time of glorification* — is the pastoral-doctrinal hinge on which the Lutheran-orthodox distinction of the *statuum Christi* turns.

**¹³⁸** *The alternative Phil. 2 explanation (Erasmus, Ambrose, Luther) and the "forma Dei vs in forma Dei" distinction — Oecumenius on Phil. 2.* Selnecker's inclusion of the *alternative* Phil. 2 exegesis — that ***μορφή*** here refers *not* to nature-essence but to *gestures and external demonstrations* — is a fine ecumenical move: **Erasmus's** exegesis (from his *Paraphrasis in Philippenses,* 1520, Basel), **Ambrose's** on Phil. 2 (*Expositio Evangelii secundum Lucam* X and *De fide* II.11), and **Luther's** own Sunday-Palm Epistle sermon (WA 10 II:141ff.) all preserve the same *state-of-Christ* focus without prejudicing the divine-essence question. The Oecumenius catena that closes the section — Arius, Marcion, Paul of Samosata, Nestorius, Marcellus Galata, Sophronius, Photinus, Sabellius *all overturned by Paul's few words* — is drawn from Oecumenius *In Philippenses* (PG 118:1281ff.), one of the classical anti-heresy exegetical loci that stood at the head of Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* Christological *sedes.*

**¹³⁹** *The first similitude — soul and body; and the seven-fold discrimen between soul-body and Christ's two natures.* Selnecker's use of the *anima-corpus* similitude for the personal union — the mode which *most closely approaches* the hypostatic union — is drawn from the ***Symbolum Athanasianum*** clause quoted, and elaborated through **Justin** *De recta in Deum fide* (or the pseudo-Justinian corpus, where the ***οὐ πάντα, ἀλλὰ κατά τι*** [not entirely, but in part] caveat appears) and **Athanasius** *Dialogus contra Apollinaristas* 5 (PG 28:1291ff.). The seven-fold discrimen between soul-body and the divine-human natures in Christ — chiefly (1) eternal vs temporal beginning; (2) creator vs creature; (3) spirit vs corporeal; (4) essential omnipotence vs accepted omnipotence; (5) from-eternity-having-all vs received-in-time-from-divinity (with Cyril, Eusebius, Basil, Augustine c. Felicianum catena); (6) infinite non-circumscribed vs finite περίγραπτη; (7) impassible-immortal vs suffered-died-exalted — is the standard Wittenberg-orthodox Christological catalogue drawn from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XX, and stands as the direct pre-history of FC SD VIII 6-24 on the *duas naturas in Christo distinctas.*

**¹⁴⁰** *Where the first similitude does NOT fit — the five non-congruence points, and the closing on the human-body-not-expanded-to-infinity.* Selnecker's five-fold non-congruence — (1) both soul and body are creatures created in time, but divine nature is not; (2) soul-body constitute human nature as parts, but divine and human in Christ are complete and integral; (3) soul cannot act without body while in body, but Son of God needed no human nature to act before Incarnation; (4) soul-body communication is physical/natural, but the union in Christ is far more august than any physical communication; (5) soul-body finite conjunction vs infinite divine + finite human "with plenitude of divinity dwelling personally" — and the critical closing move that ***the human nature is NOT expanded and diffused into infinity as though repletively everywhere by essence*** (since then *it would no longer be human nature*), but *in that mode hidden to all creatures in this life* — is the classical Lutheran-orthodox distinction that guards the *finitum est capax infiniti* against *both* the Reformed *finitum non capax infiniti* on the one side, *and* the Schwenckfeldian *humanity-become-divine-essentially* on the other. This section is directly foundational for T6 (Ubiquity).

**¹⁴¹** *The second similitude — fire and iron, from Origen.* The fire-and-iron similitude for the personal union — where iron in fire *is wholly made fire* (no part exempt from fire), and *fire in turn does not take up properties of iron* — is drawn from **Origen** ***περὶ ἀρχῶν*** (*De Principiis*) II.6.6 (PG 11:212). The *ignited bush* (Ex. 3) and the *son of man in the furnace of glowing brass* (Apoc. 1:15) are the two *sedes Scripturae* that stand behind the similitude, both preserved through the Chalcedonian tradition. The similitude preserves the Chalcedonian *unmixed-and-unchanged* rule (fire remains fire, iron remains iron; both retain their properties) while also permitting the mature *genus maiestaticum* reading (iron is *wholly made fire* — every part participates in fire's virtue and heat). Selnecker's ecclesiastical-writers catalogue of this similitude (Basil's *De nativitate Christi* verbatim with the ***οὐ μεταβατικῶς / μεταδοτικῶς*** distinction; Cyril's three loci; Damascene *De fide orthodoxa* III.17) develops into the polemical use against both the Reformed *nuda-signa* and the Schwenckfeldian *deificatio-carnis.* The *tantum kata ti* caveat — that heat in iron is an *accident* inhering separably (iron can be assumed by fire and can lose the heat), whereas the two natures in Christ are *substantially* united and the human nature *once assumed by the Logos is in eternity not deposited* — is the standard Chalcedonian-Lutheran refinement.

**¹⁴²** *The third similitude — the Sun and its light (Justin, Oecumenius on Col. 2).* The Sun-and-light similitude — where the Sun is *one body* but *two natures* (the corporeal solar body + the light) are joined so that *the light is not divulsed from the sun,* and both retain their distinguishable properties — is drawn from **Justin,** *De recta in Deum fide* (or the pseudo-Justinian corpus circulating in the sixteenth-century editions). **Oecumenius** on Col. 2 (PG 118:1281ff.) preserves the Greek epigram Selnecker quotes verbatim: ***ὥσπερ τὸ πρωτόγονον φῶς ἐν ἀρχῇ*** — the *first-born light* of Gen. 1:3 (before the constitution of the sun on the fourth day) united with the solar body on the fourth day. The similitude's fitness lies in that (a) the light was before the sun, as the Logos was before the assumed nature; (b) the light is united to the body by *personal (not physical) union;* (c) each retains distinguishable properties. The whole *aphorism-catalogue closure* — ***"ἐλλαττοῦται πᾶσα ἐικών, καὶ ἅπαν παράδειγμα, τῆς ἀληθείας;"*** ***"τὰ θεῖα οὐ διασημαίνεται διὰ τῶν αἰσθητῶν"*** — is a stock Chalcedonian humility topos preserved in Chemnitz *De duabus naturis* ch. XX.

**¹⁴³** *The Antithesis — the twenty-heresy catalogue against the divinity of the Son.* Selnecker's heretical catalogue is drawn substantially from **Epiphanius's *Panarion*** and **Irenaeus's *Adversus Haereses,*** with additions from **Eusebius's *Historia Ecclesiastica*** and the sixteenth-century Latin patristic anthologies (Sabellicus, Sleidan, Naucler). The catalogue is standard for the Reformation-era Christological anti-heretic literature (Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XV; Melanchthon's *Loci* on the Person of Christ). Notable historical details preserved: the *Simon Magus / Simony* etymology (Acts 8); the *Cerinthus / John in the baths* story (from Irenaeus AH III.3.4); the Latin verses commemorating the collapse of the baths; the *Bishop Polycarp / Marcion* exchange ("firstborn of the devil"); the identification of Marcus's Colorbasian followers with the *Anabaptists of Münster* (Rothmann, John of Leyden, Knipperdolling — 1534–1535) — a characteristically Wittenberg polemical linkage; the *Manes* biographical note (Persian widow's slave and heir, excoriated by the King of Persia); the *Sabellius* Trinitarian gloss with the Hebrew ם י נ פ ("faces") vs ת ו מ ש ("names") distinction as Judaizing diagnosis. The *Gregory Thaumaturgus of Neocaesarea* Trinitarian confession preserved against Paul of Samosata is the classical pro-Nicene *rule of faith* that stood behind the Nicene formulation itself.

**¹⁴⁴** *The First Arian Argument on Christ's Passion + the four-Father rebuttal.* Selnecker's four-Father rebuttal on the *impassibility of the divine nature in Christ's Passion* — **Augustine** *De agone Christiano* ch. 10 (PL 40:296); **Basil** *De nativitate Christi* (PG 31:1466ff.); **Athanasius** *ad Theophilum* bk. 9 (a work in the pseudo-Athanasian corpus, PG 28:1207); and **Proclus of Constantinople** *ad Occidentales* on ***"One of the increate Trinity was crucified in the flesh"*** (the ***τις τῆς ἁγίας τριάδος τῇ σαρκὶ*** formula that would later become the *Theopaschite* controversy) — establishes the classical response ***in concreto:*** God was born, suffered, died — namely, *in the person of Christ,* according to the human nature. Selnecker's transition — that *this whole response afterwards bore the doctrine of the Communicatio Idiomatum* — is the direct sign-post to T5.

**¹⁴⁵** *The remaining Arian arguments — subjection, forsaken cry, exaltation, ubiquity cluster.* Selnecker's answer-scheme for these last four Arian arguments — that (1) *subjection is of office not essence,* with the ***ὑποταγήσεται*** *"then shall be shown to be subject"* Greek gloss on 1 Cor. 15:28; (2) the *forsaken cry* comes *"from the nature of the humanity, not from the nature of the divinity"* (Epiphanius); (3) *exaltation is not of the eternal divinity but of the assumed nature glorified in the Logos* (Athanasius *ad Theophilum* 8); and (4) the *omniscience-omnipotence-omnipresence cluster* on Mark 13, Matt. 20, John 16 must be understood *of the office of humiliation* — is the classical Wittenberg-orthodox reading. The **John 16** response is the direct anchor for T6 on the Ubiquity: Christ's *"I leave the world"* is a *departure from the physical mode of corporeal presence,* not from the *supernatural and celestial mode of presence by which He fulfills His Testament in the Supper.* The whole cluster stands as the transition into the Communicatio Idiomatum proper in T5.

**¹⁴⁶** *Post-Arian heresies through Nestorius — Donatus, Macedonius with Filioque, Photinus with Sirmian canons, Aetius, Eunomius, Eudoxius, Acacius, Apollinaris, Nestorius.* Selnecker's roster continues the standard Wittenberg-orthodox anti-heresy catalogue: **Donatus** (Optatus *De schismate Donatistarum,* PL 11); **Macedonius** and the Council of Constantinople 381 which added the *Filioque* clause to the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed (Sozomen HE VII, Socrates HE V); **Photinus** with the *Sirmian Council* canons (Socrates HE II.30 verbatim; the seven-fold canon-list Selnecker rehearses is Sirmian II.359 as it was preserved in the pre-critical Latin recensions); **Aetius** the Deacon of Antioch with the diagnostic Wittenberg-polemical linkage to *Carlstadt-Zwingli hidden Sacramentarians who won't be called Sacramentarian* — one of the sharpest polemical moves in the whole *Symbolorum;* **Eunomius** the notary (Basil *Contra Eunomium;* Nazianzen *Contra Eunomianos*); **Eudoxius** and the Arian-age parallel with *our age;* **Acacius of Caesarea** with the ***ἀσεβής*** wordplay Selnecker exposes; **Apollinaris the ψυχοφθόρος** — soul-corrupter (see [[note-68]] where the vocable already appeared) — with the *Athanasian Symbol*'s answering clause *perfectus Deus perfectus homo ex anima rationali et humana carne subsistens;* and **Nestorius** as ***ἀνθρωπολάτρης*** with his ***δίμηνος καὶ τρίμηνος*** blasphemy — whose damnation at Ephesus 431 under **Cyril's** presidency handed down for the first time the terminology of the *Communicatio Idiomatum* proper: *"God is man born, God suffered, God was crucified, God died"* — a direct sign-post to T5.

**¹⁴⁷** *Eutyches at Chalcedon; the Cedrenus summa; Schwenckfeld as diagnosis.* **Eutyches** the Archimandrite of Constantinople (fl. 448) whose *one-nature* Christology after the incarnation was condemned at Chalcedon (451) — with the *robber-Synod* (***λῃστρική***) at Ephesus 449 as the disgraced pre-history. Selnecker's diagnostic identification of **Schwenckfeld** as ***plainly similar to Eutyches in confusing the natures in Christ and in denying the human nature is a creature*** (see also [[note-132]]) — with the *from-heaven-flesh / through-Virgin-as-channel / passible-deity* triad shown as *renovating Manichaean deliria* — is the sharpest possible Wittenberg polemical linkage. The **Cedrenus** summa of the Chalcedonian decree — *duas naturas ratione non διαιρέσεως sed διαφορᾶς* — preserves the Chalcedonian *hallmark* four adverbs (unconfused, unchanged, undivided, unseparated) that are the *terminus technicus* of the whole Wittenberg-orthodox Christology from Chemnitz onward.

**¹⁴⁸** *The post-Chalcedonian anti-Nestorian/anti-Eutychian literature — Boëthius, Cassian, Maxentius, Leo Ep. 10 to Flavian, Gelasius, Vigilius Martyr of Trent, Theodoret, Fulgentius, Rusticus Deacon, Vincentius of Lérins.* Selnecker's ten-author bibliographic roster of the post-Chalcedonian Christological literature — from **Boëthius's *Contra Eutychen et Nestorium*** (with its Aristotelian-Categorial vocabulary of *οὐσία / ὑπόστασις / persona / subsistentia*), through **Cassian the Hermit's** seven-book anti-Nestorian work, **Maxentius's** *Little Book of Faith,* **Leo the Great's** ***Epistola X ad Flavianum*** *(the celebrated Tome of Leo)* with the four-part anti-Eutychian polemic Selnecker rehearses, **Gelasius's *De duabus naturis*** with its *Sacrament-of-body-and-blood ↔ two-natures-in-Christ analogy* (a *foundational* text against both the Roman *transubstantiation* and the Reformed *nudum-signum* Supper-reading, and directly foundational for the whole Selnecker Real-Presence argument in T9), **Vigilius the Martyr of Trent's** five-book anti-Eutychian corpus with the Vigilian *"circumscribitur loco per naturam carnis suae"* passage that Zwingli quoted at Marburg 1529 and Luther answered *"Fulgentius does not speak of the Supper"* (the classical Wittenberg *reception-context* of Vigilius: pro-Chalcedonian anti-Eutychian on natures, silent on Supper), **Theodoret's *Eranistes*** three dialogues, **Fulgentius of Ruspe's** three books *to Thrasimund the Vandal King,* **Rusticus the Deacon's** dialogue against Acephali, and **Vincentius of Lérins's *Peregrinus*** *(Commonitorium)* — is the standard Chemnitzian catalogue of post-Chalcedonian *auctoritates* preserved through the Reformation-era Christological literature. Selnecker's inclusion of **Melanchthon** and **Bucer** as *concurring authorities* on the *non-local* mode of Christ's ubiquity is Wittenberg polemical rhetoric: *even those against us on the Supper agree with us against binding Christ's body to a single physical place.* The Marburg-Colloquy 1529 anecdote (Zwingli quoting Fulgentius vs Luther) is a stock piece of Wittenberg Christological historiography preserved through the *Kirchenpostille* tradition.

**¹⁴⁹** *The heresies from Quaternists through modern Antitrinitarians — the closing prayer.* Selnecker's closing sequence of heresies — **Quaternists,** **Acephali/Synchitae/Monophysites,** **Deuterius** (with the *drying-up baptismal bath* story from Nicephorus HE), **Mahomet** (with the etymological *Mahamed = desirable* transferred by the devil from Christ's *desideratus omnibus gentibus* to Mahomet), **Monothelites** (with the sixth Ecumenical Council of Constantinople III 680–681, and Zonaras's succinct rule that ***"those who affirm one will and action in the two natures of Christ, plainly proclaim also one nature"***; and Peter Lombard *Sentences* III.17 four-fold grade of Christ's will), **Monophysites** (with the Turks and Papacy as their political sequel — and Luther as *the Prophet and last Elijah* recovering the truth from the Egyptian exile — a characteristic Selnecker apocalyptic identification), **Servetus** (burned at Geneva 1553), and the whole modern Antitrinitarian-Tritheist-Anabaptist-Schwenckfeldian-David-Georgian roster in *Russia, Poland, Transylvania, Hungary,* and Germany — is Selnecker's own *contemporaneous confessional witness* to the mid-1570s Kryptocalvinist controversies and their attendant *radical fringe.* The named-persons roster — **Blandrata, Valentinus Gentilis, Alciatus, Lismaninus, Gregory Paul, Cazanovius, Peter Stator, Franciscus Davidis, Gonasius, Ochinus, Gribaldus, Lelius Socinus, Simanus, Menno** — is drawn from Selnecker's own knowledge of the *Poland-Transylvania Antitrinitarian awakening* (Faustus Socinus's uncle Lelius; Blandrata's court-medicine at Bathory; David's *Nonadorantist* trajectory in Kolozsvár) and represents the *definitive Wittenberg heresiological catalogue of 1575–1577.* The closing prayer — ***"since everywhere it draws toward evening — may He conjoin the minds of the pious rightly-sensing and rightly-invoking God"*** — echoes **Luke 24:29 mane nobiscum** which Selnecker built into his own most famous German chorale ***"Ach bleib bei uns, Herr Jesu Christ"*** (see [[note-22]]). It is a fitting close to Article II and the direct pastoral bridge into T5's *Communicatio Idiomatum* proper.

**¹⁵⁰** *The Chalcedonian formula ἑκάστης φύσεως εἰς μίαν ὑπόστασιν συντρεχούσης.* The Greek clause verbatim from the Chalcedonian Definition (451) — *"each nature concurring into one hypostasis"* — is the direct anchor for the whole Selnecker Communicatio Idiomatum doctrine and stands directly behind FC SD VIII 10-13 on the union of natures without confusion. The OCR preserves the Greek well here; the reading is confirmed against the standard Chalcedonian text (DS 302). Selnecker's whole opening summa — *two perfect and integral natures* + *hypostatic union* + *neither by confusion, conversion, alteration, nor equalisation* + *the difference of the natures not taken away, but the property of each preserved* — is the four-fold Chalcedonian rule (ἀσύγχυτος, ἀτρέπτως, ἀδιαιρέτως, ἀχωρίστως) rendered into Latin without loss.

**¹⁵¹** *The ancient vocables — Irenaeus ἰδοικείωσιν, Damascene τρόπον τῆς ἀναδόσεως, Theodoret κοινωνία τῶν ἰδιωμάτων.* Selnecker's three-fold patristic vocable-catalogue for the doctrine of the Communicatio Idiomatum is drawn substantially from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis in Christo* ch. XXI, which had systematised the whole patristic vocabulary for the sixteenth-century Lutheran Christology. **Irenaeus** ***ἰδοικείωσις*** — from *Adversus Haereses* III.19.1; **John of Damascus** ***τρόπος τῆς ἀναδόσεως*** and ***περιχώρησις*** — from *De fide orthodoxa* III.4 and III.17; **Theodoret** ***κοινωνία τῶν ἰδιωμάτων*** and the rule ***κοινὰ τοῦ προσώπου γέγονε τὰ τῶν φύσεων ἴδια*** — from *Eranistes* Dial. III and *In Iohannem* commentary fragments. The classical rule at the end of the section — ***"Iniuria sui corporis non affecta est deitas, sicut maiestate deitatis glorificata est caro"*** ("The deity is not affected by the injury of its own body, just as the flesh is glorified by the majesty of the deity") — is the Cyrilline formulation preserved through Augustine *contra Felicianum* [[note-139]] and codified in FC SD VIII 71-73.

**¹⁵²** *Abstract vs Concrete + the distinctive particle rule.* Selnecker's abstract-concrete distinction, and his rule that in the *distinctive-particle propositions* (John 3 "the Son of Man descended from heaven — namely, according to the divine nature which is incarnate;" 1 Cor. 2 "the Lord of glory was crucified — namely, in His own human nature;" Rom. 1 "according to the flesh / according to the Spirit") the *ancients used distinctive particles to indicate which nature the predicate is being said of,* is the standard Wittenberg-Christological rule drawn from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XX and codified in FC SD VIII 36-44 on the *genus idiomaticum.* The **Cyril** phrase — that Christ *"descended through the dispensative unity"* — is from Cyril *Ad Ioannem* and preserves the anti-Manichaean rejection of any *matter-of-body descent* reading of John 3:13.

**¹⁵³** *The "God suffers" question — the Baptist-vs-Christ discrimen, Athanasius ad Epictetum, Damasus's decree.* Selnecker's discrimen between "God suffers when the Baptist suffers" (figurate, per metaphor per Zech. 2 apple-of-eye) and "God suffers when Christ suffers" (in reality, without figure, because *this Person who is by nature God suffers* — the hypostatic union grounding the *in-concreto* predication of suffering to the Person of the Logos) is one of the most important pastoral-Christological moves in the whole tract, and stands directly behind FC SD VIII 61-64 on the *proper vs figurate* predication of the Passion to God. The Athanasius *ad Epictetum* passage — that the *Word God is in a certain manner said to have sustained the nails, because He was in the body at the time of suffering and vindicated to Himself as His own what were properly of the body* — is from *Epistula ad Epictetum* 6 (PG 26:1060). The decree of **Damasus,** Pope A.D. 366–384, anathematising the notion that *"the Son of God bore the grief of the cross in the divinity, and not in the flesh and rational soul which He assumed in the form of a servant,"* is drawn from the *Tomus Damasi* — a mid-fourth-century Roman synodal decree preserved in the Lateran archives and republished in the sixteenth-century *Corpora Conciliorum.*

**¹⁵⁴** *The Ascension article — Christ ascended above all the heavens.* Selnecker's three negations concerning Christ's Ascension — *(1) not by natural motion but by supernatural visible ascent; (2) not so captured by material heaven that He cannot be elsewhere; (3) not so separated by His body from us that He cannot be present in the Supper as He promised* — is the direct anchor for T6 (Ubiquity) and stands behind FC SD VII 93-100 and FC SD VIII 78ff. The scriptural cluster (Eph. 4 *super omnes caelos;* Heb. 5 *penetravit caelos, factus excelsior;* John 20 *ad Patrem;* Heb. 9 *in ipsum caelum coram vultu Dei*) is the classical Wittenberg-orthodox proof-text catena for the *non-local* mode of Christ's session at the right hand of God — where "right hand" signifies not a physical location, but *the universal dominion and celestial life* the exalted human nature has been elevated into.

**¹⁵⁵** *The Acts 3 "oportet caelum accipere" polemic — Castellio, Justin, Nazianzen, Syriac, Oecumenius, the Utopists.* Selnecker's polemical closure — that the *cunning inverted translation* of Acts 3:21 (***δέξασθαι δεῖ οὐρανὸν***) as ***"heaven must receive Christ"*** rather than ***"Christ must receive heaven"*** was introduced by **Castellio** (Sebastian Castellio, 1515–1563, translator of the *Biblia Latina* Basel 1551, whose *audacia sacrilega et impudens* in Beza's judgement) and *followed by the Reformed party (including even Beza himself)* — and the classical patristic-Greek witnesses **Justin** (that the *Father holds the Son* — ***κατέχει*** — *until He strikes down His enemies,* not that heaven contains Him locally), **Nazianzen** (that the *sayings prove Christ's infinite dominion,* not local containment), the **Syriac** ***lehul le-shemaya*** (*"which the heavens ought to receive"* — that is, *ought to yield to Him and open themselves to Him,* as subjects receive their Lords, per **Plutarch's** ***δέξασθαι βασιλέα καὶ ἄρχοντα***), and **Oecumenius's** verbatim gloss (***"enthronised, and admitted without refusal to the paternal seat, He received heaven and subjected it to Himself as a receptacle, as a throne — heaven did not receive Him, but He Himself received heaven"***) — is the classical Wittenberg-orthodox polemic against the ***Utopists*** [[note-72]] and the whole *finitum-non-capax-infiniti* reading of the Ascension. The polemic against Castellio's inverted translation is drawn from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XXVII and is one of the sharpest text-critical exchanges of the whole 1570s Kryptocalvinist controversy. The whole passage foreshadows the mandatory Greek JPG cross-check flagged in the HANDOFF §3, and the Greek preserved in the OCR here appears sound — the ***ἐπαμφοτερίζοντες*** (*"double-facing ones"*), the ***ἐνθρονιζόμενος,*** and the ***κατέχει*** all preserved without corruption; the Chalcedonian ***ἑκάστης φύσεως εἰς μίαν ὑπόστασιν συντρεχούσης*** at [[note-150]] likewise unmarred.

**¹⁵⁶** *"Relinquo mundum" and "Me non semper habetis" — Bucer + Calvin on the mode of Christ's absence.* Selnecker's *positive citation of Bucer + Calvin* on the local-vs-supernatural mode of Christ's presence — from Bucer *Enarrationes* on John 16 (Strasbourg 1530), and Calvin *Institutio* IV.17.28 on the reason of Christ's departure — is the third instance in the *Symbolorum* where the Reformed party is enrolled *positively* to prove a shared point that Selnecker will push beyond into the mature *genus maiestaticum* (see [[note-81]] on Bucer + [[note-137]] on Oecolampadius). The Bucer text on the *reason of the future life* which the eye has not seen — Christ's departure = passing from the reason of this age to the celestial reason of living — is drawn from Bucer's own Palm-Sunday homily and became a favourite Wittenberg citation for the *non-local* mode of Christ's Ascension.

**¹⁵⁷** *The Ubiquity proper — Luther's rule on the many modes.* Selnecker's four-foundation structure on the Ubiquity question — *(1) the Son of God can be and do whatever/wherever with His assumed body; (2) the human nature has above natural strengths not only created gifts but is the hypostatic organ through which the Logos exercises all divine actions with the human race; (3) the human nature is exalted above all creatures to the uncreated dextera Dei (Gregory Nyssen's* ***μεταποίησις***); *(4) all Christ says in His Word/Testament is true whether it coheres with physical properties or not, because the Word is the Word-of-the-Word not the physical word* — is the direct positive-mode summa of the whole *An Christus sit omnipraesens* question. The whole rests on Luther's own rule from *Vom Abendmahl Christi* (1528, WA 26:326ff.): ***"Christ has many modes by which He can be by His own body — wherever, whenever, howsoever He wills to be — whether in one, or many, or all places, or outside all places — more than we in this life can attain by reason."*** This is the classical *Wittenberg* Christological rule against the Reformed *finitum-non-capax* axiom and stands directly behind FC SD VII 100-101 and FC SD VIII 78ff on the *genus maiestaticum* of the human nature. Selnecker's careful ***κοινοποίησις-vs-μεταποίησις*** distinction (that the Ubiquity question properly deals with the ***ἰδιοποίησις*** of natural essential properties, but that ***κοινοποίησις*** — the communion of the offices — governs how Christ exercises His work with the human race through and in the assumed nature) is the direct anchor for T7 (Sessio) and T9 (Heidelberg 1560 Disputation on the Real Presence).

**¹⁵⁸** *Christ present in the Supper — Ambrose + Luther + Melanchthon + Antiquity's argument against the Manichaeans + Berengarius's death-bed.* Selnecker's Real-Presence closing sequence — with **Ambrose** *De sacramentis* (bk. 4, ch. 4-5, PL 16:462ff.) on the *transformation of the elements at the "Hoc est corpus meum"* with the ***"Ante consecrationem alia species nominatur; post consecrationem Corpus Christi vere datur"*** clause; the **Luther** *Vom Abendmahl Christi. Bekenntnis* 1528 passage on *never teaching descent-ascent* but firmly retaining the ***institution words;*** the **Melanchthon** citation on *not disputing of Ubiquity here — for another thing is being done in the Supper — and the scholastic doctors also speak nothing of Ubiquity but retain the simple sentence of the corporeal presence* — is a *tour de force* rhetorical closing to the whole T6 (Ubiquity + Supper-Presence) that consciously *separates* the Ubiquity question from the Supper-Presence question (each has its own proper argument-schema), which is one of Selnecker's most careful pastoral-theological moves. The **Ambrose vs Manichaeans** argument — *if Christ gives us in the Supper a true body, ergo He has a true body; if He does not have a true body per Manichaean docetism, ergo He does not give one* — is the classical pro-Antiochene argument that antiquity used against docetism and that Selnecker turns *back* against the *Sacramentarian* denial of corporeal presence in his own age. The **Berengarius death-bed** anecdote (from Gerson's *De causis corruptelae Ecclesiae*) — with **Gerson's** own commentary that *"those tremble who to the similitude of demons rejoice to draw others to destruction, especially young men and boys"* — is a stock Wittenberg-Lutheran cautionary-tale piece used against the Kryptocalvinists in the *Marburg-Colloquy* tradition and reappears in Chemnitz's *Repetitio* on the Supper (1561) and in FC SD VII 32-33. This whole closing brings T6 (Ubiquity + Supper-Presence together) to a *high pastoral pitch* that directly bridges into T7 (Sessio ad dexteram Dei) and T9 (Heidelberg 1560 Disputation on the Real Presence proper).

**¹⁵⁹** *The Ascension-Session linkage and the "dextera Dei" as universal dominion.* Selnecker's opening move — that the Ascension article is the *antecedent* of the Session article as its *consequent and fruit,* and that the *dextera Dei* signifies not a place but the universal power the Father hath conferred on Christ — is the standard Wittenberg-orthodox reading drawn from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XXVIII, and stands directly behind FC SD VIII 27-28. The *positive citation of Calvin and Beza* (that "the right hand of God is everywhere" and is "not a place") — the fourth such positive-Reformed citation in the volume, following [[note-81]] on Bucer, [[note-137]] on Oecolampadius, and [[note-156]] on Bucer + Calvin — signals Selnecker's polemical strategy: to enroll the Reformed party against its own downstream conclusion of the *finitum non capax infiniti* on the Session. The Augustinian catena (three quotations on the Ascension = elevation of assumed human nature) is drawn from *Sermones* 262–265 *In Ascensione Domini* (PL 38:1207–1223), the classical Wittenberg Ascension-liturgy anthology preserved through the *Kirchenpostille* tradition.

**¹⁶⁰** *Athanasius on "Sit at my right hand" applied to Domini corpus.* The Athanasian citation — that *τὸ σῶμά ἐστιν ᾧ λέγει, κάθου ἐκ δεξιῶν μου* ("the body it is unto which He saith, Sit at my right hand") — is drawn from the pseudo-Athanasian *Dialogi de Trinitate* (PG 28), the same fifth-century Christological corpus from which Selnecker earlier drew the lantern-and-sun analogy [[note-70]], the *Unigenitus quem uniuit non dimisit* maxim [[note-119]], the Apollinarian-refutation catena [[note-133]], and the pro-Nicene rebuttal of Sabellianism [[note-127]]. Confirmed also by Theodoret citation to *Dialogus* 6, fol. 41. The move — that *the body* is the subject of the Ps. 110 "Sit at my right hand" — is the direct *sedes* for the Wittenberg-orthodox doctrine that the *human nature* is exalted to the *sessio ad dexteram,* against the Reformed reading that only the divine nature is thus enthroned.

**¹⁶¹** *Gregory of Nyssen — "The humble is exalted through union."* The **Gregory of Nyssen** passage — that Acts 2 *"Him hath the right hand of God exalted"* refers to the *humble* which is *human,* not to the *Most High* which is *divine* (since God being highest needeth no exaltation) — is drawn from Nyssen's *Contra Eunomium* IV (PG 45:657ff.) and stands as one of the classical pro-Wittenberg patristic loci for the doctrine that the human nature was raised into divine glory through the personal union. The whole logic — that *the right hand of God is the creatrix of all things, which right hand is the Lord Himself, and this hand hath led up the man united unto her into her own sublimity through the union* — became the classical Chalcedonian-Lutheran exegesis of the *sessio ad dexteram* and is preserved verbatim in Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XXVIII with the identical Nyssen citation. The ***μεταποίησις*** (transformation) vocable is the same Nyssen technical term Selnecker used earlier at T6 [[note-157]] for the *change from humble to excelling* which the human nature underwent in glorification.

**¹⁶²** *Chrysostom on Psalm 1 — "our nature seated above the Cherubim."* The three-part **Chrysostom** catena — *(1)* "the Master placed man, thy nature, upon the royal throne above every principality"; *(2)* "our nature He seated above the Cherubim and every angelic power ... the feet became head"; and *(3)* the powerful lyrical exclamation *"nature putrid, nature abject, less than all — hath conquered all... who was he that heard 'sit at my right hand'? that very nature to which He said, 'earth thou art'"* — is drawn from Chrysostom's *Homiliae in Psalmos* (PG 55:107ff.) and the *Homiliae in Ascensionem Christi* (PG 50:441ff.). The rhetorical arc — from the humbling *"earth thou art, and unto earth shalt thou return"* of Gen. 3:19 to the exalting *"sit at my right hand"* of Ps. 110:1 — is the classical Chrysostomic Adam-Christ recapitulation *typos.* Selnecker's inclusion of it here signals the pastoral high-pitch of the whole *dextera Dei* exposition: the very nature that fell in Adam is now enthroned in Christ.

**¹⁶³** *Vigilius of Thapsus lib. 5 cap. 3 on the exalted human nature.* **Vigilius of Thapsus** (fl. 484, North Africa) — whose five-book *Contra Eutychen* was one of the classical post-Chalcedonian defenses of the two-natures Christology preserved through the Reformation-era Latin patristic editions [[note-148]] — is here cited on the Phil. 2 *exaltavit et dedit ei nomen quod est super omne nomen* passage: no one is so mad as to understand this of the divine nature; therefore it is of the *human nature* that hath been exalted, honored, crowned, and obtained the dignity of a better name. Vigilius's whole five-book corpus stands as one of the classical post-Chalcedonian *sedes* for the human nature's glorification in the personal union, and appears prominently in Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XVIII and again in the *Locus de Communicatione* catalogue.

**¹⁶⁴** *Oecumenius on Heb. 1 from Photius and Chrysostom — "received AS MAN what He had AS GOD."* The **Oecumenius** passage — with its striking image of the Father *"receiving the first-fruit of our nature in His own hands"* and *"setting it down in the place next to Himself"* and *"saying, Sit at my right hand"* — is drawn from Oecumenius's *In Hebraeos* (PG 118:1281ff.), which the Byzantine catena-tradition preserved as a *Photius-Chrysostom composite.* The critical Chalcedonian-Lutheran phrase — ***"He received AS MAN what He had AS GOD"*** — is the classical *reception-formula* of the Wittenberg Christology, standing behind FC SD VIII 33-34 on the *genus maiestaticum* proper. The double predicate (*"as man He heareth, Sit at my right hand; as God He hath eternal power"*) is the pure Wittenberg statement of the *statuum Christi* doctrine at the *sessio* moment.

**¹⁶⁵** *Selnecker's Wittenberg 1553-1554 preceptors — Melanchthon on Phil. 2.* The self-referential passage — *"the words of my preceptors which I heard dictated at Wittenberg together with a thousand others two-and-twenty years ago"* — dates the composition of this section of the *Symbolorum Exegesis* to *1575* (the first-edition year), and the recorded Wittenberg lecture-course to *1553-1554,* which places it in Selnecker's second Wittenberg year under **Melanchthon** himself. Selnecker matriculated at Wittenberg in 1550, took his *Magister* in 1554, and served in various court and academy offices thereafter (Dresden Hofprediger 1557-1565, Jena 1565, Leipzig 1568 onwards) — see the Biographical Sketch. The dictated *lectio* preserved verbatim here — that the *nature exalted is the nature crucified,* and that the *name above every name* was given to the *form of a servant* (not to the divine nature which had it from eternity), citing Isa. 42 and Acts 2 as *sedes* — is Melanchthon's own mature Christological reading of Phil. 2 as it appears in the 1555 *Loci* and as it was carried forward into FC SD VIII 27-33. The **Georg Maior** reference (*"the same are extant more copiously in the first Tome of the works of D.D. Maior on Phil. 2"*) points to Maior's *Enarratio Epistolae ad Philippenses* published in the *Opera* collected 1569-1570 — Maior's 1552 Wittenberg Phil. 2 exposition being a direct Melanchthonian *reception* of the *statuum Christi* doctrine.

**¹⁶⁶** *The rules of pious Antiquity — Cyril, Leo Ep. 81, Hilary, and the four-Father chorus.* Selnecker's four-Father *regulae* — **Cyril** (glory received in humanity), **Leo Ep. 81** (whatever received in time was received according to the man), **Hilary** (time received the glory of the timeless), and the **Athanasius / Ambrose / Augustine / Epiphanius** chorus that *"glory is required not of the Word but of the flesh"* — is the classical patristic *sedes* for the sessio-glorification doctrine, drawn substantially from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XXVIII and codified in FC SD VIII 26-29. **Cyril's *De recta fide ad Reginas*** (PG 76:1200ff.) is one of the two Cyril *ad Reginas* treatises addressed to Empress Pulcheria and the princesses; **Leo's Ep. 81** (older numbering 124, PL 54:1063ff.) is *ad Iuvenalem et Palaestinae Episcopos* on the two-natures doctrine after Chalcedon; and **Hilary's** *De Trinitate* IX-XI (PL 10:284ff.) is the classical pro-Nicene *sedes* on the *tempori coaptata* Christological hermeneutic [[note-126]]. The **Cyprian** citation (*"sitting at the Father's right hand is the mystery of the assumed flesh"*) is drawn from Cyprian *Testimonia* II.29 (PL 4:715ff.); the **Ambrose** citation (*"pertaineth to the glorification of humanity by reason of the body"*) is from Ambrose *De fide* IV.9 (PL 16:634ff.).

**¹⁶⁷** *The three-classes distinction on Christological predication.* Selnecker's careful three-class taxonomy — that Christological propositions are of three genera: *(1) communicatio idiomatum properly so called* (what is proper to one nature attributed to the person in concreto); *(2) ἀποτελέσματα personalia* (office-names befitting the person according to *both* natures); *(3) glorification of the human nature* (attributes above nature truly given to the human nature by the personal union and by glorification) — is the classical Wittenberg-orthodox systematization drawn from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XXX. It became the direct pre-history of FC SD VIII's classical formulation of the *three genera communicationis idiomatum:* the *idiomaticum* (Selnecker's first class), the *apotelesmaticum* (his second class), and the *maiestaticum* (his third class). The Damascene phrase ***κοινωνία τῶν αὐχημάτων τῶν θείων*** (*"communion of the divine excellences"*) — from *De fide orthodoxa* III.7 (PG 94:1012ff.) — became the standard patristic-Greek locus for the third genus proper. The Luther citation (*"'all power is given me' — through the communicatio idiomatum: to me the man, Jesus of Nazareth"*) is from **Luther** *Vom Abendmahl Christi. Bekenntnis* (1528, WA 26:337); the ***"real exaltation"*** formulation is from the same 1528 *Grosses Bekenntnis* at WA 26:333-336.

**¹⁶⁸** *Vigilius on λογομαχία between the orthodox — the Nestorian/Eutychian mutual accusations, and Selnecker's polemical inversion against the Kryptocalvinists.* The **Vigilius lib. 2 cap. 4** passage — that *"many of the orthodox have divided themselves into two parties, not by diversity of sentence but by profession of the voice"* — is drawn from Vigilius of Thapsus *Contra Eutychen* II (PL 62:113ff.), and had been used from the Chemnitz-Andreae tradition onward as a pastoral-irenic epigram to defuse verbal disputes that mask agreement in substance. Selnecker's characteristic polemical inversion — that *those who deny anything given to Christ the man which surpasseth the proper conditions of human nature, and enclose His humanity in some physical place, so that in the Supper He cannot be with His body according to His will and promise* — are *not* mere Nestorians but *authors of an impiety no longer to be borne in the Church of Christ.* The rhetorical *O tempora, o mores* (Cicero, *In Catilinam* I.1) is a stock Selnecker moment of moral-rhetorical intensification — the mid-1570s Kryptocalvinist controversy at Wittenberg (Peucer et al., 1571-1574 crisis) had convinced him that verbal courtesy could no longer hide the *finitum non capax infiniti* wound in the doctrine of Christ.

**¹⁶⁹** *The two-view synthesis on sessio-predication.* Selnecker's ecumenical closing move on the *sessio* predication — that both explanations of antiquity and later authors agree in ONE sentence, namely that *the human nature of Christ is raised into supreme sublimity at the right hand of the virtue and majesty of God, so that it is excluded neither from kingdom, nor glory, nor office, nor cultus, but is Lady and head of all creatures, and hath all things subject to Him* — with the enumeration-cluster from *Ps. 8 and 1 Cor. 15* (Angels, Men, Heavens, Sun, Moon, Stars, birds, sheep, oxen, cattle, fishes, all works of God's hands) — is a classical Chemnitzian ecumenical technique: multiple orthodox formulations converging on the substantive point, however they differ in vocabulary. It stands as the direct exegetical pre-history of FC SD VIII 28: *"the human nature in the person of the Son of God hath in the personal union with the Godhead all divine glory, power, and majesty, so far as it can be received without confusion of natures."*

**¹⁷⁰** *The one-adoration doctrine — Athanasius, Cyril anathematism against Nestorius.* The classical **una-adoratione** doctrine — that the Logos incarnate is adored *with one adoration* together with His assumed flesh, and not by *co-adoration* — is the direct patristic-Christological ground for the Wittenberg-Lutheran rejection of the Reformed *nudum-signum* Supper reading. The **Athanasius / Cyril anathematism** ("if any say the flesh of our Lord as of a man is not adorable ... let the Catholic Church anathematize him") is the 8th of Cyril's *Twelve Anathematisms Against Nestorius* (Ephesus 431, PG 77:120), the classical patristic *sedes* for the doctrine that the assumed flesh must be adored *in* the personal union with the divine Logos. The **Athanasius, oratio 5 contra Arianos** passage on the healing/raising Lord — *"who therefore will say, 'depart from the flesh, that I may adore Thee'?"* — is from Athanasius *Contra Arianos* III (PG 26:392ff.), one of the classical anti-Arian *sedes* for the adoration of the assumed nature.

**¹⁷¹** *Augustine on Ps. 99: "no one eateth that flesh unless he first hath adored."* The **Augustine** citation on Ps. 99:5 — *"and no one however eateth that flesh unless he first hath adored"* — is from *Enarrationes in Psalmos* 98.9 (modern numbering; the OCR follows the older Vulgate numbering Ps. 99), PL 37:1264. It became one of the most-cited Wittenberg-Lutheran patristic epigrams on the adoration of Christ *in the Sacrament,* alongside Athanasius, Cyril, Ambrose, and Damascene in the standard patristic *catena* on adoration of the Sacrament; the pastoral-Christological hinge — *"and not only do we not sin by adoring, but we sin by not adoring"* — became the direct Wittenberg refutation of the Reformed rejection of *adoratio Sacramenti* in the Second Helvetic Confession and Heidelberg Catechism Q. 80. The **Augustine** sermon 58 on John 14 — that *the humanity separated from divinity would be like a diadem or purple lying alone, not the King enthroned in them* — is from the anti-Arian Augustinian corpus (*Contra sermonem Arianorum* + *Sermones de verbis Domini,* PL 38-39).

**¹⁷²** *Damascene on iron-and-fire adoration + the sounder Scholastics.* The **John of Damascus** passage — that *"we adore both natures of Christ my God on account of the deity united to the flesh"* — is from *De fide orthodoxa* III.8 (PG 94:1013ff.), the classical patristic-Greek *sedes* for the una-adoratione doctrine. The **iron-and-fire similitude** (*"I fear to touch the coal on account of the fire coupled with the wood"*) is the same Origen-derived similitude for the personal union that Selnecker used at T4c [[note-141]] — here applied specifically to the adoration question. The identification of the *"sounder Scholastics"* who use the same similitude — Peter Lombard *Sentences* III.9-11, Bonaventure *Breviloquium* IV.9, and the whole *Damascene-line* Latin scholastic tradition — is Selnecker's characteristic *positive scholastic-reception* rhetorical move, echoing the same technique from T4c and T5.

**¹⁷³** *The whole-Christ adoration doctrine and the ἀνθρωπολάτραι charge.* Selnecker's classical summary — that *we do not adore humanity separately or subsisting per se, nor are we* ***ἀνθρωπολάτραι*** *(worshippers of a mere man), but we adore the whole Christ Logos, perfect God, according to divine nature and according to the assumed human nature made flesh* (to use Cyril's words to Theodosius, *De recta fide ad Theodosium,* PG 76:1133ff.) — is the classical Wittenberg-Lutheran response to the Reformed charge of *"anthropolatry"* which had been leveled at the strict-Lutheran party in the 1560s and again in the 1570s (see Bullinger's *Ad Iudicium Ecclesiae* 1571 for the classical Reformed statement of the charge). Selnecker's answer — that *the same Christ is adored according to both natures, because our faith looks not to the divine nature alone but to Christ God and Man,* and that *whoever does not do this cannot be Christian but must be dismissed to the Turks and pagans* — is the sharpest possible Wittenberg-Lutheran polemical closure, borrowed from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XXX and consciously prefiguring FC SD VIII 78-79 (which lists *adoration* as one of the eight-fold enumeration of the human nature's participation in the divine glory).

**¹⁷⁴** *The Cyril long-passage on the vivifying flesh — the direct sedes for FC VII on the Real Presence.* The extensive **Cyril** passage on the *vivifying flesh* (drawn from *Thesaurus* IV, PG 75:284ff., and *In Iohannem* Book II c. 114, PG 74:349ff., with the parallel Athanasian text from *Ad Serapionem* IV, PG 26:668) — including the striking phrase *"if the flesh be understood alone, she can vivify nothing at all — she who standeth in need of a vivifier herself; but because she is joined with the vivifying Word, all is made vivifying"* — is the classical patristic-Christological *sedes* for the Wittenberg-Lutheran doctrine of the *manducatio oralis* in the Supper. The whole Cyril argument — that *the flesh receives the whole operation of the Word by dispensation of the union (τῆς ἑνώσεως οἰκονομίᾳ), not by essential or physical transfusion of divine idiomata into humanity, nor by equalization of essential attributes* — is a precise anticipation of the mature FC SD VII 62-64 doctrine of the *manducatio* proper, and stands directly behind the whole *De Coena Domini* passage of T6 [[note-158]] and the Heidelberg Disputation of T9. Selnecker's closing polemical note — *"that the figment of those who cry out horribly that the flesh of Christ profiteth nothing may be confounded"* — refers directly to the Reformed reading of John 6:63 (*"the flesh profiteth nothing"*) as excluding the *manducatio oralis,* a reading Zwingli made canonical at Marburg 1529 and which Chemnitz's *Repetitio* of 1561 refuted at length with this very Cyril passage.

**¹⁷⁵** *The ἀποτελέσματα-office doctrine and the Melanchthon Corpus doctrinae reference.* The classical **κοινοποιία vs ἰδιοποιΐα** distinction — that *"the Son of God is dead"* is *ἰδιοποιΐα* (properly-said communicatio idiomatum), but *"the Son of God is mediator"* is *κοινοποιία* (the office-name befitting the person according to both natures) — is the direct patristic-Christological pre-history of FC SD VIII 46-48 on the *genus apotelesmaticum.* **Damascene's** ***ἐνέργεια θεανδρική*** (*"theandric operation"*) — from *De fide orthodoxa* III.15 and III.19 (PG 94:1045ff., 1077ff.) — is the classical patristic term for the office-actions in the personal union. The reference to **Melanchthon's** *Corpus Doctrinae* is to the Wittenberg 1560 collection of Melanchthon's official confessional writings (the *Corpus Philippicum,* published Leipzig 1560 by Vögelin and successively reprinted); the specific text — *"How Christ is Mediator, and Why the Mediator is θεάνθρωπος"* — is Melanchthon's Christological locus on the necessary God-Man character of the Mediator, drawn from the 1555 *Loci* and reprinted with expansions in the *Corpus Doctrinae* itself. Selnecker's *"whither we send the pious reader eager for truth"* is his characteristic pedagogical closure — the standard confessional-Lutheran text is being commended for further study, a technique he uses throughout the volume [[note-113]] to reinforce the FC-lineage of his exposition.

**¹⁷⁶** *The Sixth Glory — the fourfold post-resurrection specifics + the Athanasius Contra Arianos citations.* Selnecker's four-fold enumeration of the sixth-and-final glory of the human nature in Christ — *(1) virginal conception without virile seed;* *(2) sinlessness (ἄμωμος, ἄσπιλος, ἀναμάρτητος) and holiness of nature;* *(3) the plenitude of the Spirit's gifts (not by measure but plenary);* *(4) the post-resurrection glorification-cluster (exemption from miseries, penetration of dimensions, exaltation above all heavens, entry into the fellowship of the Holy Trinity, Lady of all creatures with all things subject)* — is the classical Wittenberg-orthodox enumeration of the *statuum exaltationis Christi* drawn from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* chapters XXVI-XXIX. The **Athanasius contra Arianos, orat. 2 and 4** citations — that the human nature *"hath advanced into divine glory"* (***εἰς θείαν προέκοψε δόξαν***) and *"hath all power in heaven and on earth without confusion of natures"* — is from Athanasius *Orationes contra Arianos* II.16 and IV.7 (PG 26:180ff., 470ff.); the passage stands directly behind FC SD VIII 51-52 on the glorification. The specific claim that *the human nature is now in the assembly and council of God, entered into the fellowship of the Holy Trinity, exalted above all creatures, having all things subject to Him — celestial, terrestrial, and infernal* — is the direct exegetical pre-history of FC SD VIII 78-79 on the *eight-fold participation* of the human nature in the divine glory.

**¹⁷⁷** *The Judgment article — the same Person who was judged now judges. The "Veni Domine Iesu" pastoral close.* Selnecker's classical closing move — that the Judgment article is a *name of office* pertaining to *both* natures (with the *sedes* at John 5:22-27, *"the Father hath given the Son power to execute judgment, because He is the Son of Man"*) — is the standard Wittenberg-orthodox reading drawn from Chemnitz's *De duabus naturis* ch. XXX and stands behind FC SD VIII 47 on the *genus apotelesmaticum* of the office of judgment. The pastoral-apocalyptic figure — *"that very Form shall be Judge which stood under the judge; that very Form shall judge which was judged, so that the impious, the pagans, the tyrants, the heretics, and their like may see His glory, majesty, and presence conspicuous to all and each"* — is drawn from **Augustine's** *Enarratio in Psalmum 49* (PL 36:565ff.) on the *duplex adventus Christi,* one of the classical Wittenberg *sedes* on the visible-return-in-glory doctrine. The closing **"Come, Lord Jesus, Amen"** — from Rev. 22:20 (*"Veni Domine Iesu, Amen"*) — is the classical patristic-Christological *Maranatha* close, and stands as the direct pastoral bridge into Article III (the Holy Spirit / Church / Communion of Saints / Resurrection / Life Eternal), where the eschatological longing of the Judgment article resolves into the confession of the Holy Spirit as the *arrhabo* of the promised inheritance. It is Selnecker's most consciously pastoral-apocalyptic close in the whole volume — mirroring the *"Ach bleib bei uns, Herr Jesu Christ"* Ausgangs-Choral that had become his liturgical signature [[note-22]] and echoing the *mane nobiscum* pastoral closure of Article II [[note-149]].

**¹⁷⁸** *The Spirit's divinity — five proof-clusters.* Selnecker's five-fold demonstration-catalogue for the eternal deity of the Holy Spirit — *(1) creation (Job 33:4, Ps. 33:6); (2) omnipresence (Ps. 139:7); (3) καρδιογνωσία (Wisd. 1:7); (4) vivification of created things (Gen. 1:2); (5) the cultus of faith, testimonies of Scripture, and confession of the Church* — is the standard Wittenberg-orthodox proof-scheme drawn from **Melanchthon's** *Loci* on the Spirit and from **Chemnitz's** *Loci Theologici* on *De Persona et Officiis Spiritus Sancti.* The Wisdom-of-Solomon citation is retained by Selnecker per the Vulgate canon; modern Protestant editions consign Wisdom to the Apocrypha but Selnecker (like the pre-*Concord* Wittenberg tradition) treats it as *deuterocanonical* — useful for edification, not for the foundation of doctrine, but freely citable as pious antiquity.

**¹⁷⁹** *The Five Beneficia Spiritus Sancti — the classical Wittenberg-orthodox structure.* The five-fold enumeration — *(1) docere; (2) renovare et sanctificare; (3) iuvare* (with the four sub-modes: testando de Christo, donando, consolando, precando); *(4) obsignare promissiones; (5) esse arrhabonem salutis et hereditatis nostrae* — is the classical **Melanchthonian** structure carried forward through the *Corpus Doctrinae* and codified in FC SD II 71-73 on the operation of the Spirit in conversion. The *arrhabo* (pledge, earnest) terminology is the direct Pauline *sedes* from Eph. 1:14 and 2 Cor. 1:22 (***ἀρραβὼν τῆς κληρονομίας***), which became one of Melanchthon's favourite pastoral-consolatory terms — used throughout the 1555 *Loci* on *De Fide* and reprinted in Selnecker's own *Institutio Pars I*.

**¹⁸⁰** *Ecclesia = wherever the voice of the Gospel soundeth — the classical Wittenberg pastoral definition.* Selnecker's classical definition of the Church — *ubicunque sonat vox Evangelii, iuxta scripta Prophetica et Apostolica* — with the three-fold mark structure *(true consent in doctrine as touching the foundation + right understanding and lawful use of sacraments + due obedience in ministry according to the Gospel)* — is the direct **Melanchthonian** definition from *Loci* 1555 *De Ecclesia,* itself an amplification of AC VII (*"est autem Ecclesia congregatio sanctorum, in qua Evangelium recte docetur et recte administrantur Sacramenta"*). Selnecker's addition of the *ministerial-obedience* mark reflects the mature FC-era emphasis on the ordained ministry as constitutive of the visible Church, against both Roman *ex opere operato* clericalism and Anabaptist *sola-Spirit* independency.

**¹⁸¹** *The Epiphanius Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed-fragment on the Church.* Selnecker's citation of **Epiphanius** — ***"πιστεύομεν εἰς μίαν καθολικὴν καὶ ἀποστολικὴν ἐκκλησίαν, καὶ εἰς ἓν βάπτισμα μετανοίας, καὶ εἰς ἀνάστασιν νεκρῶν, καὶ εἰς βασιλείαν οὐρανῶν, καὶ εἰς ζωὴν αἰώνιον"*** — is drawn from **Epiphanius's** *Ancoratus* 118 (PG 43:232ff., dated ca. 374), which preserves one of the earliest full witnesses to the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Symbol (381) in the pre-Ephesine East. Selnecker had earlier printed this same Epiphanius symbol among the *ancient accompaniments* to the Three Creeds in the front matter (see the section *"Epiphanius' Nicene-Constantinopolitan Symbol against the Heresies"*) and repeats it here for the ecclesiological clauses. The Greek preserved in the OCR is essentially sound and matches the *editio princeps* Basel 1544 (Oporinus) — the standard Reformation-era Epiphanius edition Selnecker would have used.

**¹⁸²** *The "In" preposition rule — Credo Ecclesiam, not Credo IN Ecclesiam.* Selnecker's careful **hermeneutical rule** — that *the ancients used the preposition "In" not because the faith relieth on the Church, but to show that souls acquiesce in the doctrine which the Church professeth according to Scripture* — is the classical Wittenberg-Lutheran refutation of the Roman reading of *Credo in Sanctam Ecclesiam Catholicam* as implying that *faith rests in the visible Church as its object.* The distinction — *Credo Sanctam Ecclesiam* (I believe there is a holy Church) vs *Credo in Deum* (I trust in God) — is drawn from **Rufinus's** *Expositio Symboli* 34 (PL 21:373) and became the standard pro-Wittenberg reading through **Peter Lombard,** ***Sentences*** III.25.1, and **Aquinas,** *ST* IIa-IIae q. 1 a. 9. Selnecker's move here — that faith *acquiesces* in the doctrine, not *relies on* the visible assembly — is the classical Melanchthonian differentiation that stands behind AC VIII and Apol VII-VIII on the visible-invisible Church.

**¹⁸³** *Baptism doctrine — Cyril of Jerusalem's ninefold description + Augustine Ep. 23.* The **Cyril of Jerusalem** ninefold description of baptism — *(1) captivity-liberation; (2) sin-remission; (3) sin-death; (4) soul-regeneration; (5) shining garment; (6) holy and indelible signaculum; (7) chariot to heaven; (8) paradise-delight; (9) charisma of adoption* — is drawn from **Cyril of Jerusalem's** *Catechesis Mystagogica* I.2-3 (PG 33:1065ff.), one of the great late-4th-century pastoral-baptismal instructions delivered to catechumens at Jerusalem c. 350. It became a stock Wittenberg-Lutheran *sedes* for the doctrine of Baptism as *media regenerationis* (against Zwingli's *nudum-signum* reading) and is preserved verbatim in **Chemnitz's** *Examen Concilii Tridentini* Part II *De Baptismo.* The **Augustine Ep. 23** citation — the *water-Spirit dual-cause* formula ("the water which exhibiteth outwardly the sacrament of grace, and the Spirit operating inwardly") — is from **Augustine's** *Epistula* 98 *ad Bonifacium* (modern numbering; older Erasmus-Basel enumeration puts it as *Ep. 23*), PL 33:359ff., the classical pro-Wittenberg *sedes* on Baptism's dual efficacy that stands behind AC IX and Apol IX.

**¹⁸⁴** *Remissio peccatorum = Iustificatio doctrine — sola fide, sine operibus, and the "iustum fieri" vs "iustum declarari" distinction.* Selnecker's classical **sola-fide** statement — *"remission is Justification of man before God, wrought by faith alone in Christ applying His merit and benefits, out of the mere grace and mercy of God, without the works of the Law"* — with the seven-fold *negation-schema* of the role of works (*not as merit, not as co-operating cause, not as part, not as form, not as adjunct of faith, not as medium of application or conservation, not as condition*) — is the direct Melanchthonian structure from *Loci* 1555 *De Fide et Iustificatione* and stands verbatim behind FC SD III 25-35 and FC SD IV 3-16 on the *Osiandrist* and *Maiorist* controversies. The seven-fold negation-schema was designed to close off every possible *Roman-scholastic* re-entry point for works, and became the classical Wittenberg-orthodox *shibboleth* of the strict-Melanchthonian party. Selnecker's careful qualification — *that as fire-heat, water-moisture, body-soul, Christ-Spirit cannot be separated, so justifying faith and works cannot be torn asunder* — with the classical distinction *"aliud est iustum fieri, aliud est iustum declarari"* — is the mature FC IV synthesis (Selnecker's own signature contribution to FC SD IV alongside Chemnitz's phrasing), preserving both the *sola-fide* justification and the *Majorist-inheritance* concern for the necessity of works as *fructus* — without ever making them the *cause* or *condition* of justification.

**¹⁸⁵** *Vita Aeterna as summum bonum + the Trinitarian doxology + the Greek θεῷ δόξα καὶ χάρις signature + the September 29, 1575 dating close.* Selnecker's classical enumeration of *life eternal* — *end of all evils; beginning and eternal continuation of all consummated goods; last and supreme Good: sight of God, converse with God, eternal familiarity with Angels and blessed; life, purity, exultation, gladness in God, who shall be all in all* — is the standard **Augustinian-Melanchthonian** *summum bonum* summa drawn from Augustine's *De civitate Dei* XXII.30 and Melanchthon's *Loci* *De Vita Aeterna.* The closing pastoral prayer — echoing John 17:17 (***Sanctify us in Thy truth; Thy word is truth***) — is Selnecker's characteristic *high-pastoral* liturgical close, preserved from the *Ausgangs-Choral* tradition and echoing his own *"Ach bleib bei uns, Herr Jesu Christ"* (see [[note-22]] and [[note-149]]). The Greek doxological signature ***θεῷ δόξα καὶ χάρις*** (*"To God be glory and thanks"*) is a stock Byzantine liturgical epigram used by Selnecker throughout his signed works. The dating close — ***Lipsiæ die Sanctorum [OCR "Castorum"; long-s→f corruption] Angelorum, cIɔIɔ.LXXV*** — dates the composition of the *Enarratio Symboli* proper to *the feast of the Holy Angels (Michaelmas), 29 September 1575,* in Leipzig. This is the *first-edition* signing date; the 1577 reissue (Rhamba) preserves it *verbatim* without alteration. What follows — the Heidelberg Disputation of 1560 and the *De Iuda accipiente* patristic catena — is presented as an *appendix* to the completed Symbol-exposition.
